Sporotrichoid nodules, culture-negative ulcer, immunosuppressed host, granulomatous plaque Punch biopsy sent fresh in sterile saline for tissue culture, alongside a second punch in formalin for histology Go deep enough to include subcutis; consider an incisional or wedge biopsy for panniculitis-like disease. State the differential on the requisition so the lab sets up bacterial, fungal, and mycobacterial cultures at the right temperatures. Tissue culture — the only route to a species name and a susceptibility profile; sequencing as salvage
Scabies? Mineral oil scraping of burrows and papules; scale in crusted disease Scrape vigorously across several sites until faint capillary bleeding. In crusted scabies, any fragment of hyperkeratotic scale is teeming and virtually always diagnostic. None — this is a bedside diagnosis. Mite, egg, or scybala on your own slide is confirmatory.
Anogenital ulcer or urethritis? Lesion swab and site-appropriate swabs for nucleic acid amplification testing (NAAT) Sample the ulcer base. Syphilis remains largely a serologic diagnosis; Treponema pallidum PCR on a lesion swab exists but availability is region- and reference-lab-dependent. NAAT for Neisseria gonorrhoeae and Chlamydia trachomatis including lymphogranuloma venereum genotyping; HSV PCR; treponemal and non-treponemal serology
Clinical pattern

Ascending nodules along a lymphatic — the sporotrichoid or nodular lymphangitic pattern — is the single presentation where a thoughtless specimen costs the most, because the four main causes need four different laboratory setups. Sporothrix needs fungal culture at around 25–30 °C and is often invisible on tissue stains because organisms are sparse. Mycobacterium marinum needs mycobacterial culture at reduced temperature. Nocardia brasiliensis needs prolonged incubation and a modified acid-fast stain. Cutaneous leishmaniasis needs Giemsa, specialized culture, and ideally PCR for species. One fresh punch, split for all of them, with the exposure history written on the slip, is the whole game.

The punch biopsy that can actually be cultured

This is the specimen non-dermatologists most reliably get wrong, and the one where you can single-handedly rescue a diagnosis that has been missed for months.

Fresh, sterile, saline

The container is the whole decision

Tissue for culture goes into a sterile container with a small amount of non-bacteriostatic sterile saline — or on saline-moistened sterile gauze — and travels promptly. The saline is there to prevent desiccation, not to bathe the specimen; drowning a 4 mm punch in a tube of fluid dilutes an already small inoculum.

ContainerSterile, non-bacteriostatic saline
Bacteriostatic salineNever — it inhibits growth
Practical habitTwo punches: one fresh, one formalin

Formalin is terminal

There is no undo

Formalin cross-links proteins and kills everything in the specimen. A biopsy that has touched formalin can never be cultured — not later, not with a phone call, not by a heroic microbiologist. It also fragments nucleic acid, so molecular testing on the resulting paraffin block is a degraded salvage option rather than a plan.

Pitfall

The commonest version of this error is a single punch dropped into formalin by reflex, histology returning “suppurative and granulomatous dermatitis, organisms not identified,” and the patient being re-biopsied weeks later. Decide on the container before the punch leaves the skin.

  • Take two. One punch to formalin for histology, one fresh to microbiology. If you can only take one, take a larger punch and have it bisected sterilely — but agree that plan with the lab first.
  • Go deep. Deep fungal and mycobacterial infections live in dermis and subcutis. A superficial shave or a 2 mm punch is a negative result by design.
  • Pick the right lesion. Biopsy an early, intact, indurated area — not the necrotic center of an ulcer, which is mostly slough and secondary flora.
  • Prep without sterilizing your target. Clean the skin, but avoid soaking the sample site in residual antiseptic; let it dry before incising.
  • Send the tissue, not a swab of the tissue. Swabbing the biopsy defeats the purpose — homogenized tissue gives the lab a far larger effective inoculum.
  • Write the differential. “Rule out infection” buys you a routine bacterial culture. Naming the suspects buys you the right media, the right temperatures, and the right holding period.

The tests you own at the bedside

These are yours. They are faster than any send-out, they cost almost nothing, and in several conditions they are more definitive than anything the laboratory will produce.

What it does. Potassium hydroxide, typically 10–20 percent, digests keratin and leaves the chitinous fungal wall intact, so hyphae stand out against dissolved corneocytes. Adding dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO) accelerates clearing without heating; gentle warming works too, but boiling the slide produces artifact and cracks the coverslip.

Time to answer5–15 minutes
CondenserLowered — low light shows refractile hyphae
Best sampling siteAdvancing edge, or blister roof

Stains that raise yield. Plain KOH is unforgiving of a tired eye at the end of clinic. Chitin-binding dyes fix that. Calcofluor white is a fluorescent brightener that binds chitin and makes fungal elements glow apple-green or blue-white — excellent sensitivity, but it needs a fluorescence microscope, so it is usually a laboratory rather than bedside option. Chicago sky blue 6B and chlorazol black E are light-microscopy counterstains that color fungal elements against a contrasting background and are practical in clinic.

What you are looking at. Dermatophytes give long, septate, branching hyaline hyphae that march straight across keratinocyte borders, often breaking into barrel-shaped arthroconidia. Malassezia gives the classic “spaghetti and meatballs”: short, stubby, angulated hyphal fragments mixed with clusters of round-to-oval yeast. Candida gives budding yeast with pseudohyphae — elongated blastoconidia constricted at the septa rather than truly parallel-walled tubes.

Do not confuse

The mosaic fungus artifact — lipid and cholesterol crystals precipitating along keratinocyte outlines — is the classic false positive. Real hyphae ignore cell borders and cross them; mosaic artifact traces them. Cotton and clothing fibers are wider, non-septate, and unbranched; elastic fibers are wavy and taper. When in doubt, follow the structure: true hyphae branch and have septa at intervals.

In the United States, bedside KOH microscopy falls under provider-performed microscopy in the CLIA framework and requires the appropriate certificate. Requirements for in-clinic microscopy differ by country and by institution — check yours before setting up a scope.

What it does. De-roof a fresh vesicle, scrape the base firmly, smear thinly on a slide, air dry, and stain with Giemsa, Wright, or toluidine blue. Herpesvirus cytopathic effect appears as multinucleated acantholytic keratinocytes with nuclear molding, marginated chromatin, and a ground-glass nuclear texture.

Time to answerUnder 10 minutes
Best lesionFresh vesicle, not a crust
Discriminates HSV from VZVNo

What it cannot tell you. A multinucleated giant cell tells you a herpesvirus altered these keratinocytes. It cannot distinguish HSV-1 from HSV-2 from VZV, because all three produce identical cytopathic changes. That distinction usually matters — it drives recurrence counseling, suppression decisions, dosing intensity, and infection control — which is precisely why PCR displaced Tzanck as standard of care. Tzanck is also insensitive in crusted or late lesions, and it is operator-dependent in a way PCR is not.

Where it still earns its place. When you need an answer in the room. It also has non-viral uses a dermatology resident should keep: acantholytic cells without multinucleation in pemphigus, and Henderson–Paterson inclusion bodies in a crush preparation of expressed molluscum core.

Do not confuse

The multinucleated cells on a Tzanck smear are virally altered keratinocytes. They are unrelated to the multinucleated histiocytic giant cells of granulomatous inflammation on histology. Same adjective, different lineage, entirely different meaning.

Mineral oil scraping for scabies. Place mineral oil on the blade or the skin, scrape burrows, papules, and interdigital webs vigorously with a #15 blade until you see faint capillary oozing, and transfer to a slide. Oil keeps the material adherent and preserves the mite, eggs, and scybala — the brown fecal pellets. Any one of the three is diagnostic. Sample several sites; burden is low.

Diagnostic findingsMite, eggs, or scybala
Crusted scabiesScale is teeming — near-certain yield
Classic scabiesLow mite burden — negatives common
Pitfall

A negative scraping does not exclude ordinary scabies. An adult with classic, non-crusted disease carries only a small number of adult mites, so sampling failure is the rule rather than the exception. Treat on clinical and epidemiologic grounds when the picture fits; do not let a negative slide overturn a convincing history of nocturnal pruritus in multiple household members.

Dermoscopy as the aiming device. Dermoscopy rarely replaces sampling, but it dramatically improves where you sample. In scabies, the mite’s pigmented anterior segment at the leading end of a burrow produces the delta-wing jet with contrail sign — a small dark triangle trailing a linear whitish burrow. Put your blade exactly there. In tinea capitis, comma hairs, corkscrew hairs, zigzag and barcode or Morse-code hairs, and black dots mark the hairs actually parasitized, so you pluck those rather than sampling at random. In molluscum, the central umbilication with amorphous white-yellow lobules and crown vessels confirms the diagnosis without any specimen at all. In pediculosis, dermoscopy separates viable nits containing a nymph from empty translucent casings, which is the difference between active infestation and a treated one.

What it is. A long-wave ultraviolet source, around 365 nm, filtered through Wood glass. It detects fluorescent metabolites, not organisms. Use it in a genuinely dark room after a few minutes of dark adaptation, and hold it close.

ErythrasmaCoral-red / salmon-pink
M. canis / M. audouiniiBlue-green hair fluorescence
Trichophyton tonsuransNo fluorescence
PseudomonasGreen fluorescence

Erythrasma. The coral-red glow comes from coproporphyrin III produced by Corynebacterium minutissimum, not from the bacterium itself. This is a genuinely useful intertriginous discriminator, because candidal intertrigo and inverse psoriasis do not fluoresce.

Tinea capitis — the trap. Ectothrix infection by the fluorescent Microsporum species — chiefly M. canis and M. audouinii — produces a blue-green fluorescence of infected hairs; M. gypseum is also ectothrix but does not fluoresce. Endothrix infection does not. In North America and much of the United Kingdom, the dominant cause of tinea capitis for decades has been Trichophyton tonsurans, an endothrix organism that does not fluoresce. A dark Wood lamp exam therefore excludes essentially nothing in those regions. The epidemiology is genuinely geographic — know what predominates where you practice.

Pitfall

Washing the area immediately before examination removes water-soluble porphyrins and turns a true erythrasma Wood lamp negative. Conversely, topical products, soap and detergent residue, lint, and some medications fluoresce and generate convincing false positives. Ask what the patient applied and when.

Student pearl

Ectothrix versus endothrix is not trivia. It predicts Wood lamp behavior, it tracks with likely species and therefore with likely source — Microsporum canis points at a kitten or puppy, T. tonsurans points at human-to-human spread and household screening — and it shapes the public health conversation you have with the family. Microscopy of a plucked hair answers it the same afternoon: arthroconidia sheathing the outside of the shaft with a destroyed cuticle is ectothrix; arthroconidia packed inside an intact shaft is endothrix.

What the laboratory does next, and how long it takes

Culture requestedTypical mediaIncubationWhat you should expect
Dermatophyte / superficial fungal Sabouraud dextrose agar with cycloheximide to suppress saprophytic molds and chloramphenicol or gentamicin to suppress bacteria; or dermatophyte test medium (DTM) Around 25–30 °C, held for weeks, not days — commonly up to about four weeks, though holding periods vary by laboratory Identification from colony morphology plus microscopic macroconidia and microconidia, often on a lactophenol cotton blue mount or slide culture; increasingly confirmed by MALDI-TOF MS or sequencing
Routine bacterial Blood, chocolate, MacConkey agar; anaerobic media for deep or foul collections 35–37 °C; growth usually evident within 18–24 hours Gram stain the same day; identification and susceptibility typically within two to three days of collection; anaerobes held longer
Mycobacterial (AFB) Automated liquid broth systems plus solid media; requires a decontamination step for non-sterile specimens Rapid growers may flag within a week; slow growers take weeks; cultures are typically held six to eight weeks Must be requested explicitly. It is a separate order with separate media and processing — it does not come free with a bacterial culture
Deep / dimorphic fungal Fungal media without cycloheximide in parallel, because cycloheximide inhibits many non-dermatophyte molds and several true pathogens Around 25–30 °C for the mold phase; prolonged holds Dimorphic fungi such as Sporothrix are recovered as molds at lower temperature and convert to yeast at 37 °C; several dimorphs are laboratory hazards on the plate
Advanced pearl

Cycloheximide is the reason the standard dermatophyte plate is so clean — and the reason it can silently lose your organism. It suppresses saprophytic contaminants, but it also inhibits many non-dermatophyte molds and a number of genuine pathogens, including Cryptococcus neoformans and various filamentous fungi. So if you are chasing a non-dermatophyte mold in a nail, or any deep fungal infection, ask for a cycloheximide-free medium set up in parallel with the routine one. Most laboratories will do it without argument; almost none will do it unprompted.

Pitfall

Dermatophyte test medium is a screen, not an identification. Dermatophytes generate alkaline metabolites that turn the phenol red indicator red, which is useful for a quick yes-or-no — but some non-dermatophytes produce a color change on prolonged incubation, and the medium tells you nothing about species. A red DTM is a reason to look down a microscope, not a reason to name an organism in the chart.

Temperature, supplements, and the request slip

This is the most genuinely high-yield laboratory point in cutaneous infectious disease, and it takes one sentence on a requisition.

Cutaneous mycobacteria run cool

Skin is not 37 °C

The skin surface is cooler than core, and several mycobacteria have adapted to exactly that. Mycobacterium marinum — fish tank and swimming pool granuloma, classically sporotrichoid on the hand and forearm — grows best at around 30 to 32 °C and grows poorly or not at all at 37 °C. M. ulcerans, the agent of Buruli ulcer, is similar and extraordinarily slow. If the requisition does not say you suspect a cutaneous mycobacterial infection, a laboratory may reasonably incubate at 35–37 °C only, and the culture comes back negative on an organism that was there the whole time.

M. marinum optimum~30–32 °C
Exposure to ask aboutAquariums, fish, brackish water

Some need a supplement, not just a temperature

Growth requirements you must declare

Mycobacterium haemophilum is the sharpest example: it needs both reduced incubation temperature and an iron source such as hemin or ferric ammonium citrate. Without the supplement it simply will not grow, no matter how long you wait. It is a recurring cause of ulcerating and nodular skin disease in transplant recipients and other immunosuppressed patients — precisely the population in whom a negative culture is most likely to be believed.

M. haemophilumLow temp + iron supplement
M. lepraeNot cultivable at all
  • Prolonged incubation for Cutibacterium acnes. Formerly Propionibacterium acnes, it is slow-growing and anaerobic to aerotolerant, and standard incubation periods can miss it. Where it matters — implant- and device-associated disease, some indolent postprocedural inflammation — you must ask for extended anaerobic incubation, often on the order of one to two weeks.
  • Nocardia needs patience and the right stain. Branching filamentous, partially acid-fast, and often overgrown by faster bacteria. Prolonged incubation and, in some labs, selective media are needed, and susceptibility testing typically goes to a reference laboratory.
  • M. leprae cannot be cultured on artificial media. Diagnosis rests on slit-skin smears and biopsy with a Fite stain, supported by PCR. Do not order a culture and wait.
  • Warn the lab about hazard organisms. Suspected Francisella tularensis in an ulceroglandular presentation, Brucella, and the dimorphic fungi are laboratory-acquired infection risks in their mold or culture phase. Telling the laboratory is not only a yield issue; it is a safety obligation to the technologist who will open the plate.
  • Prior antimicrobials change everything. Say what the patient has already received. It reframes a negative culture, and it may push the lab or the clinician toward molecular methods that do not depend on viability.
What to do next

Before a punch for suspected atypical infection leaves the room, write four things on the slip: the organisms you suspect, the exposure that raised them, the patient’s immune status, and any antimicrobials already given. Then add the explicit request — “bacterial, fungal, and mycobacterial culture; please include incubation at approximately 30 °C and prolonged hold.” If the differential is genuinely unusual, phone the microbiology laboratory before you biopsy rather than after.

Molecular testing: what it buys and what it costs

Where molecular has already won

Standard of care, not a luxury

HSV and VZV PCR from a lesion swab is now the standard approach and is substantially more sensitive than viral culture, direct fluorescent antibody, or Tzanck. It types the virus in the same run, which Tzanck can never do.

Dermatophyte PCR returns in days rather than weeks and generally outperforms culture on sensitivity, particularly on nail. Availability, target coverage, and reimbursement vary widely by country and by laboratory.

Mycobacterial PCR and broad-range sequencing — 16S ribosomal RNA gene sequencing for bacteria, internal transcribed spacer (ITS) or 28S sequencing for fungi — are the salvage pathway for the culture-negative granulomatous biopsy, especially in a patient already on antibiotics.

NAAT for sexually transmitted infections is the reference method for gonorrhea and chlamydia, including lymphogranuloma venereum genotyping in anogenital ulcer and proctitis.

What nucleic acid does not tell you

Three structural limits

It does not prove viable infection. PCR detects nucleic acid, including nucleic acid from dead organisms. A positive dermatophyte PCR on a nail weeks into terbinafine does not establish treatment failure, and residual DNA can persist after successful therapy.

It does not give susceptibility. With narrow exceptions where a specific resistance determinant is targeted, molecular testing yields no minimum inhibitory concentration and no phenotypic profile. For anything you will treat for months — mycobacteria, Nocardia — you still want an isolate.

It only finds what it looks for. A targeted panel is blind to any organism outside its target set. Broad-range sequencing is the answer to that, but it is slower, is often a send-out, and is vulnerable to background contamination that requires experienced interpretation.

Pitfall

Formalin fragments DNA. Molecular testing on a paraffin block works, but yield falls, and a negative result on fixed tissue is much weaker evidence than a negative on fresh tissue. Fixation is a salvage route, never the plan.

Histopathology as microbiology: the stains, by name

Requesting a special stain by name, with a reason, is a core dermatology skill. Each of these answers a different question, and one of the pairings is a genuine board-level discriminator.

StainHighlightsAppearanceWhen you specifically ask for it
Periodic acid–Schiff (PAS) Fungal cell wall polysaccharides Magenta organisms; also stains glycogen and basement membrane, so a diastase-digested version is used to remove glycogen Suspected dermatophyte, deep fungal infection; and on nail plate, where it is more sensitive than culture
Grocott methenamine silver (GMS) Fungi, including sparse or degenerate forms; also Pneumocystis Black organisms against a pale green counterstain — high contrast, easy to scan at low power When organisms are expected to be scanty, or when PAS is equivocal
Fite (Fite–Faraco, modified acid-fast) Mycobacterium leprae and Nocardia Red bacilli; the gentler modified protocol preserves weakly acid-fast walls that harsher decolorization strips Ask by name for suspected leprosy or nocardiosis — a standard acid-fast stain can miss both
Ziehl–Neelsen / Kinyoun Acid-fast mycobacteria generally Red bacilli against a blue counterstain Suspected tuberculous or non-tuberculous mycobacterial infection — but pair it with Fite if leprosy or Nocardia is in the differential
Gram tissue stains (Brown–Hopps, Brown–Brenn) Bacteria in tissue sections Gram-positive organisms blue-purple, gram-negative red — tissue Gram stains are technically harder to read than a smear Suspected bacterial folliculitis, botryomycosis, or an unexpected bacterial component in a granulomatous process
Warthin–Starry (silver impregnation) Spirochetes and Bartonella Black filamentous or pleomorphic organisms on a golden-brown background; notoriously prone to background precipitate Suspected syphilis or bacillary angiomatosis — though treponemal immunohistochemistry is more specific and easier to interpret where available
Giemsa Leishmania amastigotes; useful on smears and touch preparations Amastigotes as small intracellular bodies with a nucleus and a rod-shaped kinetoplast — the kinetoplast is the diagnostic feature Suspected cutaneous leishmaniasis, on a slit-skin smear or tissue touch prep; molluscum inclusion bodies are also demonstrable on a crush preparation of expressed core, though they are already obvious on routine sections
Mucicarmine / Fontana–Masson Cryptococcus — capsular mucin and cell wall melanin respectively Rose-red capsule on mucicarmine; Fontana–Masson catches capsule-deficient forms that mucicarmine misses Suspected cutaneous cryptococcosis, especially in an immunosuppressed host with umbilicated papules
Advanced pearl

The Fite versus Ziehl–Neelsen distinction is worth committing to memory as a mechanism rather than a fact. Standard acid-fast protocols use vigorous acid-alcohol decolorization, which strips the weakly acid-fast envelope of M. leprae and Nocardia. The Fite modification uses a gentler, oil-based deparaffinization and milder decolorization, preserving that fragile acid-fastness. Same principle, different aggressiveness — which is why “AFB stain negative” on a plaque you genuinely suspect is leprosy should prompt you to ask which stain was actually run.

Student pearl

Hold the pairing in your head as a division of labor. Stains tell you a class of organism, within hours. Fungus. Acid-fast bacillus. Spirochete. Culture tells you a species and a susceptibility profile, over days to weeks. Neither substitutes for the other, and the species and the susceptibilities are what determine which drug and for how long. This is why every biopsy taken for suspected infection should be accompanied by tissue for culture — the decision to send histology alone is a decision to accept a category instead of an answer.

How to talk to the microbiology lab

  • Name the suspects. “Rule out infection” produces a routine workup. “Sporotrichoid nodules on the forearm; considering M. marinum, Sporothrix, Nocardia” produces the correct media, the correct temperatures, and the correct holding period.
  • Give the exposure. Aquarium and fish handling, gardening and rose thorns, soil, freshwater or saltwater, hot tubs, animal contact, occupational contact, recent travel, nail salons, tattooing and cosmetic procedures. Exposure history is the single line that most often reframes a specimen.
  • State the immune status. Neutropenia, solid organ or hematopoietic transplant, tumor necrosis factor inhibitor or other biologic therapy, systemic corticosteroids, advanced HIV. It widens the plausible organism list and changes how hard the lab looks and how long they hold.
  • List prior and current antimicrobials. Including topicals. It explains a sterile culture and justifies a molecular request.
  • Describe the specimen honestly. Site, depth, whether it is superficial or from debrided base, whether it is one of two punches, and the time of collection. The lab processes tissue and swabs differently, and a mislabeled specimen gets the wrong protocol.
  • Ask what container they want before you cut. A thirty-second call to microbiology prevents the most expensive errors in this entire section. Laboratories differ in transport media, in whether they accept dry swabs for PCR, and in what they can and cannot set up on site.
  • Say when the answer would change management. Laboratories can prioritize, add stains reflexively, and phone preliminary results — but only if they know the clinical stakes.
Why it matters

Microbiology is one of the few laboratory disciplines where a conversation genuinely changes the result. A chemistry analyzer does not care what you suspect. A microbiologist deciding which plates to set up, at which temperature, held for how long, and which stains to run cares enormously. Residents who build a working relationship with their micro lab solve cases their peers send out.

Traps worth rehearsing before rounds

The patient has been using clotrimazole for three weeks. Can I still scrape?

You can, but interpret a negative with real caution. Recent antifungal exposure suppresses organism recovery on both microscopy and culture, and this is one of the commonest causes of a “KOH negative” tinea. Common practice is a washout of roughly a couple of weeks off topical agents and longer off systemic agents before repeat sampling, but the exact interval is not universally standardized — follow local guidance. If washout is not feasible, favor a more sensitive route: nail plate PAS rather than culture, or PCR where you have it.

The same logic applies to a partially treated bacterial or mycobacterial process. Antibiotics before biopsy are a reason to add molecular testing, not a reason to trust the sterile culture.

The nail culture grew a mold. Is that the pathogen?

Not necessarily, and this is a real interpretive problem rather than a technicality. Non-dermatophyte molds — Scopulariopsis, Fusarium, Aspergillus, Neoscytalidium and others — are common environmental contaminants of nail, and a single isolate proves nothing on its own. The conventional approach is to require corroboration: fungal elements on direct microscopy or nail histology, repeated isolation of the same mold from separate samplings, and absence of a dermatophyte on culture.

It matters therapeutically, because non-dermatophyte molds and Candida do not respond to the same agents as dermatophytes, and terbinafine-first reasoning can fail. This is also the situation where the cycloheximide problem bites in reverse: the standard dermatophyte plate may have suppressed the very mold you are debating.

The Wood lamp was negative. Have I excluded tinea capitis?

No, and in much of North America and the United Kingdom a negative Wood lamp excludes almost nothing, because Trichophyton tonsurans is an endothrix organism that does not fluoresce and has predominated there for decades. Fluorescence is a property of certain Microsporum ectothrix infections, not of tinea capitis as a category.

The correct next step is not a brighter lamp. It is a hair pluck with roots, black-dot stubs, and broken hairs, sent for microscopy and culture — guided, if you have a dermatoscope, by the comma and corkscrew hairs that mark which shafts are actually parasitized.

I ordered a fungal culture on the biopsy. Will that catch a mycobacterium?

No. Bacterial, fungal, and mycobacterial cultures are three separate orders with different media, different processing including a decontamination step for mycobacteria, different temperatures, and different holding periods. A fungal culture will not report an acid-fast organism, and a routine bacterial culture will not hold long enough for a slow grower.

For a granulomatous or otherwise atypical cutaneous process, order all three by name on the same fresh specimen, and add the temperature request. It is the difference between one biopsy and three.

Histology says “suppurative granulomatous dermatitis, no organisms seen.” What now?

Treat that phrase as a prompt, not a conclusion. Work through it in order. First, was tissue ever sent for culture, or did the whole specimen go to formalin? If the latter, that is the fixable problem, and a fresh repeat biopsy is the answer. Second, which stains were actually performed — if leprosy or Nocardia is plausible, was a Fite run, or only a standard acid-fast? Third, was the specimen deep enough to reach the compartment where the process lives? Fourth, had the patient received antimicrobials before biopsy?

If tissue culture was sent, is genuinely negative, and the clinical picture still says infection, that is the point at which broad-range 16S or ITS sequencing on tissue earns its cost — and the point at which you also revisit the non-infectious granulomatous differential rather than escalating antimicrobials indefinitely.

Why not just swab the ulcer? It is faster and the patient prefers it.

Because the answer it produces is not the answer you need. A chronic ulcer surface carries a polymicrobial biofilm that is present whether or not the wound is infected, so a surface swab reliably returns a list of organisms and no information about which, if any, is driving disease. Acting on that list means antibiotics chosen for colonizers, selection pressure, and a patient no better.

If the question is “is this wound infected,” that is a clinical determination supported by tissue from the debrided base — and if the ulcer has failed to heal despite adequate wound care, the more valuable question is often histologic rather than microbiologic, since the differential includes pyoderma gangrenosum, vasculitis, vasculopathy, and malignancy alongside infection. Send both.

Turnaround times, holding periods, available molecular assays, transport media, and which tests are run on site versus referred all vary substantially between institutions and between countries. Treat the intervals above as orientation, and confirm the specifics with your own laboratory once — then you will know them for the rest of residency.

Bacterial infections

Pyodermas, cellulitis, toxins, and the exposure organisms

Bacterial skin and soft tissue infection

Almost all of bacterial dermatology resolves into a short list of organisms behaving in predictable ways. The clinical morphology you already read fluently is, in effect, a phenotype of the organism. What follows organizes that by pattern, and at each step names the specimen the laboratory actually needs, what it can and cannot do with it, and where the traps are.

Staphylococcus aureus

Follicular, purulent, walled-off

Gram-positive cocci in clusters. It colonizes follicular ostia and the anterior nares, and its lesions are centered on the follicle or on a discrete collection: folliculitis, furuncle, carbuncle, abscess, bullous impetigo. The morphologic signature is pus with a border.

CatalasePositive
CoagulasePositive
Species IDMALDI-TOF mass spectrometry from a colony, minutes
MRSA detectionCefoxitin disk as surrogate, PBP2a latex, or mecA/mecC PCR
Advanced pearl

Cefoxitin is a screening surrogate, not a treatment. It induces mecA expression more reliably than oxacillin does, so the laboratory tests cefoxitin and reports oxacillin. If the isolate is methicillin-resistant S. aureus (MRSA), assume every beta-lactam is inactive except the anti-MRSA cephalosporins such as ceftaroline, regardless of what a panel appears to show.

Streptococcus pyogenes

Spreading, non-purulent, lymphangitic

Gram-positive cocci in chains, group A. It spreads through dermal lymphatics rather than pocketing, so it gives you sheet-like erythema, an advancing edge, lymphangitic streaking and regional adenopathy, with little or no pus to aspirate. Erysipelas, non-purulent cellulitis, ecthyma, and the toxin syndromes.

CatalaseNegative
HemolysisBeta on sheep blood agar
Presumptive IDPYR positive, bacitracin susceptible, Lancefield group A latex
PenicillinUniversally active
Student pearl

No clinically significant penicillin resistance has ever been documented in S. pyogenes, which is why laboratories do not routinely report a penicillin minimum inhibitory concentration for it. Macrolide and clindamycin resistance, by contrast, is real and regionally variable, so check your own antibiogram before choosing either.

Why it matters

The Infectious Diseases Society of America organizes skin and soft tissue infection around a single question asked at the bedside: is there pus? That split is not academic. Purulent disease is staphylococcal until proven otherwise, is drainable, and gives you a culturable specimen, so you drain it and you culture it. Non-purulent disease is streptococcal until proven otherwise, has nothing worth culturing, and does not need empiric MRSA coverage in uncomplicated cases. Getting the branch right determines whether you send anything to the laboratory at all.

1
Is there purulence, an abscess, or a drainable collection?

Palpate for fluctuance; use bedside ultrasound if you are unsure, because it changes management more often than examination alone.

Purulent

Staphylococcal pathway. Incision and drainage is the treatment. Culture the pus, not the skin over it. Cover MRSA empirically if there is systemic illness, immunosuppression, or failure of drainage alone, then narrow to the susceptibility report.

Non-purulent

Streptococcal pathway. Cellulitis and erysipelas. Cultures are low-yield and generally not indicated. Treat with a beta-lactam active against streptococci. Routine empiric MRSA coverage is not required in uncomplicated non-purulent cellulitis.

2
Before you commit to either branch, ask whether this is an infection at all.

If the process is bilateral, chronic, itchy rather than painful, scaly, or afebrile with a normal white count, the pretest probability of cellulitis collapses. Work the mimic list first.

3
Then ask whether it is necrotizing.

Pain out of proportion, rapid hourly progression, systemic toxicity, crepitus, hemorrhagic bullae, or anesthetic skin means the next call is to surgery, not to the laboratory.

What the laboratory actually needs from you

The single most common reason a skin culture is uninformative is that the wrong material was collected. A dry swab dragged across intact or crusted skin samples the resident flora and nothing else. Match the specimen to the compartment the organism occupies.

Clinical situationSend thisDo not sendWhat the laboratory can tell you
Abscess, furuncle, carbuncleAspirated pus or a swab of the cavity wall after incision, in transport mediumSwab of intact overlying skinGram stain, aerobic culture, species identification, full susceptibilities including MRSA
Uncomplicated non-purulent cellulitisUsually nothingSurface swabs, routine blood culturesVery little. Yield is poor enough that a negative result gives false reassurance
Cellulitis with immunosuppression, neutropenia, immersion injury, or an animal biteBlood cultures, and consider punch biopsy for cultureSurface swab aloneThese are the recognized carve-outs where blood cultures are worth drawing
Bulla or vesicleAspirate the blister fluid with a needle and send itA swab of the roof or dried crustIn bullous impetigo the organism is present in the fluid; in scalded skin syndrome it is not
Ulcer, eschar, retiform purpura, deep or granulomatous lesionPunch or incisional biopsy split in two: one half in formalin for histology, one half in a sterile container with a few drops of saline for cultureThe whole specimen in formalinFormalin kills everything. A biopsy that goes only to pathology cannot be cultured later
Necrotizing infectionDeep tissue taken at debridement, aerobic and anaerobicWound surface swabSurface flora will not tell you what is destroying the fascia
Suspected anaerobes, deep or perineal collectionsAspirated pus in an anaerobic transport deviceA dry swab left on the counterAnaerobes die on exposure to air; the transport device is the whole game
Blood cultures, when indicatedTwo sets, adequate volume, before antibiotics if that does not delay themA single small-volume setIn adults, blood volume is the strongest determinant of yield
Pitfall

Read the Gram stain comment, not just the culture. A report describing abundant squamous epithelial cells is telling you the specimen sampled the skin surface rather than the lesion, and the organisms grown from it are colonizers. Equally, MALDI-TOF gives you a species name within minutes of a colony appearing, but it says nothing whatsoever about susceptibility. Identification and susceptibility are separate questions on separate timelines, and treatment decisions made on the identification alone are guesses.

The superficial pyodermas

Non-bullous impetigo accounts for the large majority of cases. Neutrophils are recruited into the subcorneal epidermis and the pustule ruptures, leaving the golden adherent crust. S. aureus predominates in most contemporary settings, with S. pyogenes contributing, and mixed infection is common.

Bullous impetigo is a different mechanism entirely. It is toxin-mediated: S. aureus at the site elaborates exfoliative toxin A or B, serine proteases that cleave desmoglein 1, producing a subcorneal split whose cavity contains neutrophils and occasional acantholytic cells — in contrast to scalded skin syndrome, where the same cleavage plane is strikingly free of inflammatory cells because the organism is elsewhere. Because the toxin acts locally, the organism is present in the lesion and blister fluid is culturable.

Culture is often unnecessary for uncomplicated impetigo treated topically. Send one where MRSA prevalence is high, where there are recurrent or extensive lesions, in an outbreak setting, or after topical therapy has failed. Aspirate a fresh bulla or swab beneath a lifted crust rather than swabbing dried crust.

Advanced pearl

Desmoglein 1 is the shared target of bullous impetigo, staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome and pemphigus foliaceus. Toxin in the first two, autoantibody in the third, identical cleavage plane in all three. It is also why all three spare mucosa, where desmoglein 3 compensates.

Streptococcal impetigo can be followed by post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis with nephritogenic strains. Acute rheumatic fever classically follows pharyngeal, not cutaneous, infection, and treating impetigo does not reliably prevent the renal complication.

Ecthyma is impetigo that has broken through the basement membrane. The lesion becomes a punched-out ulcer with an adherent crust and an indurated violaceous rim, extends into the dermis, and therefore heals with a scar. It favors the lower legs, occlusion, poor hygiene, and preceding excoriation or arthropod bites.

S. pyogenes is the classic organism, frequently with S. aureus. Unlike impetigo, ecthyma warrants a specimen: lift the crust and swab or curette the ulcer base, and treat systemically rather than topically.

Do not confuse

Ecthyma and ecthyma gangrenosum are unrelated. Ecthyma is a superficial streptococcal ulcer in an otherwise well patient. Ecthyma gangrenosum is a necrotic eschar with an erythematous halo that reflects bacteremic vascular invasion, most often by Pseudomonas aeruginosa, usually in a neutropenic host. The first needs an oral antibiotic; the second needs blood cultures and urgent systemic therapy tonight.

A single continuum of staphylococcal follicular infection: folliculitis confined to the ostium, furuncle when the whole follicle and surrounding dermis suppurate into a single walled-off nodule, and carbuncle when adjacent follicles coalesce into a deeper multiloculated mass draining through several sinuses, typically on the nape, back or thigh, and typically with systemic symptoms.

Small furuncles resolve with warm compresses. Anything fluctuant is drained. Carbuncles usually need drainage plus systemic anti-staphylococcal therapy.

For recurrent furunculosis, culture at least once to establish whether the strain is MRSA, then consider decolonization: intranasal mupirocin, chlorhexidine or dilute bleach washes, and attention to household contacts and shared items. Protocols are institution-specific and evidence for durable benefit is modest, so present it as risk reduction rather than cure. If decolonization fails, ask the laboratory specifically for mupirocin susceptibility, since high-level plasmid-mediated resistance is not on the routine panel.

Community-associated MRSA lineages carrying Panton-Valentine leukocidin are associated with recurrent furunculosis, although the toxin’s causal contribution remains debated.

Erysipelas, cellulitis, and the thing that is neither

Erysipelas

Upper dermis and lymphatics

Sharply demarcated, raised, brightly erythematous, warm, with a border you can feel with a fingertip because the involved tissue is edematous and elevated above the adjacent skin. Onset is abrupt, often with fever and rigors preceding the visible change. Face and lower leg are the classic sites; peau d’orange texture and superficial bullae are common.

Usual organismBeta-hemolytic streptococci, chiefly group A
PortalTinea pedis, fissure, ulcer, lymphedema

Cellulitis

Deep dermis and subcutis

Deeper, so the erythema fades into normal skin without a palpable edge, the color is duskier, and induration is more diffuse. Systemic features are more variable. Non-purulent cellulitis is predominantly streptococcal; cellulitis surrounding an abscess or draining focus is predominantly staphylococcal.

Blood culture yieldLow in uncomplicated disease
Skin swab yieldNear zero on intact skin
What to do next

Treat the portal of entry, not only the leg. Interdigital tinea, fissuring and lymphedema are the reason cellulitis recurs, and treating interdigital tinea and controlling edema with compression address the causes of recurrence in a way no antibiotic choice for the acute episode does. For patients with repeated episodes despite that, prophylactic penicillin V or erythromycin reduces recurrence while it is being taken, with the benefit waning after it stops.

Do not confuse

Cellulitis and pseudocellulitis. This is the single most common error generating a dermatology consult. A frequently cited figure is that around 30 percent of patients admitted for presumed lower-extremity cellulitis have something else: stasis dermatitis, lipodermatosclerosis, allergic or irritant contact dermatitis, gout, deep vein thrombosis, erythema nodosum or another panniculitis, lymphedema, Wells syndrome, or a drug eruption. Bilateral lower-leg cellulitis is almost never cellulitis — bacteria do not arrive symmetrically. Other flags for a mimic are a chronic or fluctuating course, itch rather than pain, epidermal change such as scale or vesiculation, absent fever and normal white count, and no response after two or three days of an appropriate beta-lactam. Decision aids exist, most familiarly the ALT-70 score built on asymmetry, leukocytosis, tachycardia and age, but they support judgment rather than replace it. Dermatology consultation for suspected cellulitis has been shown to reduce unnecessary antibiotic use and unnecessary admission, which is a large part of why this section exists.

Pitfall

Do not resolve diagnostic uncertainty in cellulitis by ordering more microbiology. Surface swabs of intact skin grow colonizers, needle aspiration and punch biopsy for culture have modest yield even in expert hands, and blood cultures are positive in only a small minority of uncomplicated cases. A negative result does not exclude infection and a positive one is frequently a contaminant, so a low-yield test ordered into genuine uncertainty tends to mislead in both directions. Blood cultures earn their place in the recognized carve-outs: immunocompromise, neutropenia, malignancy on chemotherapy, immersion injury, and animal bites. Otherwise, reassess the diagnosis rather than the culture.

Abscess: drainage is the treatment

Incision and drainage cures most cutaneous abscesses. Antibiotics without drainage do not, because no agent reliably penetrates an avascular purulent cavity at bactericidal concentration. Open it, break loculations, and send the pus.

Adjunctive systemic therapy after drainage produces a modest but real improvement in cure rates in randomized trials of small uncomplicated abscesses, and it is conventionally added when any of the following are present:

  • Systemic inflammatory response or fever
  • Surrounding cellulitis, phlebitis or lymphangitis
  • Immunosuppression, poorly controlled diabetes, or extremes of age
  • High-risk anatomy: face, hand, genitalia, or overlying an indwelling device or prosthesis
  • Multiple abscesses, or failure of drainage alone at reassessment

Choose an agent with MRSA activity while awaiting culture where local prevalence justifies it, then narrow. The susceptibility report from a properly collected pus specimen is one of the few pieces of microbiology in dermatology that reliably changes management.

Advanced pearl

If the isolate is erythromycin-resistant but clindamycin-susceptible, look for a comment about the D-test. Erythromycin and clindamycin disks are placed adjacent to each other; flattening of the clindamycin inhibition zone on the side facing erythromycin produces a D shape and indicates inducible erm-mediated resistance. Clindamycin should then be reported and treated as resistant, because resistant subpopulations will be selected during therapy. Most laboratories now perform this reflexively, but confirm rather than assume it.

Necrotizing soft tissue infection: the can’t-miss

Necrotizing infection is a surgical diagnosis. Imaging and scoring systems can support it and can never exclude it, and every hour spent obtaining them while the patient deteriorates is an hour of fascial destruction. If the question has been seriously raised, the correct next action is a surgical consultation for exploration.

The findings that should raise it: pain out of proportion to visible signs, progression measurable in hours rather than days, systemic toxicity disproportionate to the skin, crepitus, hemorrhagic or violaceous bullae, dusky or gray discoloration, woody induration extending beyond the erythema, and anesthesia of the overlying skin from destruction of cutaneous nerves. Early on, the skin can look deceptively unremarkable over necrotic fascia, which is exactly why the examination understates the disease.

Recognized patterns are polymicrobial infection mixing aerobes and anaerobes, typically in diabetes or in the perineum; monomicrobial group A streptococcal infection, sometimes in previously healthy patients after minor trauma; and clostridial or marine Vibrio disease.

What to do next

Empiric therapy is broad, covering gram-positives including MRSA, gram-negatives and anaerobes, and conventionally includes a protein synthesis inhibitor such as clindamycin or linezolid whenever group A streptococcal or clostridial infection is possible. The rationale is toxin suppression: ribosomal inhibitors shut down exotoxin production independently of bacterial growth phase, whereas cell-wall agents are less effective at the very high inoculum found in necrotic tissue. Rising clindamycin resistance in group A streptococci in some regions is the main argument for linezolid. Intravenous immunoglobulin is used at some centers for streptococcal toxic shock; the evidence remains contested and practice varies by institution. The definitive specimen is deep tissue taken at debridement, sent for Gram stain with aerobic and anaerobic culture. A wound surface swab has no role.

Pitfall

A reassuring laboratory risk score does not exclude necrotizing infection. The LRINEC score and its relatives were derived to raise suspicion, and their sensitivity in prospective use has been disappointing enough that a low score in a patient who looks like this should be discarded rather than trusted. The same holds for computed tomography without gas: absence of fascial gas is unremarkable in streptococcal type II disease, where gas is not expected. Nothing in the laboratory or the scanner outranks the operative finding of gray, friable fascia that separates without resistance.

When the skin is the readout for a toxin

Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome

Exfoliative toxin, hematogenous

The same exfoliative toxins A and B as bullous impetigo, but disseminated from a distant focus rather than acting locally. Cleavage of desmoglein 1 gives a subcorneal split, so the skin sheets off in thin, moist, superficial layers with a positive Nikolsky sign, periorificial crusting and radial perioral fissures. Mostly young children, and adults with renal impairment or immunosuppression, in whom toxin clearance is reduced.

MucosaSpared
Blister fluid cultureSterile
Culture insteadNares, conjunctivae, throat, umbilicus, perineum, any wound, plus blood

Toxic shock syndrome

Superantigen, T-cell storm

Diffuse macular erythroderma resembling sunburn, fever, hypotension, multiorgan involvement, and desquamation of palms and soles one to two weeks later. Staphylococcal disease is driven by toxic shock syndrome toxin-1 and related enterotoxins; streptococcal disease by streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins, usually alongside an invasive soft tissue infection.

Advanced pearl

Blood cultures are usually negative in staphylococcal toxic shock, because the organism sits at a mucosal or wound focus and only the toxin disseminates. Culture the focus. In streptococcal toxic shock, blood cultures are usually positive, since it accompanies genuine invasive infection. Same syndrome, opposite microbiology.

Scarlet fever

Group A streptococcus

Pyrogenic exotoxin from a pharyngeal or, less often, cutaneous group A streptococcal infection. Fine sandpaper-textured erythema, accentuation in flexures with linear petechiae along the antecubital and axillary folds, circumoral pallor, strawberry tongue, and later desquamation of hands and feet.

Confirm withThroat culture, rapid antigen, or nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT)
TreatPenicillin class
Do not confuse

Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis. This is a standard board and consult question, and the answer lives in the cleavage plane. In SSSS, exfoliative toxin cleaves desmoglein 1 and the split is intraepidermal, subcorneal — the epidermis is viable, healing is rapid and scarless, and mucosa is spared. In TEN, the split is subepidermal at the dermal-epidermal junction with full-thickness epidermal necrosis, mucosal involvement at two or more sites is characteristic, and a culprit drug is nearly always identifiable. The fastest way to settle it is a frozen section of the sloughed blister roof, which can be reported within the hour: stratum corneum and granular layer only means SSSS, full-thickness epidermis with necrotic keratinocytes means TEN. The consequences of confusing them run in both directions — anti-staphylococcal therapy and supportive care versus drug withdrawal and burn-unit-level management.

Pseudomonas aeruginosa in the skin

A non-lactose-fermenting, oxidase-positive gram-negative rod that thrives in moisture and produces pigments you can see at the bedside. It grows readily at 42 degrees Celsius, which the laboratory uses to separate it from other pseudomonads, and it has a grape-like or corn-tortilla odor on the plate. Its resistance is a combination of intrinsic mechanisms and acquired ones, so susceptibility testing is mandatory and empiric choices should never be assumed to hold.

The benign end

Moisture, occlusion, colonization

Hot tub folliculitis. Follicular papulopustules appearing roughly one to two days after immersion, concentrated where a swimsuit held water against skin and sparing the head and neck. Self-limited over one to two weeks; treat the tub, not usually the patient.

Green nail syndrome. Green-black discoloration of an onycholytic nail from pyocyanin (blue-green) and pyoverdine (yellow-green, fluorescent) diffusing into the plate. The pigment is a marker of colonization of the subungual space, not invasion. Management is drying and mechanical: trim the onycholytic nail, eliminate wet-work and occlusion, and apply a topical acidifying or antipseudomonal agent. Systemic therapy is rarely needed.

Toe web intertrigo. Macerated, malodorous, often greenish interdigital spaces, frequently superimposed on a dermatophyte infection that opened the door. Treat both.

The dangerous end

Invasion and bacteremia

Otitis externa, and its necrotizing form in older diabetic patients, which is a skull base osteomyelitis presenting as ear pain and granulation tissue at the bony-cartilaginous junction. It requires imaging and prolonged systemic antipseudomonal therapy, not ear drops.

Ecthyma gangrenosum. A painless erythematous macule that evolves within a day or two into a hemorrhagic bulla and then a necrotic ulcer with a gunmetal or black eschar surrounded by an erythematous halo. Anogenital region and axillae are favored. It reflects bacterial invasion of dermal vessel walls during bacteremia, characteristically in a neutropenic host, and the biopsy shows organisms in vessel walls with strikingly little neutrophilic response.

What to do next

Ecthyma gangrenosum is a bacteremia until proven otherwise. Draw blood cultures, biopsy the lesion with half sent for culture and half for histology, request a Gram stain on the tissue for an answer within the hour, and start systemic antipseudomonal therapy immediately rather than waiting.

Pitfall

Ecthyma gangrenosum is a morphologic pattern, not an organism. P. aeruginosa is the most familiar cause, but other gram-negative rods, Aeromonas, S. aureus, Candida, and angioinvasive molds such as Fusarium and the mucormycetes produce an indistinguishable lesion in a neutropenic patient. Anchoring on Pseudomonas and covering only for it is how angioinvasive fungal disease gets missed. Biopsy for culture and histology, and tell the laboratory the patient is neutropenic so fungal stains and cultures are set up alongside bacterial ones.

The coryneform triad: nearly unique to dermatology

Three superficial overgrowths of coryneform bacteria that share a habitat of warmth, occlusion and hyperhidrosis, frequently coexist in the same patient, and are routinely misdiagnosed as fungal and treated with antifungals for months. None of them is normally diagnosed by culture; the diagnosis is made at the bedside with a Wood lamp and a clinical eye.

EntityOrganismWhere and whatBedside testPrinciple of treatment
ErythrasmaCorynebacterium minutissimumWell-demarcated red-brown finely scaly patches in groin, axillae, inframammary folds and toe websWood lamp coral-red fluorescence from bacterial coproporphyrin IIITopical azole, fusidic acid, or a topical or oral macrolide or related agent; moisture control
Pitted keratolysisKytococcus sedentarius and other keratinase producersCrateriform pits coalescing on the pressure-bearing plantar surface, with striking malodorInspect the sole after soaking; pits become obvious when hydratedAluminum chloride or another antiperspirant, topical clindamycin, erythromycin or benzoyl peroxide, and footwear change
Trichomycosis axillarisCorynebacterium speciesYellow, occasionally red or black, concretions adherent to the outside of axillary or pubic hair shaftsExamine the shaft: the material is stuck to the hair, not invading itShave or clip, topical antibacterial, antiperspirant
Pitfall

Two ways the Wood lamp lies to you in erythrasma. Coproporphyrin III is water-soluble, so if the patient has washed or if the area has been recently cleaned with an alcohol swab, the coral-red fluorescence is gone and you get a false negative — examine before anything touches the skin. Conversely, textile fibers, topical preparations and some other bacteria fluoresce, so fluorescence in an unusual distribution is not automatically erythrasma. A negative Wood lamp examination in a compatible intertriginous eruption does not exclude the diagnosis.

Do not confuse

Erythrasma, tinea cruris, candidal intertrigo and inverse psoriasis. All four occupy the same folds. Erythrasma is uniformly red-brown with fine wrinkled scale and no active edge, and fluoresces coral-red. Tinea has a raised advancing scaly border with central clearing and shows hyphae on potassium hydroxide preparation. Candidal intertrigo is beefy red, macerated at the fold, with satellite pustules. Inverse psoriasis is glazed, sharply marginated, without scale in the depth of the fold, and usually has evidence elsewhere. A potassium hydroxide preparation plus a Wood lamp examination separates them in under five minutes and prevents the very common outcome of an antifungal prescribed indefinitely for a bacterial overgrowth.

Cutibacterium acnes: commensal, driver, and prosthetic pathogen

In acne

Not a simple infection

An anaerobic, aerotolerant, pleomorphic gram-positive rod resident in the pilosebaceous unit. Its contribution to acne is not overgrowth in the ordinary sense; it is strain- and phylotype-dependent, mediated through biofilm, innate immune activation via Toll-like receptor 2, inflammasome-driven interleukin-1 beta release, and modification of sebum lipids in a follicle already altered by hyperkeratinization and androgen-driven sebum production. Total organism counts correlate poorly with disease severity.

That biology explains why antibiotics help partly through anti-inflammatory rather than antibacterial effects, and why they are an adjunct rather than the foundation of treatment.

What to do next

Stewardship principles that hold across guidelines: never use a topical antibiotic as monotherapy; always pair antibiotics with benzoyl peroxide, which reduces the emergence of resistant strains; limit oral antibiotic courses to the shortest effective duration, conventionally a few months, with a defined exit to a retinoid-based maintenance regimen. Macrolide and clindamycin resistance is now widespread, and tetracycline-class resistance is rising. Suspect gram-negative folliculitis when acne deteriorates into monomorphous pustules or nodules after months of oral tetracyclines: swab the pustules and the nares for Enterobacterales or Pseudomonas, and recognize that the treatment is isotretinoin, not a further antibiotic course.

In device and implant infection

The prolonged-culture organism

A separate and clinically important role. C. acnes causes indolent infection of shoulder arthroplasties, spinal hardware, cerebrospinal fluid shunts, breast implants and cardiac devices. Because the shoulder and upper back are richly sebaceous, it is disproportionately a shoulder pathogen. The presentation is pain, stiffness or radiographic loosening months to years after surgery, usually without fever and often with unremarkable inflammatory markers.

AtmosphereAnaerobic
IncubationHold 14 days
SpecimensMultiple periprosthetic tissue samples, not swabs
HardwareSonication of the explant to dislodge biofilm
Pitfall

A standard five-day culture will miss C. acnes and the result will come back sterile. You must ask the laboratory in advance to hold anaerobic cultures for a prolonged period, commonly fourteen days, because they will not do it by default. The mirror-image error is treating a single positive as proof of infection: C. acnes is a skin commensal and a frequent contaminant, so interpretation depends on multiple independent specimens growing the organism concordantly. Both failure modes come from the same fact — it is a normal inhabitant of the tissue you had to cut through to get the specimen.

Bites, animals and water: the exposure history that cracks the case

These organisms are recognized from the history, not from the morphology, and several of them are fastidious enough that the laboratory needs to be told what you suspect. Writing “cellulitis” on the requisition when you mean “cat bite to the hand” or “wound exposed to warm seawater” throws away the information that determines how the specimen is handled.

ExposureOrganismClinical signatureLaboratory and treatment notes

Aminopenicillin with a beta-lactamase inhibitor is conventional; first-generation cephalosporins, antistaphylococcal penicillins, clindamycin and macrolides have unreliable activity
Dog bite or even a lick, in an asplenic, cirrhotic or immunosuppressed hostCapnocytophaga canimorsusFulminant sepsis, purpura fulminans, symmetric peripheral gangrene, sometimes from a trivial woundFastidious, capnophilic, slow-growing thin gram-negative rod. Clenched-fist injury over the knucklesEikenella corrodens with oral floraThe “fight bite”: a small wound over a metacarpophalangeal joint that has already inoculated the joint capsule when the fist opensCapnophilic, pits or corrodes agar, bleach-like odor. Fish, shellfish, poultry, swine; butchers, fishmongers, veterinariansErysipelothrix rhusiopathiaeErysipeloid: a violaceous, sharply defined, burning rather than tender plaque on a finger or hand, spreading centrifugally with central fading, usually without fever or suppurationGram-positive rod, hydrogen sulfide positive in triple sugar iron agar, and intrinsically resistant to vancomycin — a genuine trap when vancomycin is the reflex gram-positive drug. Fresh or brackish water, water-related trauma, medicinal leechesAeromonas hydrophilaCellulitis that can progress to necrotizing infection and bacteremia, particularly in hepatic disease or malignancyOxidase-positive gram-negative rod. Conventional therapy pairs a tetracycline-class agent with a third-generation cephalosporin, alongside urgent surgical debridement. The organism is iron-avid, which is why liver disease and iron overload are the risk factors
Aquarium, fish tank, filleting injuryMycobacterium marinumIndolent nodule or sporotrichoid chain on the hand, weeks after the injuryRequires mycobacterial culture incubated at 30 °C, not 37, and it will be missed if the laboratory is not told to set it up that way

Spirochetes, rickettsiae and vector-borne disease with a cutaneous signature

Erythema migrans is a clinical diagnosis. In a patient with plausible exposure in an endemic area, an expanding erythematous patch at a plausible tick-attachment site is sufficient to treat, and serology should neither be ordered to confirm it nor waited upon.

The reason is kinetics. Antibody responses to Borrelia burgdorferi take weeks to develop, so standard two-tier serologic testing — an initial immunoassay followed by either an immunoblot or a second immunoassay — is insensitive in early localized disease. A negative result at the time of the rash is expected and means nothing. Serology also remains positive long after successful treatment, so it cannot be used to judge cure.

Morphology worth knowing: the lesion expands over days, is typically warm but not especially painful, and is often mildly pruritic or entirely asymptomatic. The classic central clearing “bull’s-eye” is the minority appearance — uniform, homogeneous erythema is at least as common and probably more so. Favored sites are the axilla, groin, popliteal fossa, waistband, and in children the hairline and neck. Multiple lesions indicate early disseminated disease.

Culture is not part of routine practice; Borrelia requires specialized medium and prolonged incubation. Polymerase chain reaction on a skin biopsy is available but adds little to a confident clinical diagnosis. Doxycycline class is first-line in adults, with amoxicillin and cefuroxime as alternatives.

Advanced pearl

In the southeastern and south-central United States, an erythema-migrans-like lesion after a lone star tick bite may be southern tick-associated rash illness, in which Lyme serology stays negative and the etiology remains undefined. Knowing the local tick fauna and the local Lyme incidence is part of the diagnosis.

Treponema pallidum cannot be grown on artificial media. Everything in the diagnostic pathway follows from that: direct visualization, molecular detection, tissue stains, or serology.

Direct methods. Darkfield microscopy of serous exudate expressed from a moist lesion — a chancre, condyloma lata or mucous patch — can show motile spirochetes, but requires immediate examination and an experienced observer, and is not valid on oral or rectal lesions because commensal treponemes are morphologically indistinguishable. Direct fluorescent antibody and treponemal PCR are offered by some reference and public health laboratories. On biopsy, the histology is a plasma-cell-rich lichenoid or psoriasiform infiltrate with endothelial swelling; immunohistochemistry for T. pallidum is considerably more sensitive and specific than Warthin-Starry or Steiner silver stains and is the preferred confirmatory stain.

Serology comes in pairs. Treponemal tests such as particle agglutination, enzyme or chemiluminescence immunoassays and fluorescent antibody absorption detect antibody to treponemal antigens and generally remain reactive for life, so they establish exposure but not activity. Non-treponemal tests, the rapid plasma reagin and VDRL, are quantitative, track disease activity, and are what you follow after treatment; a fourfold (two-dilution) change is the conventional threshold for significance, and serial titers should be run on the same assay and ideally the same laboratory. The traditional algorithm screens with a non-treponemal test and confirms with a treponemal one; the reverse-sequence algorithm screens with an automated treponemal immunoassay, reflexes to a non-treponemal test, and resolves discordance with a second, different treponemal test.

Clinical. Primary disease is the painless, indurated, clean-based chancre that heals without treatment. Secondary disease is the great mimic: a papulosquamous eruption characteristically involving palms and soles, condyloma lata in moist intertriginous sites, mucous patches, split papules at the oral commissures, generalized lymphadenopathy, and non-scarring “moth-eaten” alopecia. Test every patient for HIV, since syphilis is both a marker of and a cofactor for transmission. Warn about the Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction in the hours after treatment so it is not mistaken for drug allergy.

Rocky Mountain spotted fever. Fever, headache and myalgia come first; the rash usually appears a few days later and a substantial minority of patients never develop one at all. When it appears it begins on the wrists and ankles and spreads centripetally toward the trunk, involving palms and soles, evolving from blanching macules to petechiae as the underlying small-vessel infection progresses. Despite the name, incidence is concentrated in the south-central and southeastern United States.

The critical point is diagnostic timing. Serology by indirect immunofluorescence is retrospective: acute-phase antibody is usually absent, and confirmation depends on seroconversion in a convalescent sample weeks later. Whole-blood PCR has limited sensitivity because rickettsiae are largely confined to endothelium rather than free in plasma; PCR or immunohistochemistry on a biopsy of a petechial lesion performs better and is one of the few tests that can confirm during the acute illness, though it is a reference laboratory service in most places.

What to do next

Start doxycycline on clinical suspicion and do not wait for any test. Delayed treatment is strongly associated with mortality, and doxycycline is the drug of choice at every age, including young children; the historical concern about dental staining has been revised and should not drive management in a potentially fatal illness. Sulfonamides may worsen rickettsial disease and should be avoided when it is under consideration.

An inoculation eschar, the tache noire, points away from Rickettsia rickettsii and toward Rickettsia parkeri, African tick-bite fever or scrub typhus, which are generally milder. Presence of an eschar is a useful discriminator precisely because RMSF classically lacks one.

Cat scratch disease. Bartonella henselae inoculated by a kitten scratch or bite produces a small papule or pustule at the site within days, followed one to three weeks later by tender regional lymphadenopathy that dominates the picture. Parinaud oculoglandular syndrome is the conjunctival variant. Diagnosis rests on serology or PCR of tissue or node aspirate; culture is difficult and slow, requiring enriched media and prolonged incubation over weeks, so it is not a practical front-line test.

Bacillary angiomatosis. Caused by B. henselae or the louse-borne B. quintana, and seen in advanced HIV infection and other profound immunosuppression. Friable red to violaceous vascular papules and nodules that bleed readily, sometimes with a collarette, arising anywhere on the skin, with visceral counterparts including peliosis hepatis. It is exquisitely responsive to a macrolide or doxycycline-class agent given for a prolonged course, which is exactly why misdiagnosis is costly.

Do not confuse

Bacillary angiomatosis and Kaposi sarcoma. Both present as violaceous vascular papules and nodules in advanced HIV, and they are not reliably separable clinically. The answer is histologic. Bacillary angiomatosis shows a lobular vascular proliferation with plump epithelioid endothelial cells, a neutrophilic infiltrate with leukocytoclastic debris, and amphophilic clumps of bacilli demonstrable on Warthin-Starry silver stain. Kaposi sarcoma shows spindle cells, slit-like vascular spaces with extravasated erythrocytes, and is positive for HHV-8 latency-associated nuclear antigen by immunohistochemistry. One is cured with antibiotics; the other is not treated with antibiotics at all. Biopsy before you decide.

Pitfall

The prozone phenomenon. In florid secondary syphilis, antibody concentration can be so high that it saturates both antigen and antibody binding sites and prevents lattice formation, so the undiluted rapid plasma reagin reads negative or only weakly reactive in the patient with the most spirochetes. The laboratory resolves it by diluting the serum, but only if it thinks to. If your patient has a palmoplantar eruption, condyloma lata or moth-eaten alopecia and the RPR comes back non-reactive, telephone the laboratory, state that you suspect secondary syphilis, and ask for the specimen to be diluted and retested. The same call is worth making when the RPR is reactive at an implausibly low titer.

Meningococcemia and purpura fulminans

Retiform purpura with a rapidly deteriorating patient is a dermatologic emergency and, occasionally, dermatology is the service that recognizes it first. Neisseria meningitidis produces petechiae that coalesce into angulated purpuric plaques with central necrosis, sometimes progressing within hours to purpura fulminans with disseminated intravascular coagulation, acral gangrene and adrenal hemorrhage.

  • Antibiotics precede diagnostics. Do not delay therapy for lumbar puncture, imaging, or a biopsy.
  • Blood cultures before antibiotics only if that does not delay them. After antibiotics, culture sensitivity falls sharply, but nucleic acid amplification testing on blood or cerebrospinal fluid retains much of its yield, which is the single most useful modern point about this diagnosis.
  • The skin biopsy is a real contribution. A punch biopsy of a purpuric lesion sent for Gram stain, culture and PCR can establish the organism even after antibiotics have sterilized the blood. Split the specimen: half in formalin for histology, half in a sterile container for microbiology.
  • Screen survivors for terminal complement deficiency (C5 through C9 and properdin), and remember that patients on terminal complement inhibitors carry a markedly increased risk of invasive meningococcal disease.
  • Chronic meningococcemia is the version that gets missed: weeks of intermittent fever, migratory arthralgia and a sparse polymorphic eruption that reads as vasculitis or adult-onset Still disease until someone draws a blood culture during a febrile episode.
Clinical pattern

Retiform purpura means vaso-occlusion, and the differential is split between infectious and non-infectious causes that look identical at the bedside. Infectious: meningococcus, pneumococcus (especially in asplenia), S. aureus, group A streptococcus, Capnocytophaga, and angioinvasive fungi. Non-infectious: protein C or S deficiency, warfarin-induced skin necrosis, heparin-induced thrombocytopenia, antiphospholipid syndrome, calciphylaxis, cryoglobulinemia, and disseminated intravascular coagulation from any cause. Because you cannot tell them apart by morphology, every retiform purpura biopsy should be split between histology and tissue culture. That habit is one of the highest-yield things a dermatology resident can carry into a consult.

Talking to the microbiology laboratory

What should I write on the requisition that I probably am not writing?

The exposure, the host, the anatomy, and the question. “Cellulitis, right leg” tells the laboratory to set up a routine aerobic culture and nothing else. “Necrotic ulcer, neutropenic patient, on antibiotics, rule out angioinvasive infection” tells them to add fungal stains, extend incubation and call you with the Gram stain. Specifically flag: neutropenia or other immunosuppression, salt-water or fresh-water exposure, animal or human bite, foreign body or prosthesis, recent antibiotics, and travel. Laboratories cannot infer any of this from a specimen container, and several of the organisms in this section will simply not be recovered unless somebody chooses non-default conditions.

Which organisms in dermatology need something other than a standard five-day aerobic culture?

Enough of them that it is worth memorizing the list.

  • Cutibacterium acnes in a suspected device infection: anaerobic, held approximately fourteen days, on multiple tissue specimens.
  • Bartonella: prolonged incubation on enriched media over weeks with poor yield — use serology or PCR instead.
  • Capnocytophaga and Eikenella: capnophilic, requiring increased carbon dioxide and often extended incubation.
  • Mycobacterium marinum: mycobacterial media incubated at 30 degrees Celsius rather than 37.
  • Vibrio species: halophilic, best recovered when the laboratory knows to consider selective media.
  • Treponema pallidum, Rickettsia and Borrelia: not recovered by any routine culture, so the diagnostic pathway is serologic, molecular or histologic from the outset.
Why did the laboratory not report a susceptibility for the organism it grew?

Usually because susceptibility is predictable and testing would add nothing — S. pyogenes and penicillin being the standard example. Sometimes because the organism has no established interpretive criteria for that agent, since breakpoints are set by standards bodies such as CLSI on an organism-by-agent basis and not every combination has them. Sometimes because the isolate was judged to be normal flora or a contaminant and was not worked up further. And sometimes because the agent you want is simply not on the routine panel: mupirocin for decolonization failure is the common dermatologic example. In each case the fix is the same, which is to telephone and ask, since supplementary testing on a stored isolate is often possible for a few days after the report.

How do I decide whether a growth is a pathogen or a colonizer?

Weigh four things. First, the specimen: material from a closed space such as aspirated pus, a deep tissue sample or blood carries far more weight than anything swabbed from an open surface. Second, the Gram stain: organisms seen among neutrophils in the direct smear, in a morphology matching what grew, argue strongly for a pathogen, whereas squamous epithelial cells argue for surface contamination. Third, quantity and consistency: heavy pure growth, or the same organism from several independent specimens, is meaningful in a way that scant mixed growth is not. Fourth, biological plausibility: coagulase-negative staphylococci and C. acnes from a superficial swab are almost always passengers, but the same organisms from multiple periprosthetic tissue samples in a painful shoulder replacement are the diagnosis.

What is genuinely worth culturing in routine outpatient dermatology?

A short list, and being disciplined about it improves the signal from the ones you do send. Worth culturing: any drainable abscess or furuncle, especially with recurrence or treatment failure; impetigo that has failed topical therapy or is occurring in a high-MRSA or outbreak setting; ecthyma and other ulcers; any lesion in a neutropenic or transplant patient; suspected device or implant infection; wounds with an animal, human or water exposure; and any retiform purpura or necrotic eschar, split with histology. Generally not worth culturing: intact uncomplicated cellulitis, erythrasma, pitted keratolysis, trichomycosis axillaris, and acne, all four of which are clinical diagnoses where a culture result will describe the resident flora and tempt somebody into treating it.

Mycobacteria and the skin

Indolent, granulomatous, and easily missed

Cutaneous mycobacterial disease is a small domain with an outsized failure rate, and almost all of the failure happens in the specimen, not in the clinic. You will usually be right about the morphology. What decides whether the diagnosis is ever made is whether the microbiology laboratory was told what you were thinking — because mycobacteria are the one group of organisms where the default laboratory workflow is actively wrong for them. This block is written from the laboratory backwards.

Why mycobacteria break the standard workflow

They are slow

Weeks, not days

Routine bacteriology plates are read at 24 to 48 hours and discarded within a few days. Slowly growing mycobacteria need weeks in dedicated media — solid egg-based or agar-based media plus a liquid broth system — and the broth is what recovers most isolates. A specimen sent as a generic “wound culture” is thrown away long before a mycobacterium would ever have declared itself.

Typical hold, slow growers6–8 weeks
M. ulceransup to 12 weeks

They are fussy about temperature

The single most actionable fact

The skin-tropic mycobacteria are skin-tropic precisely because they prefer cooler-than-core temperatures. Incubators default to roughly 35–37 °C. If you do not ask for a second, cooler incubation, several of the organisms on your differential simply will not grow — and the report will read “no growth,” which you will misread as “not an infection.”

Standard set35–37 °C
Add for skin28–32 °C

They look like everything else

Granuloma is a pattern, not a diagnosis
