Histology gives you granulomatous inflammation, which is shared with deep fungi, sarcoidosis, foreign body reaction, and granulomatous rosacea. Acid-fast stains on tissue are specific but insensitive in paucibacillary disease, so a negative acid-fast stain in a lupus vulgaris plaque or a tuberculoid leprosy lesion is expected and excludes nothing.

Stain for tissue AFBZiehl-Neelsen or Kinyoun
Stain for M. lepraeFite (modified)
Why it matters

Every other organism in dermatology is found by a laboratory doing its normal job. Mycobacteria are found only when the laboratory does something extra, and the laboratory only does something extra when you ask. The request form is part of the diagnostic test. Write the suspicion and the exposure on it.

Mycobacterium marinum: the aquarium granuloma

The most commonly encountered non-tuberculous mycobacterium in dermatology practice, and the one where a single sentence on the requisition changes the result.

The clinical picture

History does the work

An indolent violaceous papulonodule or verrucous plaque on the dorsal hand, finger, or forearm, appearing weeks after minor trauma. It may ulcerate, and in roughly a quarter to a third of reported cases it ascends the forearm as a chain of nodules along the lymphatics — the sporotrichoid or lymphocutaneous pattern.

The exposure is the diagnosis: home aquarium maintenance, tropical fish handling, cleaning a tank without gloves, occupational fish or shellfish processing, or brackish and salt water. The historical name “swimming pool granuloma” predates routine pool chlorination and you will rarely see that source now.

Incubation to lesion2–6 weeks typically
Ask specifically“Do you keep fish?”

Patients do not volunteer aquariums. They volunteer gardening and cats. You have to name the fish tank.

The laboratory picture

Where cases are lost

M. marinum grows optimally near 30 °C and grows poorly or not at all at standard 37 °C incubation. It is a photochromogen: colonies develop a yellow pigment after light exposure. It is a slowly growing species, so plates need weeks, not days.

Optimal growth~30 °C
At 37 °Cpoor to absent growth
Specimentissue biopsy, not a swab
Runyon groupI, photochromogen

Treatment uses combinations drawn from the macrolides, tetracyclines, rifamycins, ethambutol, and sulfonamides, continued for months and typically for several weeks beyond clinical resolution. Following CLSI guidance, many laboratories do not routinely perform susceptibility testing on M. marinum because its pattern is predictable, reserving testing for patients who fail prolonged therapy.

Advanced pearl

Interferon-gamma release assays (IGRAs) use the RD1-region antigens ESAT-6 and CFP-10, which most non-tuberculous mycobacteria lack — but M. marinum, M. kansasii, and M. szulgai do carry them. A positive IGRA in a patient with a fish-tank nodule is therefore entirely compatible with M. marinum and is not evidence of tuberculosis. The older tuberculin skin test cross-reacts even more broadly.

Sporotrichoid (lymphocutaneous) nodulesExposure that cracks itWhat the laboratory needsTrap
Sporothrix schenckii complexRose thorns, sphagnum moss, hay, timber; zoonotic feline transmission in parts of BrazilTissue for fungal culture; thermally dimorphic — mold at ~25–30 °C, yeast at 37 °COrganisms are sparse in tissue; a negative GMS does not exclude it, culture is the standard
Nocardia brasiliensisSoil, plant or thorn trauma; the Nocardia species most associated with lymphocutaneous diseaseSay “Nocardia” out loud — branching filamentous gram-positive rods, partially acid-fast on modified stain, needs extended plate holdHarsh decontamination used for mycobacterial culture can kill it; routine plates are discarded before it appears
Mycobacterium marinumAquarium, fish handling, brackish or salt waterMycobacterial culture with an added 30 °C incubation and a prolonged holdGrows poorly at 37 °C — a single standard incubator yields a false “no growth”
Leishmania speciesSand fly bite; travel or residence in endemic Old or New World regionsTissue or lesion scraping for Giemsa-stained smear (amastigotes), specialized culture medium, and molecular testing with species-level identificationSpecies identification changes management, so send to a laboratory that can speciate rather than settling for “amastigotes seen”
Francisella tularensis (ulceroglandular tularemia)Rabbit or hare handling, tick or deer fly bite, lawn mowing over an infected carcassWarn the laboratory before sending. Fastidious, cysteine-requiring; serology and molecular testing are often used instead of cultureA genuine laboratory biosafety hazard with documented laboratory-acquired infections — an unannounced culture puts technologists at risk
Pitfall

Sporotrichoid spread is a pattern, not an organism. Do not anchor on Sporothrix because the nodules ascend. Order the workup that covers the whole list in one biopsy: histology with acid-fast, Fite, PAS and GMS stains, plus fresh tissue split for bacterial, fungal, and mycobacterial culture — and state the exposure so the laboratory sets up the right temperatures and holds.

Leprosy (Hansen disease)

Mycobacterium leprae, and the more recently characterized M. lepromatosis, are obligate intracellular organisms that cannot be grown on any artificial medium. There is no culture to order. The diagnosis is clinical, histopathologic, and bacteriologic by smear — which makes it one of the few infections where the dermatologist, not the microbiologist, holds the decisive test.

The cardinal signs

WHO diagnostic triad

Any one of three findings supports the diagnosis, and the first two are done with your hands in clinic.

  • A hypopigmented or erythematous skin lesion with definite loss of sensation.
  • A thickened or enlarged peripheral nerve, with sensory loss or weakness in its distribution.
  • Acid-fast bacilli demonstrated on slit-skin smear.

Palpate the nerves as part of the exam: ulnar at the medial epicondyle, greater auricular in the neck, common peroneal at the fibular head, posterior tibial behind the medial malleolus, radial cutaneous at the wrist, and the supraorbital nerve. Test light touch and pinprick in the lesion and compare it with adjacent normal skin.

Also look foranhidrosis, alopecia in the plaque

Why the distribution looks the way it does

Temperature again

M. leprae favors cooler tissues, which is why disease concentrates in the skin, superficial peripheral nerves, the nasal mucosa, the anterior eye, and the testes, and characteristically spares warmer sites such as the axilla, groin, perineum, scalp vertex, and the midline of the back. If a proposed leprosy lesion sits squarely in a warm zone, reconsider.

The organism also divides extraordinarily slowly — a doubling time on the order of two weeks — which explains incubation periods measured in years and the deliberately long treatment courses. Recognized zoonotic reservoirs include the nine-banded armadillo in the southern United States and red squirrels in the British Isles.

Cultivable on agarNo — never
Research propagationmouse footpad, armadillo

A vigorous cell-mediated (Th1-polarized) response contains the organism at the cost of destroying the nerve it lives in. Clinically: one or a few sharply demarcated plaques with a raised active border, dry and hairless because adnexal structures are destroyed, and markedly anesthetic. Nerve involvement is asymmetric and often a single large trunk, which can be dramatically thickened.

Bacillary loadpaucibacillary — bacilli rarely found
Histologywell-formed epithelioid granulomas tracking along and destroying dermal nerves; granulomas reach the epidermis

The paradox worth remembering: the more effective the immune response, the fewer the lesions, the more profound the anesthesia, and the harder the organism is to demonstrate.

Between the poles sit borderline tuberculoid, mid-borderline, and borderline lepromatous disease, described by the Ridley-Jopling classification. Lesion number rises and definition falls as you move toward the lepromatous end; annular plaques with a sharp inner and a sloping outer edge, and “punched-out” inner clearing, are characteristic of the mid-borderline zone.

Borderline states are immunologically unstable, which is exactly why type 1 reactions occur in this part of the spectrum. Separately, the WHO uses a simplified operational classification for treatment purposes based largely on lesion count and smear status — paucibacillary for a small number of lesions with a negative smear, multibacillary otherwise — because Ridley-Jopling classification requires histopathology that is unavailable in many endemic settings.

Ridley-JoplingTT — BT — BB — BL — LL
WHO operational splitpaucibacillary vs multibacillary

Poor M. leprae-specific cell-mediated immunity permits unrestricted multiplication. Clinically: numerous, symmetric, poorly-defined macules, papules, and nodules; infiltration of the face producing leonine facies; loss of the lateral eyebrows (madarosis); nasal congestion, epistaxis, and eventual septal collapse; and a glove-and-stocking sensory neuropathy from diffuse small-nerve involvement rather than discrete anesthetic patches.

Bacillary loadmultibacillary — very high
Histologysheets of foamy macrophages (Virchow cells) packed with bacilli, often in clumps (globi), beneath an uninvolved subepidermal Grenz zone

Diffuse lepromatous leprosy without discrete nodules, particularly as reported from Mexico and Costa Rica, has been associated with M. lepromatosis and with the Lucio phenomenon — a necrotizing vasculopathy producing angulated, retiform infarctive ulcers.

Slit-skin smear. The skin is pinched to bleach it, a small slit is made, the dermis is scraped, and the material is smeared and stained. Sites classically sampled include an active lesion edge plus standard sites such as both earlobes. Two indices are reported: a bacterial index, a logarithmic score of bacillary density, and a morphologic index, the proportion of uniformly solid-staining bacilli, which is taken as an estimate of viability. Availability of skin smears is institution- and region-dependent; in many centers biopsy carries the load.

Biopsy. Take a deep punch or incisional biopsy from the active edge, including subcutis, and request a Fite stain explicitly.

Pitfall

M. leprae is only weakly acid-fast and is readily decolorized by the standard Ziehl-Neelsen method. The Fite (Fite-Faraco) modification uses a gentler decolorization and an oil-containing deparaffinization step to preserve the acid-fastness. If you write “AFB stain,” you may receive a routine acid-fast stain and a falsely negative result. Write “Fite stain, rule out Hansen disease.”

Molecular detection of M. leprae DNA is available through reference and national programs and can help in paucibacillary or histologically ambiguous cases, but access varies by country.

Treatment. The WHO recommends multidrug therapy combining rifampicin, clofazimine, and dapsone. Check glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase status before starting dapsone, since haemolysis is dose-related and can be severe, and warn the patient that clofazimine causes reversible red-brown to violaceous skin pigmentation — the commonest reason patients abandon therapy. Regimens are given with the duration determined by classification — shorter for paucibacillary and longer for multibacillary disease. Multidrug therapy exists because dapsone monotherapy generated resistance historically. Leprosy is a notifiable disease in most jurisdictions; in the United States a national Hansen disease program provides expert consultation and supplies therapy, and analogous national programs exist elsewhere.

Reactions. These are immunologic events, not microbiologic failure, and they are the main cause of permanent disability.

Type 1 (reversal)delayed-type hypersensitivity; borderline forms; existing lesions become edematous and erythematous, with acute neuritis
Type 2 (ENL)immune-complex mediated; borderline lepromatous and lepromatous disease; crops of tender nodules plus fever, neuritis, iritis, orchitis, arthritis

Type 1 reactions are treated with systemic corticosteroids, with acute nerve function impairment treated as an emergency because the window to preserve function is short. Erythema nodosum leprosum is managed with corticosteroids and, in appropriate settings, thalidomide — which is highly effective but requires strict pregnancy-prevention controls and is restricted or unavailable in some countries — and higher-dose clofazimine.

Do not confuse

A reaction is not treatment failure and is not a drug allergy. Multidrug therapy is continued through the reaction while the reaction itself is treated. Stopping antimycobacterial therapy because the patient got worse on it is the classic error, and it costs nerve function.

Cutaneous tuberculosis and the tuberculids

Split this topic in two before you start: lesions that contain Mycobacterium tuberculosis, and lesions that are a hypersensitivity response to M. tuberculosis sitting somewhere else in the body. They are worked up differently and they are reported differently.

Lupus vulgaris

Paucibacillary, chronic, progressive

The classic chronic form in a previously sensitized host with moderate to high immunity. A slowly expanding reddish-brown plaque with a soft, atrophic scarring center and an advancing edge; head and neck predominate in European series, buttocks and lower limbs in South Asian series. Diascopy — pressing a glass slide or lens against the lesion — blanches the vascular component and reveals translucent brownish “apple-jelly” nodules.

Organism burdenvery low — AFB rarely seen
Cultureoften negative
Tuberculin / IGRAtypically strongly positive

Long-standing untreated lesions carry a recognized risk of squamous cell carcinoma arising in the scar.

Scrofuloderma

Contiguous spread — higher yield

Direct extension to the skin from an underlying tuberculous focus — most often a cervical lymph node, sometimes bone or joint. A firm subcutaneous nodule softens into a cold abscess, then breaks down into ulcers and sinus tracts with undermined bluish edges, healing with cord-like and puckered scars.

Because the lesion is fed directly by a caseating focus, bacillary load is comparatively high — this is the form of cutaneous tuberculosis in which acid-fast smear and culture from tissue and drainage are most likely to be positive. It is also your reminder to image the underlying node, bone, or joint rather than treating the skin in isolation.

Best yielddeep tissue and sinus wall
Do notsend only surface drainage

Tuberculosis verrucosa cutis

Exogenous reinoculation

Inoculation into the skin of an already sensitized, immunocompetent host. A solitary indolent verrucous plaque on the hand, knee, buttock, or foot with an irregular serpiginous border, sometimes discharging pus from fissures. The historical occupational name is “prosector’s wart,” from anatomists and pathologists handling infected tissue; contemporary cases follow contact with contaminated soil or sputum.

Organism burdenlow — paucibacillary
Mimicsverrucous carcinoma, chromoblastomycosis, deep fungal disease

Primary inoculation tuberculosis in a non-sensitized host is different: an ulcer with regional lymphadenopathy, and multibacillary, so smears are more often positive.

Acute miliary cutaneous tuberculosis

Hematogenous, multibacillary, urgent

Seen in profound immunosuppression — advanced HIV infection, transplant recipients, infants, patients on tumor necrosis factor inhibitors — as widespread crops of tiny erythematous papules, vesicles, pustules, or purpuric lesions in a patient who is systemically unwell.

Unlike the paucibacillary forms, organisms are abundant: acid-fast stains and cultures from skin are frequently positive, and skin biopsy can be the fastest route to a systemic diagnosis. Treat this as disseminated tuberculosis and escalate accordingly.

Skin biopsyhigh yield — send for culture
Also sendrespiratory specimens, blood mycobacterial cultures
Do not confuse

True cutaneous tuberculosis versus the tuberculids. The tuberculids — erythema induratum of Bazin (nodular vasculitis), papulonecrotic tuberculid, and lichen scrofulosorum — are hypersensitivity reactions to mycobacterial antigen, not sites of infection. Organisms are typically absent, acid-fast stains are negative, and cultures do not grow; molecular testing is inconsistent, though M. tuberculosis DNA has been reported in some cases, particularly erythema induratum. Tuberculin skin testing or IGRA is characteristically strongly positive. The point that matters clinically: a negative culture does not exonerate the patient. There is tuberculosis somewhere — most often pulmonary or nodal — and you are obliged to go find it. Resolution with antituberculous therapy is part of the diagnostic definition.

How do I tell the three tuberculids apart at the bedside?

Erythema induratum of Bazin — tender, deep, recurrent nodules and plaques on the posterior calves, classically in women, sometimes ulcerating; histology shows a lobular panniculitis with vasculitis. Note that identical histology occurs without tuberculosis, in which case it is called nodular vasculitis, so the label depends on the tuberculosis evaluation.

Papulonecrotic tuberculid — symmetric crops of dusky papules with central necrosis on extensor extremities, ears, buttocks, and knees, healing with varioliform (pitted, pox-like) scars.

Lichen scrofulosorum — grouped, subtle, skin-colored to reddish follicular lichenoid papules on the trunk, most often in children or adolescents with tuberculous lymphadenitis or bone disease. It is easy to overlook because it is asymptomatic and undramatic.

In all three, the diagnostic effort belongs to finding the occult focus: chest imaging, tuberculin or IGRA testing, node and bone evaluation as indicated, and consideration of HIV status.

Buruli ulcer (Mycobacterium ulcerans)

Recognizing it

Painless is the whole clue

Often described as the third most common mycobacterial infection of immunocompetent people worldwide, after tuberculosis and leprosy, and recognized by the WHO as a neglected tropical disease. Endemic foci concentrate in West and Central Africa — Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Benin, Cameroon, the Democratic Republic of the Congo — and in temperate southeastern Australia, with additional foci in Japan, South America, and elsewhere.

It begins as a painless nodule, plaque, or area of firm edema, most often on a limb, which breaks down over weeks into an ulcer with deeply undermined edges and a necrotic, slough-covered base. The subcutaneous necrosis extends well beyond the visible skin defect, which is why the wound is always larger than it looks.

Cardinal featurelarge ulcer, minimal pain
Systemic upsetusually absent

Transmission is environmental and linked to slow-moving or stagnant water; the precise route remains incompletely understood, with mosquitoes and native possums implicated in the Australian setting.

Confirming and treating it

Molecular first, culture slow

Laboratory confirmation in endemic programs rests principally on a polymerase chain reaction assay targeting the M. ulcerans insertion sequence IS2404, which is the WHO-recommended confirmatory test. Acid-fast smear from the ulcer base is supportive but less sensitive; histopathology shows extensive coagulative necrosis of subcutaneous fat with clumps of extracellular bacilli and a strikingly muted inflammatory infiltrate.

Confirmatory testIS2404 PCR
Culture temperature~29–33 °C
Culture time6–12 weeks

Treatment is antimicrobial, with an all-oral combination regimen — a rifamycin together with a macrolide — recommended by the WHO for a defined course, with surgery, wound care, and physiotherapy as adjuncts rather than the primary therapy. Paradoxical worsening during or shortly after treatment is recognized and reflects returning immune responsiveness, not failure.

Advanced pearl

M. ulcerans secretes mycolactone, a polyketide-derived macrolide lipid toxin encoded on a large plasmid. Mycolactone is simultaneously cytotoxic and locally immunosuppressive: a major mechanism is blockade of the Sec61 translocon, the channel through which host cells thread newly made proteins into the endoplasmic reticulum, so secreted and membrane proteins — including cytokines and immune mediators — are not produced. That single toxin explains the whole clinical picture: the necrosis, the sparse inflammatory infiltrate on histology despite abundant bacilli, and the analgesia that lets a patient walk in with a fist-sized ulcer and no complaint of pain.

Clinical pattern

Buruli ulcer is the one place in this block where a swab is acceptable: WHO diagnostic guidance directs sampling the undermined edge beneath the overhanging skin of an ulcerated lesion for PCR, with fine-needle aspiration used for non-ulcerated nodules and plaques. That is a targeted deep sample obtained through a swab, not a surface culture — the principle still holds.

Rapidly growing mycobacteria after procedures

This is the mycobacterial problem you are most likely to cause rather than merely encounter, and it belongs squarely to cosmetic and procedural dermatology. Runyon group IV organisms — the M. fortuitum group, M. chelonae, and the M. abscessus complex — are so named because they grow within about seven days on subculture, which makes them fast for mycobacteria and still far too slow for a routine plate.

  • Tattooing. Documented outbreaks of M. chelonae and related species traced to contaminated ink and to ink diluted with non-sterile tap water; lesions appear only within the areas of a single shade, classically the gray-wash regions, which is a striking and diagnostic distribution.
  • Nail salon whirlpool footbaths. Furunculosis on the lower legs after pedicures, classically M. fortuitum, arising from biofilm in the bath piping, and typically worse in clients who shaved their legs beforehand.
  • Mesotherapy, injectables, and liposuction. Nodules and abscesses at multiple injection points, following the map of the procedure.
  • Surgical and laser procedures. Wound infections and suture-line nodules after excision, augmentation, and resurfacing, appearing later than a conventional surgical site infection would.
  • Acupuncture, needling, and body piercing. Same principle, same timeline.

The unifying reservoir is water. Rapidly growing mycobacteria persist in municipal water systems and in biofilms, and they tolerate chlorine and some standard disinfectants far better than ordinary bacteria — which is why “we used tap water to dilute it” and “the equipment was disinfected” can both be true and still produce an outbreak.

Clinical pattern

Weeks — not days — after a procedure, the patient returns with persistent erythematous or violaceous nodules, fluctuant abscesses, or draining sinuses restricted to the treated area. They have already had one or two courses of a cephalosporin or an antistaphylococcal agent with no benefit, and cultures were reported as no growth. That combination, post-procedural plus antibiotic-unresponsive plus culture-negative, is the presentation. Biopsy it for mycobacterial culture rather than prescribing a third antibacterial.

Pitfall

Rapidly growing mycobacteria can grow on ordinary bacteriology media — and when they do, on a Gram stain they may appear as faintly staining or beaded gram-positive rods and be dismissed as diphtheroid contaminants, or appear after the plate has already been signed out and discarded. Two consequences: send a dedicated mycobacterial culture rather than relying on a routine wound culture, and if the laboratory calls about an unexpected late-appearing gram-positive rod from a post-procedure lesion, ask them to hold it and stain it for acid-fastness.

Advanced pearl

Susceptibility testing is mandatory for rapidly growing mycobacteria, and for the M. abscessus complex the macrolide result has a trap built into it. Many isolates carry erm(41), an inducible macrolide-resistance gene: the organism looks susceptible on a standard-length read and resistant only after extended incubation, so CLSI methodology calls for an extended macrolide read to unmask it. Whether the gene is functional depends on sequevar and on subspecies — M. abscessus subsp. massiliense carries a truncated, non-functional copy and generally remains macrolide-treatable, whereas subsp. abscessus frequently does not. M. chelonae lacks erm(41) altogether. This is precisely why subspecies-level identification, usually by molecular targets beyond 16S ribosomal RNA such as rpoB, hsp65, and erm(41) sequencing, is not academic: it changes whether an oral macrolide is a real option.

The mycobacterial line-up

OrganismExposure or settingClassic lesionLaboratory key
M. marinumAquarium, fish handling, brackish or salt waterHand or forearm nodule, often sporotrichoidIncubate at 30 °C; photochromogen; may cross-react on IGRA
M. leprae / M. lepromatosisProlonged close contact in endemic regions; armadillo contact in the southern United StatesAnesthetic plaques with thickened peripheral nerves, across a tuberculoid-to-lepromatous spectrumCannot be cultured; slit-skin smear plus biopsy with Fite stain
M. tuberculosis (skin)Endemic exposure, prior or concurrent tuberculosis, immunosuppressionLupus vulgaris, scrofuloderma, verrucosa cutis, miliary lesionsPaucibacillary forms are frequently smear- and culture-negative; tuberculin or IGRA usually strongly positive; PCR helpful but imperfect
M. ulceransSlow-moving or stagnant water in West and Central Africa, southeastern Australia, other fociPainless undermined ulcer with necrotic baseIS2404 PCR; culture at reduced temperature for 6–12 weeks; mycolactone toxin
M. fortuitum, M. chelonae, M. abscessusTattoo ink, footbaths, mesotherapy, liposuction, surgery, needling — water-bornePost-procedure nodules and abscesses appearing weeks later, unresponsive to standard antibacterialsRapid growers, under 7 days on subculture; susceptibility testing required, including an extended macrolide read for M. abscessus
M. haemophilumImmunosuppression — transplant, HIV, biologic therapy; also pediatric cervical lymphadenitisPainful ulcerating nodules on cool acral and periarticular skinNeeds iron-supplemented media (hemin or ferric ammonium citrate) and 30–32 °C — will not grow on a standard setup
M. kansasiiWater systems; often underlying lung disease or immunosuppressionVerrucous or sporotrichoid nodules, cellulitis-like plaquesPhotochromogen; carries RD1 antigens so IGRA may be positive
Student pearl

M. haemophilum is the cleanest illustration of the entire theme. It requires both a lower temperature and an iron-supplemented medium. No amount of clinical acumen recovers it if the requisition said “tissue culture.” The organism is not fastidious about your diagnosis — it is fastidious about the plate.

How to actually work this up

1
Take the exposure history before you take the biopsy

Aquarium or fish; fresh, brackish, or stagnant water; soil, thorns, or plant matter; travel and country of origin; any procedure, injection, tattoo, or pedicure in the preceding months; animal contact; immunosuppression and biologic therapy; known tuberculosis contact. The history determines what you write on the form, and the form determines what the laboratory does.

2
Biopsy deep and from the active edge

Use a generous punch or an incisional biopsy that reaches subcutaneous fat, taken from the advancing border rather than the necrotic center. Superficial shaves and surface swabs are the two commonest reasons a mycobacterial workup fails. Consider taking two specimens rather than dividing one small core.

3
Split the tissue — this is the step people get wrong

Half into formalin for histopathology. Half fresh, in a sterile container with a few drops of sterile non-bacteriostatic saline to prevent drying, delivered promptly to microbiology.

Formalin half

Request hematoxylin and eosin, an acid-fast stain (Ziehl-Neelsen or Kinyoun), Fite if leprosy is on the differential, and PAS plus GMS for fungi. Ask the pathologist to comment on granuloma pattern, necrosis, Grenz zone, and nerve involvement.

Fresh half

Request mycobacterial culture, fungal culture, and routine bacterial culture from the same specimen. Never place tissue intended for culture in formalin — it is unrecoverable, and it is the single most common irreversible error in this workup.

4
Tell the laboratory what you suspect, in words

Write the suspected organisms and the exposure on the requisition, and call for anything unusual. Specifically request incubation at both standard and reduced temperature, a prolonged hold, and supplemented media if M. haemophilum is possible. If tularemia or a similar hazard is being considered, warn the laboratory before the specimen arrives, not after.

5
Set expectations and do not stop the clock early

Tell the patient this takes weeks. Do not accept a preliminary negative at two weeks as a final answer, and do not let an interim negative trigger a fourth course of an antibacterial that was never going to work. Where systemic or disseminated disease is plausible, add mycobacterial blood cultures and imaging in parallel rather than sequentially.

6
Granulomas but no growth? Escalate the method, not the wait

If histology shows granulomatous inflammation and cultures remain negative at the end of the hold, consider molecular testing — targeted PCR or broad-range amplification with sequencing — on fresh tissue if any remains, or on the paraffin block, accepting reduced sensitivity from fixed material. A repeat biopsy taken with the full split protocol frequently outperforms molecular salvage on a suboptimal original specimen.

What to do next

Build one order set and reuse it: deep biopsy from the active edge, split formalin and fresh, stains for acid-fast plus Fite plus fungal, cultures for bacteria plus fungi plus mycobacteria, dual-temperature incubation, prolonged hold, and a free-text line stating the exposure and the suspected organism. Then add tuberculin or IGRA testing and chest imaging when tuberculosis or a tuberculid is plausible, and check HIV status when the presentation suggests immunosuppression.

What exactly should I say when I telephone the microbiology laboratory?

Keep it to five items and they will do the rest.

  • The specimen and where it is. “Fresh skin biopsy, no formalin, accession pending, sent from clinic at 2 pm.”
  • The suspicion, named. “I am concerned about Mycobacterium marinum, and I want to exclude Sporothrix and Nocardia.”
  • The exposure. “He cleans a saltwater aquarium bare-handed.” This single sentence is what makes the laboratory set up a second incubator.
  • The specific requests. Incubation at 30 °C as well as 35 °C; extended hold; supplemented media if relevant; extended macrolide read if a rapid grower is isolated.
  • Your contact details and a request for preliminary calls. Ask to be told when anything grows rather than waiting for the final report.

Laboratories are not obstructive; they are running a standard operating procedure that assumes an ordinary organism. You are the only person who knows the story.

Why can a mycobacterial culture be negative when acid-fast bacilli were seen on the slide?

Several reasons, and they are worth being able to recite.

The organism is not cultivable. M. leprae will never grow on artificial media, so bacilli on a Fite stain with a sterile culture is the expected result, not a discrepancy.

The organism was killed in processing. Specimens from non-sterile sites undergo chemical decontamination to suppress faster-growing flora. That step is hard on fragile mycobacteria and it is lethal to Nocardia, which is one reason Nocardia must be requested as its own culture rather than expected to fall out of a mycobacterial workup.

The conditions were wrong. Wrong temperature, no iron supplementation, or the plates were discarded before a slow grower appeared.

Prior antimicrobials. Weeks of a macrolide or a tetracycline given empirically for a “non-healing wound” can suppress growth without curing the infection.

Sampling. Necrotic ulcer center rather than the advancing edge, or too small a specimen for the number of tests requested.

Board-style rapid review

Ascending nodules on the forearm of a fish keeper. M. marinum — and the examiner wants the number 30 °C.

Anesthetic plaque with a thickened ulnar nerve. Hansen disease — and the examiner wants Fite stain and “cannot be cultured.”

Apple-jelly nodules on diascopy. Lupus vulgaris — and the examiner wants “paucibacillary, so expect negative smear and culture.”

Sinus tracts over a cervical node with cord-like scars. Scrofuloderma — contiguous spread, and the highest-yield form for culture.

Tender recurrent nodules on the posterior calves. Erythema induratum of Bazin — a tuberculid, so hunt for the focus, do not expect organisms.

A large painless undermined ulcer in a patient from Ghana or coastal Victoria. Buruli ulcer — and the examiner wants mycolactone.

Nodules confined to the gray shading of a new tattoo. Rapidly growing mycobacterium, classically M. chelonae — and the examiner wants “susceptibility testing is required.”

Fungal infections

Superficial, subcutaneous, systemic, opportunistic

Fungal infection: organize by depth, not by name

There are hundreds of fungi that touch skin and only four compartments they can occupy. Depth predicts the specimen, the specimen predicts what the laboratory can do, and what the laboratory can do predicts whether you get a species name, a susceptibility result, or nothing at all. Work the compartment first and the organism list shrinks to something manageable.

CompartmentRepresentative organismsWhat the laboratory needsWhat the laboratory can give you
Superficial
stratum corneum, hair, nail		 Dermatophytes (Trichophyton, Microsporum, Epidermophyton), Malassezia, Candida Scale, plucked hairs with roots, nail clipping plus subungual debris. Not a surface swab. Direct microscopy in minutes; culture with a species name in 1–4 weeks; PAS on nail clippings via histopathology
Subcutaneous
implantation through the dermis		 Sporothrix, dematiaceous fungi of chromoblastomycosis, agents of eumycetoma Deep tissue — punch to subcutis or excisional wedge — split fresh for culture and formalin for histology; discharged grains if present Culture often definitive; histology shows the tissue form (asteroid body, muriform body, grain)
Systemic / disseminated
skin as a window on deep disease		 Histoplasma, Blastomyces, Coccidioides, Cryptococcus, Talaromyces marneffei Tissue for histology and culture, plus blood; antigen and serologic testing ordered in parallel Antigen assays in hours to days; culture in days to weeks — and a biosafety problem if you do not warn them
Opportunistic angioinvasive molds
vessel-centered, in the immunocompromised		 Aspergillus, Fusarium, Mucorales (Rhizopus, Mucor, Lichtheimia) Urgent deep biopsy for both histology and culture; blood cultures; serum biomarkers Histology is frequently the fastest and sometimes the only positive test; culture can be falsely negative
Why it matters

Every superficial fungal misadventure you will see as a resident traces back to one of three things: the wrong specimen was sent, the laboratory was not told what you suspected, or systemic therapy was started on a clinical guess that turned out to be psoriasis. The microbiology is not an afterthought to the dermatology here — it is the dermatology.

How to actually talk to the mycology bench

Mycology is one of the few laboratory sections where a free-text comment on the requisition genuinely changes the result. The bench chooses media, incubation temperature, hold time, and containment level based on what you tell them. Say the following out loud on the form.

  • Name the differential, not just “fungal culture.” “Rule out dermatophyte” and “rule out non-dermatophyte mold” get different plates.
  • Ask about cycloheximide. Standard dermatophyte media contain cycloheximide to suppress contaminants — but it also inhibits many of the opportunistic molds, Cryptococcus, and several non-albicans yeasts. If you suspect a non-dermatophyte mold in a nail, or an opportunist in an immunocompromised host, request a parallel plate without cycloheximide.
  • Ask about lipid supplementation if you want Malassezia to grow (see the advanced pearl below).
  • Ask about incubation temperature when your differential includes organisms with unusual optima — the sporotrichoid differential is the classic example.
  • Ask about hold time before you accept a negative. Fungal plates are typically held for weeks; a five-day negative is not a negative.
  • Warn them about dimorphic fungi and Mucorales. The mold phases of Coccidioides, Histoplasma, and Blastomyces are serious laboratory-acquired infection hazards and are handled at enhanced containment. An unannounced plate opened on the open bench is a real exposure event.

Local practice varies. Which media are stocked, whether molecular identification is in-house or referred, and turnaround times are institution-dependent — find out what your own laboratory actually offers before you promise a patient a species name.

Superficial I: the dermatophytes

Dermatophytes are keratinophilic molds that digest keratin with secreted proteases and, in the ordinary case, never breach the basement membrane. Three genera matter clinically, and the practical differences between them are about which keratinized tissue they can invade and where they came from.

Trichophyton

Skin, hair, and nail

The genus that does everything. T. rubrum is the dominant cause of tinea pedis, cruris, corporis, and onychomycosis in much of the world; T. tonsurans dominates tinea capitis across much of North America; T. verrucosum comes off cattle and produces spectacular inflammation.

Invades hairYes
Invades nailYes
Hair patternEndothrix or ectothrix by species

Microsporum

Skin and hair

M. canis is the classic zoophilic species from kittens and puppies. Clinically the important property is that Microsporum species produce a small-spore ectothrix and several of them fluoresce under a Wood lamp, which Trichophyton tonsurans does not.

Invades hairYes
Invades nailRarely
Wood lampOften blue-green

Epidermophyton

Skin and nail only

A single clinically relevant species, E. floccosum, classically causing tinea cruris and tinea pedis. The examinable point is negative: it does not invade hair. If the laboratory reports Epidermophyton from a scalp specimen, be suspicious of the sample or the identification.

Invades hairNo
Invades nailYes
Typical sitesGroin, feet, trunk
Advanced pearl

Dermatophyte taxonomy has been substantially revised on molecular grounds, and your laboratory report may not use the names in your textbook. Several former Microsporum species have moved to Nannizzia (the geophilic organism you learned as M. gypseum is now usually reported as Nannizzia gypsea), and the T. mentagrophytes group has been resolved into several species including T. interdigitale. When a report returns an unfamiliar genus, assume a nomenclature change before you assume a contaminant — and call the laboratory to confirm.

Anthropophilic

Human reservoir

Well adapted to the human host, so they provoke little inflammation and become chronic and relapsing. T. rubrum, T. tonsurans, E. floccosum. These are the quietly scaly, minimally erythematous, years-long infections — and the ones that spread within households, gyms, and wrestling teams.

InflammationLow
CourseChronic, relapsing

Zoophilic

Animal reservoir

Poorly adapted to humans, so the host mounts a vigorous inflammatory response: brightly erythematous, pustular, sometimes vesicular lesions, and in the scalp a far higher likelihood of kerion. M. canis (cats and dogs), T. verrucosum (cattle), T. benhamiae (guinea pigs), T. mentagrophytes group (rodents, rabbits).

InflammationHigh
History that cracks itA new kitten

Geophilic

Soil reservoir

Acquired from soil, typically in gardeners, farm workers, and children playing in dirt. Also inflammatory, because again the organism is not host-adapted. Nannizzia gypsea is the representative.

InflammationModerate to high
ExposureSoil contact
Clinical pattern

Inflammation is an ecological readout, not a severity score. A furiously inflamed, pustular annular plaque in a child who just got a kitten is telling you the organism is zoophilic — probably M. canis — and that the animal needs veterinary assessment or the child will be reinfected. A bland, faintly scaly, slowly enlarging plaque in an adult with chronic tinea pedis is telling you it is anthropophilic, probably T. rubrum, and that the feet and nails are the reservoir you have to treat.

Tinea corporis is the annular plaque with an active, scaly, advancing edge and relative central clearing — the fungus is at the periphery, which is where you must scrape. Scraping the pale center is one of the commonest reasons for a falsely negative potassium hydroxide (KOH) preparation.

Tinea cruris characteristically spares the scrotum, which is a genuinely useful discriminator from candidal intertrigo and from inverse psoriasis. It is usually an autoinoculation from the patient’s own feet, so examine the feet and nails in every case; treating the groin alone guarantees recurrence.

What to do next

Scrape the leading edge with a No. 15 blade or a glass slide edge onto a second slide, apply KOH, and read it yourself. Send a portion for culture in a paper envelope or sterile container — not in a sealed plastic bag, which traps moisture and encourages contaminant overgrowth.

Three patterns, and they behave differently.

  • Interdigital — maceration and fissuring in the lateral web spaces. Frequently a mixed dermatophyte-plus-bacterial process; when it becomes malodorous, macerated, and eroded (“dermatophytosis complex”), Gram-negative and coryneform overgrowth is contributing and an antifungal alone will disappoint.
  • Moccasin — diffuse fine silvery scale over the sole and lateral foot in a sharply demarcated distribution, typically T. rubrum, typically chronic, and typically accompanied by onychomycosis. This is the phenotype that fails topical therapy.
  • Vesiculobullous — acute tense vesicles on the instep, often T. interdigitale, and the one most often mistaken for dyshidrotic eczema. Unroof a vesicle and take the blister roof for KOH; that is the highest-yield specimen.
Student pearl

The interdigital web space is also the portal of entry for bacterial cellulitis of the leg. Treating tinea pedis in a patient with recurrent cellulitis is not cosmetic — it is source control.

Tinea manuum is diffuse dryness and fine scale accentuated in the palmar creases, usually unilateral. The classic pattern is “two feet, one hand”: bilateral tinea pedis with unilateral palmar involvement, presumably from the hand the patient uses to pick and scratch at the feet.

The trap is that unilateral palmar scale reads as chronic hand eczema or irritant dermatitis, gets a potent topical corticosteroid, and improves briefly before worsening. Any asymmetric “hand eczema” deserves a KOH before it deserves a steroid — and a look at the feet and toenails in the same visit.

Tinea faciei loses the tidy annular morphology that makes tinea corporis easy. It is often photodistributed-looking, poorly annular, and pruritic or burning, so it collects diagnoses of lupus, polymorphous light eruption, seborrheic dermatitis, and rosacea — each of which attracts a topical corticosteroid or calcineurin inhibitor that makes it worse.

Tinea barbae involves terminal hair in the beard area, usually zoophilic and usually in agricultural workers. Because the hair follicle is invaded, it behaves like tinea capitis: it requires systemic therapy. The same is true of Majocchi granuloma, follicular dermatophyte invasion into the dermis typically triggered by shaving over an infected area or by topical steroid use — perifollicular papulonodules that will not clear with a topical agent because the organism is below the epidermis.

Tinea incognito is dermatophytosis modified by a topical corticosteroid or calcineurin inhibitor. The steroid suppresses the inflammatory response that generates the two features you rely on: the scale disappears and the raised annular edge flattens. What is left is an ill-defined, larger, less symptomatic patch that no longer looks fungal at all — and, because the local immune response is suppressed, the fungal burden is often higher and follicular invasion more likely.

Because the scale is gone, the KOH yield falls. If you are suspicious, stop the steroid, let the surface recover for a week or two, and re-scrape; or biopsy and ask for a periodic acid–Schiff (PAS) stain.

Pitfall

Combination corticosteroid–antifungal products are the industrial-scale generator of tinea incognito. The potency of the steroid component often outruns the antifungal, and in many regions these are available over the counter. Ask specifically what the patient has been putting on the lesion, including anything given by a relative or bought abroad — patients rarely volunteer it because they do not consider a cream a medication.

What actually happens to your scale specimen

Four distinct tests come off one specimen, and they answer different questions. Knowing which one you need is most of the skill.

Direct microscopy

Minutes, at the bedside

Potassium hydroxide (KOH) at 10–20 percent dissolves keratinocytes and leaves fungal elements. Adding dimethyl sulfoxide speeds clearing without heating. Chlorazol black E stains chitin and makes hyphae far easier to see against artifact; calcofluor white is more sensitive still but needs a fluorescence microscope, so it is a laboratory test rather than a clinic one.

AnswersIs there fungus?
Does not answerWhich fungus
Pitfall

Mosaic artifact — the refractile outlines of intercellular lipid between keratinocytes — is the classic false positive. True hyphae cross cell borders, branch, and are septate; mosaic artifact follows the cell outlines.

Culture

One to four weeks

Sabouraud dextrose agar with an antibacterial (chloramphenicol or gentamicin) and cycloheximide to suppress saprophytic molds. Dermatophyte test medium (DTM) is a screening variant containing phenol red: alkaline metabolites from a dermatophyte turn it red. Incubation is at around room temperature to 30 °C, not at 35–37 °C.

Incubation25–30 °C
Hold before calling negativeup to ~4 weeks
Pitfall

DTM is a screen, not an identification. Some contaminants also turn the indicator red with prolonged incubation, and the color change alone should never be reported as “dermatophyte.” Confirm with morphology or a molecular method.

Histopathology with PAS

Days

Periodic acid–Schiff or Gomori methenamine silver on formalin-fixed tissue or on a nail clipping. Highly sensitive for the presence of fungus in keratin, and it is the test that rescues you when clinical suspicion is high and KOH and culture are both negative. It gives no species and no viability information.

Best useNail clippings; steroid-modified tinea

Molecular testing

Hours to days, where available

Polymerase chain reaction (PCR)–based nucleic acid amplification tests (NAATs) detect dermatophyte DNA directly from nail or scale, and internal transcribed spacer (ITS) region sequencing is the reference method for identifying an isolate that morphology cannot resolve. Matrix-assisted laser desorption/ionization time-of-flight mass spectrometry (MALDI-TOF MS) identifies many isolates rapidly once they have grown.

CaveatDetects nonviable organism
CaveatPanels miss off-target species

Availability of PCR and sequencing for superficial mycoses varies widely by country and institution; in many centers it remains a send-out with a cost implication. Confirm what your laboratory offers rather than assuming.

Advanced pearl

Trichophyton indotineae is the reason antifungal susceptibility testing has entered dermatology. Emerging from South Asia and now reported across Europe and North America, it causes extensive, highly inflammatory, often steroid-modified tinea corporis and cruris that fails standard terbinafine courses. The mechanism is point mutation in the gene encoding squalene epoxidase, terbinafine’s target. It was historically reported within the T. mentagrophytes / T. interdigitale complex, so a routine morphologic or MALDI-TOF identification may not flag it — ITS sequencing is the reference approach. Practically: in a patient with extensive tinea failing an adequate terbinafine course, especially with relevant travel or contact history, call the laboratory, ask whether species-level molecular identification and antifungal susceptibility testing are available, and say why. There is a reference broth microdilution method for filamentous fungi, but there are no broadly accepted clinical breakpoints for dermatophytes, so results are interpreted comparatively rather than as a categorical “susceptible” or “resistant.”

Superficial II: tinea capitis, a laboratory and therapeutic problem

Tinea capitis is worth its own section because it is the one superficial dermatophytosis where the microbiology dictates the drug, the Wood lamp is regionally misleading, and topical therapy is categorically inadequate.

Ectothrix

Arthroconidia outside the hair shaft

The organism forms a sheath of arthroconidia on the outside of the shaft and destroys the cuticle. Hairs break a few millimeters above the scalp, so you see gray, dull, sheathed, broken hairs and a scaly patch — the classic “gray patch.”

Typical generaMicrosporum, some Trichophyton
Wood lampOften blue-green with Microsporum
CuticleDestroyed

Endothrix

Arthroconidia inside the hair shaft

The organism fills the shaft while leaving the cuticle intact. The weakened hair snaps flush with the scalp surface, leaving the black dot pattern — comedo-like dark points within a patch of alopecia. Diffuse scale with minimal obvious hair loss is an equally common and easily missed presentation.

Typical speciesT. tonsurans, T. violaceum
Wood lampNon-fluorescent
CuticleIntact
Pitfall

The Wood lamp is a screening tool with a strong geographic dependency. Across much of North America the predominant organism is Trichophyton tonsurans, an endothrix that does not fluoresce — so a dark scalp under the lamp excludes essentially nothing. Fluorescence, when present, is most often Microsporum. Never use a negative Wood lamp to withhold mycologic sampling in a child with a scaly, patchy scalp and occipital or posterior cervical lymphadenopathy.

Sampling and treating the scalp

Get the right specimen

Hair root, not hair shaft

The organism lives in and on the proximal hair and in the adherent scale, so pluck hairs with a forceps to obtain roots and scrape the scale. Cut hairs are close to useless. Brushing the scalp with a sterile toothbrush or a cytology brush and pressing it into the agar is well established in children and samples a wide area painlessly.

BestPlucked hairs + scale
Acceptable in childrenBrush or moistened swab of scalp
InadequateCut hair

The scalp is the one site where a firmly rubbed moistened swab performs reasonably for culture — unlike glabrous skin and nail, where a surface swab remains the wrong specimen.

Why topicals cannot work

Pharmacology, not compliance

The fungus is inside or wrapped around the hair shaft within the follicle. A topical agent cannot reach that compartment in fungicidal concentration, so tinea capitis always requires systemic therapy — as do tinea barbae and Majocchi granuloma, for the same anatomic reason.

Griseofulvin and terbinafine are the mainstays. Species matters: terbinafine generally performs well against Trichophyton endothrix disease, while griseofulvin has traditionally been favored for Microsporum. That is precisely why obtaining a culture, even though you start therapy empirically, changes management if the patient is not responding.

What to do next

Add an adjunctive sporicidal shampoo (selenium sulfide or ketoconazole) for the patient and consider it for household contacts. It reduces shedding of viable spores and transmission — it does not treat the infection. Screen household members and, in zoophilic disease, arrange veterinary assessment of the animal.

Kerion

Host response, not superinfection

A boggy, exquisitely tender, pustular inflammatory mass with overlying alopecia and regional lymphadenopathy. It is a vigorous cell-mediated response to the dermatophyte — most often a zoophilic or geophilic species — not a bacterial abscess, even though pus expresses from the follicular openings.

The consequence of getting this wrong is permanent: kerion managed as an abscess with incision and drainage and antibacterials alone delays antifungal therapy and increases the risk of scarring, permanent alopecia.

Student pearl

Bacterial cultures from a kerion frequently grow skin flora or Staphylococcus aureus. That does not make it a bacterial disease. Send a fungal specimen at the same time, and treat the fungus.

Adjunctive systemic corticosteroids are used by some clinicians to limit inflammation; the evidence base is limited and practice varies. The systemic antifungal is not optional.

The id reaction (dermatophytid)

Sterile, and not a drug allergy

A distant, symmetric, often papular or vesicular eruption — commonly on the trunk, face, or hands — appearing days to weeks after a kerion or after starting systemic therapy, as antigen release peaks. It is an immunologic reaction: KOH and culture from the id lesions are negative, because there is no organism there.

The trap is that it is timed exactly like a drug eruption, so the antifungal gets stopped and the primary infection is undertreated.

What to do next

Continue the antifungal. Treat the id eruption symptomatically with emollients, a topical corticosteroid, and an antihistamine if pruritus warrants. Reserve stopping the drug for a genuine hypersensitivity picture — mucosal involvement, systemic features, eosinophilia, or transaminase derangement.

Superficial III: onychomycosis and the case for confirming before you treat

Roughly half of dystrophic nails referred as “fungal nails” are not fungal. Since systemic therapy runs for months, carries drug interactions — terbinafine and itraconazole are both CYP substrates and inhibitors, and itraconazole is avoided in ventricular dysfunction — and in most services requires baseline and interval liver enzyme monitoring, mycologic confirmation before starting is standard practice in most guidelines and most institutions.

Distal and lateral subungual

The common one

Invasion via the hyponychium and lateral nail folds, producing subungual hyperkeratosis, onycholysis, and yellow-brown discoloration advancing proximally. Usually T. rubrum, usually preceded by moccasin tinea pedis.

SampleProximal-most clipping + subungual debris

Superficial white

On the plate surface

Chalky white friable patches on the dorsal surface of the nail plate, classically T. interdigitale. Because the organism sits superficially, it is the one subtype where curettage of the plate surface both samples and debulks.

SampleScrape the plate surface

Proximal subungual

The one that means something

Invasion through the proximal nail fold and the ventral plate, producing whiteness emerging from under the cuticle. Uncommon, and long recognized as a marker that should prompt consideration of immunosuppression, including HIV infection. Also seen with peripheral vascular disease and after trauma.

SampleProximal plate; drilling or biopsy may be needed
ThenConsider HIV testing
TestWhat it tells youRelative sensitivitySpecies?Practical notes
KOH direct microscopy Fungal elements present or absent Moderate; operator-dependent no Immediate and cheap. Nail keratin is thick, so allow prolonged clearing or use a keratolytic-enhanced preparation
PAS on nail clipping Fungal elements within the nail plate Highest of the routine tests no Clippings go to histopathology in formalin. Cannot distinguish viable from dead organism, and cannot distinguish dermatophyte from mold
Fungal culture A viable, named organism Lowest — frequently negative despite true infection yes The only routine test that names the species and enables susceptibility testing. Weeks to result. Request media without cycloheximide if a non-dermatophyte mold is suspected
PCR / NAAT Dermatophyte DNA, often to species High often Fast. Detects nonviable organism, so it can stay positive after cure; panel content limits which molds are detectable. Availability varies by institution
Student pearl

A common and effective strategy is to pair a highly sensitive test with a specific one: PAS on a clipping to establish that fungus is present, plus culture or PCR to name it. If both are negative in a nail you are convinced is fungal, resample — poor sampling is a far commoner explanation than a false negative. Clip as far proximally as the nail allows and, crucially, curette the subungual debris under the advancing edge, where the viable organism actually is. A distal clipping alone is mostly dead keratin.

Do not confuse

Onychomycosis versus every other cause of nail dystrophy. Nail psoriasis gives pitting, oil-drop or salmon-patch discoloration, and onycholysis, and frequently coexists with true onychomycosis, so a positive mycology does not exclude it. Nail lichen planus gives longitudinal ridging, plate thinning, and pterygium — and is a scarring process where delay costs the nail permanently. Trauma and onychogryphosis in an older patient with ill-fitting shoes look convincingly fungal and are not. And a single persistently dystrophic or pigmented nail that will not respond, particularly with pigment extending onto the proximal nail fold, must raise subungual melanoma — that nail needs a biopsy of the matrix, not another course of terbinafine.

Superficial IV: Malassezia, a commensal that behaves like a pathogen

Malassezia yeasts are normal residents of sebaceous skin. Disease reflects overgrowth and host response rather than acquisition of a new organism — which explains both the recurrence rate and why you almost never culture it.

Pityriasis versicolor

Stratum corneum overgrowth

Finely scaly, sharply marginated macules and thin plaques on sebaceous sites — upper trunk, shoulders, neck, and in some patients the face. They are hypopigmented in some patients and hyperpigmented in others, often in the same person simultaneously; the pigment change is attributed to dicarboxylic acids produced by the organism inhibiting tyrosinase, plus altered melanosome transfer. Stretching the skin brings out fine scale that is not obvious at rest.

KOHShort hyphae + clustered yeasts
Classic phrase“Spaghetti and meatballs”
CultureNot routinely indicated

Malassezia folliculitis

Follicular, and mistaken for acne

Monomorphic follicular papules and pustules on the upper back, chest, and shoulders, and characteristically itchy — acne is not. The discriminating feature is the absence of comedones and the uniformity of the lesions.

It is precipitated by occlusion, heat and humidity, topical or systemic corticosteroids, immunosuppression, and notably by broad-spectrum antibacterial therapy. A patient whose “acne” got worse on a tetracycline is telling you the diagnosis.

PruritusPresent
ComedonesAbsent

Seborrheic dermatitis

Driver, not simple infection

Malassezia lipases liberate free fatty acids from sebum, and the host inflammatory response to those metabolites — not tissue invasion — produces the erythema and greasy scale. The consistent response to antifungal agents supports the causal role, but this is a dysbiosis-plus-host-reactivity disease, which is why it relapses.

Explosive, severe, or refractory seborrheic dermatitis is a recognized flag for advanced HIV infection and is also associated with Parkinson disease and other neurologic conditions. It is worth a second thought rather than a third shampoo.

Advanced pearl

Malassezia is lipid-dependent and will not grow on routine fungal media. Apart from M. pachydermatis, these yeasts cannot synthesize their own long-chain fatty acids and require exogenous lipid. If you genuinely need a culture, the laboratory must overlay Sabouraud agar with sterile olive oil or use a lipid-enriched medium such as modified Dixon or Leeming–Notman agar — and they will only do that if you ask. Submitting “fungal culture” on a versicolor specimen reliably returns “no growth,” which is a laboratory artifact and not a negative result. Two further consequences worth knowing: M. pachydermatis, the non-lipid-dependent exception, is the species associated with dogs and has been implicated in neonatal intensive care outbreaks traced to caregiver hand carriage; and because diagnosis of versicolor is clinical and confirmed by KOH, culture is simply not part of routine practice.

Superficial V: Candida in the skin

Candidal skin disease is a disease of moisture, occlusion, and barrier failure. The organism is usually endogenous, so the useful clinical question is rarely “where did it come from” and almost always “what made this site hospitable.”

Intertrigo

Satellite lesions are the tell

Beefy-red, macerated, sometimes eroded plaques in inframammary, axillary, abdominal, and inguinal folds, with satellite papules and pustules beyond the border. Contrast with tinea, which advances as a scaly annular edge with central clearing and no satellites.

BorderSatellites, no central clearing
DriversObesity, diabetes, occlusion, incontinence

Diaper dermatitis

Creases in or creases out

Candidal diaper dermatitis involves the inguinal creases, with satellite pustules; irritant diaper dermatitis characteristically spares them because the convex surfaces take the contact. That single observation directs therapy.

CandidalCreases involved
IrritantCreases spared

Angular cheilitis

Anatomy plus organism

Fissured, macerated erythema at the oral commissures where saliva pools. Candida, Staphylococcus aureus, or both. The driver is usually mechanical — loss of vertical dimension, ill-fitting dentures, drooling, lip licking — so antimicrobial therapy without addressing the anatomy relapses. Also consider iron and B-vitamin deficiency in the recalcitrant case.

Chronic paronychia

Not primarily an infection

Loss of the cuticle from wet work or manipulation opens the proximal nail fold to irritants and allergens; the result is a chronic eczematous barrier disease that is secondarily colonized by Candida. Culturing yeast from the fold does not make yeast the cause.

What to do next

Manage it as dermatitis: strict avoidance of wet work and manipulation, barrier protection, and a topical anti-inflammatory — which generally outperforms an antifungal used alone. Antifungal therapy is adjunctive, not the backbone.

Erosio interdigitalis blastomycetica

The web space of the hand

A macerated, white, fissured erosion with a collarette in a finger web space — classically the third — in wet workers, people with diabetes, and those with occupational hand immersion. The equivalent finding in the toe web spaces is far more often dermatophyte, so the anatomy points you at the organism.

Chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis

A phenotype, not a diagnosis

Persistent or recurrent candidal infection of skin, nails, and mucosa should trigger an immunologic evaluation, because it is a recognized manifestation of defects in the interleukin-17 axis — including STAT1 gain-of-function, AIRE mutations (autoimmune polyendocrinopathy–candidiasis–ectodermal dystrophy), and CARD9 deficiency. CARD9 deficiency additionally predisposes to deep dermatophytosis and to central nervous system fungal disease, which is a link a dermatologist is uniquely placed to make.

Also screen forAssociated endocrinopathy
Why it matters

This is where species-level identification and susceptibility testing genuinely exist, unlike for dermatophytes. Yeasts are identified rapidly by MALDI-TOF MS or chromogenic agar, and CLSI has established interpretive breakpoints for common Candida species against azoles and echinocandins. Much of the susceptibility is predictable from the name alone: Nakaseomyces glabratus (formerly C. glabrata) shows reduced azole susceptibility, Pichia kudriavzevii (formerly C. krusei) is intrinsically fluconazole-resistant, and C. parapsilosis tends to have higher echinocandin minimum inhibitory concentrations. Separately, Candida auris is a skin-colonizing, environmentally persistent, frequently multidrug-resistant organism that some legacy identification platforms misidentify — if a laboratory flags it, that is an infection-prevention event, not just a result.

Subcutaneous (implantation) mycoses: the gardening group

These are traumatic inoculation diseases of soil and plant fungi through the dermis into subcutaneous tissue. They are chronic, they are frequently in a returning traveler or an agricultural worker, and every one of them needs tissue — a surface swab of a discharging sinus answers nothing.

Sporotrichosis

Sporothrix species complex

Classically inoculated by rose thorns, sphagnum moss, hay, or timber. The signature is the lymphocutaneous pattern: a papulonodule or ulcer at the inoculation site with a chain of subcutaneous nodules ascending the limb along lymphatics.

The exposure history that most residents miss is zoonotic transmission from cats. In parts of South America, particularly Brazil, S. brasiliensis is transmitted by scratch, bite, or contact with an infected cat, is highly transmissible, and behaves more aggressively than the classic plant-associated disease. Ask about cats, not only about roses.

SpecimenDeep tissue or aspirate, not a swab
CultureGrows readily
HistologyOften paucifungal
Student pearl

The asteroid body — a central yeast surrounded by radiating eosinophilic Splendore–Hoeppli material — is the classic histologic finding, but it is insensitive. Organisms are typically sparse in tissue, so a negative special stain does not exclude sporotrichosis. Culture is the higher-yield test here, which is the reverse of the situation with the angioinvasive molds.

Chromoblastomycosis

Dematiaceous (melanized) fungi

Slowly expanding verrucous, cauliflower-like plaques, usually on a lower limb, over years. Causative organisms include Fonsecaea, Cladophialophora carrionii, Phialophora verrucosa, and Rhinocladiella species — all pigmented by cell-wall melanin.

The tissue form is diagnostic and species-independent: muriform (sclerotic, medlar) bodies — thick-walled, brown, rounded cells that septate in more than one plane, described as “copper pennies.” They are visible on KOH of surface scrapings as well as on histology.

Where to scrapeThe black dots on the surface
Names the speciesCulture only
Pitfall

Histology shows florid pseudoepitheliomatous hyperplasia and can be reported as well-differentiated squamous cell carcinoma if the pathologist is not told what you suspect. Write the differential on the requisition and ask explicitly for fungal stains.

Mycetoma

Tumefaction, sinuses, grains

A chronic, usually painless, grossly deforming infection of subcutaneous tissue that extends to fascia and bone. The diagnostic triad is tumefaction, draining sinus tracts, and grains — macroscopic aggregates of organism discharged in the exudate. The foot is the classic site (“Madura foot”), reflecting barefoot inoculation. WHO recognizes mycetoma as a neglected tropical disease.

Grain color is a genuine clue: black grains are fungal (typically Madurella), pale or white grains may be either, and red grains point to Actinomadura pelletieri, a bacterium.

SpecimenDeep tissue plus the grains themselves
RequestFungal AND bacterial culture
Do not confuse

Eumycetoma versus actinomycetoma — identical clinically, opposite therapeutically. Eumycetoma is caused by true fungi (Madurella mycetomatis, Falciformispora, Scedosporium and relatives) and requires prolonged antifungal therapy, usually an azole, often with surgery, and cure rates are modest. Actinomycetoma is caused by aerobic actinomycete bacteria (Nocardia brasiliensis, Actinomadura madurae, A. pelletieri, Streptomyces somaliensis) and is treated with antibacterials — classically a sulfonamide-based regimen, sometimes with an aminoglycoside — with substantially better cure rates. Antifungals do nothing for actinomycetoma and antibacterials do nothing for eumycetoma, so the distinction is the whole management decision. Two things resolve it fast: crush a grain and examine it — delicate filaments around one micrometer across indicate an actinomycete, while broad septate hyphae several micrometers across indicate a fungus — and request a modified acid-fast stain, since Nocardia is partially acid-fast. Tell the laboratory you suspect mycetoma and send grains, or the granule gets discarded as debris and the actinomycete plate is never set up.

Systemic and endemic mycoses with cutaneous manifestations

For this group, geography and exposure are the diagnostic test you perform first. A skin lesion is usually a marker of disseminated disease, so finding it obliges you to look for the deep focus — and to warn the laboratory before the plate is opened.

Geography and exposure. Classically the Ohio and Mississippi River valleys, but also widely distributed in Central and South America, parts of Africa, and Southeast Asia. Exposure to bird or bat guano — caves, chicken coops, demolition and renovation of old buildings.

Skin findings. Cutaneous lesions in progressive disseminated histoplasmosis are protean: papules, molluscum-like umbilicated papules, ulcers, and panniculitis-like nodules, most often in advanced HIV infection or other profound T-cell deficiency. Oral or oropharyngeal ulceration is a classic and frequently the presenting sign. Separately, erythema nodosum and erythema multiforme can occur as reactive phenomena in acute pulmonary infection in immunocompetent hosts — sterile, and a sign of good immunity.

Laboratory. Urine and serum Histoplasma antigen testing is the fast route in disseminated disease. Histology shows small intracellular yeasts within macrophages, best seen on silver stain. Culture is definitive but slow. The African variant, H. capsulatum var. duboisii, is notable for prominent skin and bone involvement and larger yeasts.

Geography and exposure. Overlaps Histoplasma in the Ohio and Mississippi valleys and extends to the Great Lakes and St. Lawrence River basins. Moist soil and decaying wood; outdoor and waterway exposure.

Skin findings. The characteristic lesion is a verrucous, crusted plaque with a heaped, irregular border and pustules at the advancing edge, often with central atrophic scarring. Subcutaneous nodules also occur. Cutaneous disease usually reflects dissemination from a pulmonary focus.

Pitfall

The verrucous plaque of blastomycosis is a near-perfect mimic of well-differentiated squamous cell carcinoma, and the histology compounds the error, because it shows florid pseudoepitheliomatous hyperplasia. Pathologists have read these as SCC and patients have gone to wide excision for an infection. Put “rule out deep fungal infection” on the requisition and ask for PAS and silver stains — the broad-based budding yeast with a thick, refractile double-contoured wall is unmistakable once it is looked for.

Geography and exposure. Arid and semi-arid regions of the southwestern United States, notably central and southern California and Arizona, plus northern Mexico and foci in Central and South America. Dust exposure, excavation, dust storms, and archaeological or agricultural fieldwork.

Two very different cutaneous stories.

  • Reactive — erythema nodosum, often with arthralgia and erythema multiforme, in the setting of acute pulmonary infection. This is a hypersensitivity phenomenon, the lesions are sterile, and it is a marker of a vigorous cell-mediated response and a good prognosis. Do not biopsy it looking for organism, and do not read it as dissemination.
  • Disseminated — true cutaneous dissemination presents as verrucous papulonodules and plaques, classically involving the face and nasolabial fold. This is the finding that mandates evaluation for meningeal and skeletal disease.

Risk of dissemination is described as higher in people of African and Filipino ancestry, in pregnancy (particularly later in gestation and the postpartum period), and in those with cellular immunosuppression or poorly controlled diabetes. Serology — immunodiffusion and complement fixation, with the complement fixation titer followed serially — is central to diagnosis and to monitoring disseminated disease.

Why it matters

Coccidioides arthroconidia are among the most infectious agents encountered in a clinical laboratory, and mold-phase cultures are handled under enhanced containment. If your differential includes coccidioidomycosis, that belongs on the requisition. This is a genuine occupational safety issue for the technologists, not paperwork.

Organism and host. Cryptococcus neoformans in advanced HIV infection and other profound T-cell deficiency; C. gattii notably in the Pacific Northwest and Vancouver Island, and capable of causing disease in apparently immunocompetent hosts. Environmental exposure to soil and avian droppings.

Skin findings. The classic is umbilicated, dome-shaped, molluscum-like papules, most often on the head and neck, in a patient with advanced HIV. Ulcers, cellulitis-like plaques, and nodules also occur. Cutaneous cryptococcosis is almost always a marker of disseminated disease, and the skin lesion frequently precedes recognition of meningitis.

Laboratory. Cryptococcal antigen testing on serum by lateral flow assay is rapid and highly sensitive. Histology shows encapsulated yeast; mucicarmine stains the capsule and Fontana–Masson stains cell-wall melanin, which is useful in capsule-deficient strains. India ink on cerebrospinal fluid is the traditional direct preparation. Note that beta-D-glucan is not a useful marker here.

What to do next

Biopsy for histology and culture, send serum cryptococcal antigen, and escalate for lumbar puncture and infectious diseases involvement the same day. The dermatologic finding is a screening test for meningitis.

Geography and exposure. Talaromyces marneffei (formerly Penicillium marneffei) is endemic in Southeast Asia and southern China — Thailand, Vietnam, southern China, and northeastern India — where it is associated with bamboo rats and the rainy season. It is an AIDS-defining opportunistic infection in that region and should be considered in any traveler or migrant from it.

Skin findings. Umbilicated, molluscum-like papules concentrated on the face, upper trunk, and arms, with fever, weight loss, anemia, and hepatosplenomegaly. Clinically it is very hard to separate from disseminated cryptococcosis or histoplasmosis on morphology alone.

Student pearl

Two laboratory features settle it. On Sabouraud agar at around 25 °C the mold phase produces a striking diffusible red pigment into the medium — a genuinely useful visual clue. And in tissue the yeast-like cells divide by transverse septation (fission) rather than by budding, distinguishing them from the narrow-based budding of Histoplasma.

Do not confuse

Molluscum contagiosum versus disseminated fungal infection in advanced HIV. Umbilicated dome-shaped papules on the face of a patient with a low CD4 count are the classic presentation of disseminated cryptococcosis, and also of disseminated histoplasmosis and talaromycosis — all of which look like molluscum and none of which are. The error is consequential in both directions: curetting them as molluscum treats nothing and misses a life-threatening systemic infection, most often meningitis. In an immunocompromised patient, umbilicated papules get a biopsy sent for histology with fungal stains and a separate portion sent fresh for culture, plus serum cryptococcal antigen — not a curette. Conversely, giant or rapidly proliferating true molluscum is itself a marker of advanced immunosuppression and warrants the same investigation of immune status.

Opportunistic angioinvasive molds: the dermatologic emergency

These are environmental molds that become pathogens only when host defenses fail — principally neutropenia, hematologic malignancy, transplantation, high-dose corticosteroids, poorly controlled diabetes with ketoacidosis, and burns. They invade blood vessels, so the skin lesion is an infarct. Time to diagnosis determines outcome.

AspergillusFusariumMucorales
Typical host Prolonged neutropenia, transplant, high-dose corticosteroid Prolonged profound neutropenia, hematologic malignancy Diabetic ketoacidosis, neutropenia, iron overload or deferoxamine therapy, trauma and burns
Cutaneous presentation Primary lesions at catheter, adhesive dressing, or occlusion sites; necrotic ulcer with eschar. Secondary lesions from dissemination Disseminated, painful nodules and targetoid lesions with central necrosis, appearing in crops on the extremities and trunk Rapidly enlarging necrotic ulcer with a black eschar; rhino-orbito-cerebral disease with palatal or nasal turbinate necrosis
Blood cultures rarely positive frequently positive — it sporulates in vivo rarely positive
Histology Septate hyphae, acute-angle branching, uniform width Septate hyphae, morphologically indistinguishable from Aspergillus in tissue Broad, ribbon-like, pauci-septate hyphae with wide-angle branching
Serum biomarkers Galactomannan and beta-D-glucan may support Beta-D-glucan may be positive; galactomannan can cross-react Both negative — Mucorales lack these cell-wall targets
Dermatologic clue Look under the dressings and at line sites Look at the feet and nails — onychomycosis or interdigital tinea is a common portal of entry Look in the nose and mouth; examine the palate
Pitfall

Culture is frequently falsely negative in mucormycosis, and the commonest reason is a processing step: Mucorales hyphae are coenocytic, essentially one continuous cell, so routine grinding or homogenizing of tissue destroys the organism before it is ever plated. When you suspect mucormycosis, write it on the requisition and ask the laboratory to mince rather than homogenize the specimen. Second reason for a false negative: sending the whole biopsy in formalin. Split the specimen — part in formalin for histology, part fresh in a sterile container with a small volume of sterile saline for culture. Third: histology and culture answer different questions here, and histology is often the faster and only positive result, so always send both. Where available, panfungal PCR on tissue can identify the organism when culture fails, with better performance on fresh tissue and when organisms are visible histologically; availability varies by center.

What to do next

A necrotic eschar on a neutropenic or ketoacidotic patient is an emergency, not a consult for tomorrow. The pathway is: deep incisional or punch biopsy through to subcutaneous tissue the same day — superficial samples miss the angioinvasive front; split the specimen for histology and culture as above; blood cultures; urgent surgical assessment for debridement, because antifungal therapy alone rarely controls mucormycosis in devitalized tissue; and urgent infectious diseases involvement for systemic therapy. On drug selection, know the shape of it rather than the doses: echinocandins have no useful activity against Mucorales, and voriconazole has no Mucorales activity — breakthrough mucormycosis on voriconazole prophylaxis is a described phenomenon.
