Amphotericin B formulations and the mold-active triazoles isavuconazole and posaconazole are the agents with Mucorales coverage. Fusarium is intrinsically resistant to echinocandins and variably resistant to much else, which is why the species identification actually changes therapy.

Student pearl

Fix one mechanistic map and the antifungal classes stop being a memorization exercise. Allylamines (terbinafine) inhibit squalene epoxidase — the target mutated in resistant T. indotineae. Azoles inhibit lanosterol 14-alpha-demethylase in ergosterol synthesis, and they differ enormously in spectrum: fluconazole has essentially no mold activity, voriconazole covers Aspergillus but not Mucorales, and isavuconazole and posaconazole extend to Mucorales. Polyenes (amphotericin B) bind ergosterol directly and have the broadest mold coverage. Echinocandins inhibit beta-1,3-glucan synthase and are excellent against Candida — and useless against dermatophytes, Cryptococcus, Mucorales, and Fusarium. Griseofulvin is a microtubule inhibitor active only against dermatophytes. “Antifungal” is not one thing, and this is why the species name earns its cost.

Asked on rounds

The patient has an ulcer with a chain of nodules ascending the forearm. What do I tell the laboratory?

Say the words “nodular lymphangitic” or “sporotrichoid,” and list the differential, because it changes how the specimen is handled. The classic causes are Sporothrix, Mycobacterium marinum, Nocardia brasiliensis, Leishmania, and Francisella tularensis, along with other nontuberculous mycobacteria.

Two of those have specific laboratory requirements that will not happen by default. M. marinum grows optimally at a cooler temperature than routine mycobacterial cultures are incubated at — around 30 °C rather than 35–37 °C — so unless you request a low-temperature set, a true infection can be reported as no growth. Nocardia needs prolonged incubation and benefits from selective media, and is identified by its partial acid-fastness on a modified stain.

Then get the right specimen: a deep tissue biopsy or aspirate, split for mycobacterial, fungal, and routine bacterial culture plus histology. Ask about the aquarium, the fish tank cleaning, the roses, the sphagnum moss, the cat, and recent travel — the exposure history usually narrows this before any plate is read.

Why is my dermatophyte culture negative when the KOH clearly showed hyphae?

Several legitimate reasons, none of which mean you were wrong.

  • Nonviable organism. KOH and PAS detect fungal structure regardless of viability; culture requires living organism. Prior topical or systemic antifungal therapy is the commonest cause.
  • Sampling. Too little material, or material taken from the wrong place — the center of an annular plaque, or a distal nail clipping without subungual debris.
  • Media selection. If the organism was a non-dermatophyte mold, cycloheximide in standard dermatophyte media may have suppressed it. Request a parallel plate without cycloheximide.
  • Hold time. Some dermatophytes are slow. A plate discarded at one week is not a negative culture.
  • Transport. Scale sealed in a plastic bag retains moisture and grows contaminants that overgrow the pathogen. Paper envelopes or dry sterile containers are preferred.

If the clinical picture and direct microscopy agree, treat — and if you need a species name for a therapeutic decision, resample properly or ask about PCR.

The laboratory grew a mold from a toenail. Is it the pathogen?

Not necessarily, and this is a genuine interpretive problem rather than a technicality. Non-dermatophyte molds — Scopulariopsis brevicaulis, Fusarium, Aspergillus, Neoscytalidium — are common environmental contaminants of nail specimens and can be true pathogens, particularly in older patients and in previously traumatized nails.

The conventional approach to calling a non-dermatophyte mold causative requires convergent evidence: fungal elements visible on direct microscopy or PAS, isolation of the same mold on repeated separate specimens, absence of a dermatophyte, and a compatible clinical picture. A single isolate on one plate with a negative KOH is a contaminant until proven otherwise.

It matters therapeutically because these molds respond poorly or unpredictably to the agents used for dermatophyte onychomycosis, and because a Fusarium in the nail of a patient about to become neutropenic is a portal of entry for disseminated disease — a reason to involve the treating hematology team.

When does a superficial dermatophyte infection actually need a systemic drug?

The principle is anatomic: if the organism is somewhere a topical agent cannot reach in fungicidal concentration, it needs systemic therapy. That means:

  • Tinea capitis and tinea barbae — the organism is within the hair shaft and follicle.
  • Onychomycosis involving the matrix or a substantial proportion of the plate — though superficial white disease and limited distal disease are reasonable topical or device-based targets.
  • Majocchi granuloma and any follicular or dermal dermatophyte invasion.
  • Extensive, multifocal, or refractory glabrous disease, and disease in the immunosuppressed.

This is also why the diagnostic threshold rises with the therapy: a topical agent for a suspicious annular plaque is a low-cost empiric trial, whereas months of a systemic agent with drug interactions and monitoring should rest on a confirmed mycologic diagnosis.

How does antifungal susceptibility testing actually work, and when should I ask for it?

The reference methods are broth microdilution — separate standards for yeasts and for filamentous fungi — reporting a minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC), the lowest drug concentration that suppresses visible growth. The MIC is only interpretable against a breakpoint, and breakpoints are the limiting factor in mycology.

  • Yeasts. Clinical breakpoints exist for common Candida species against azoles and echinocandins, so a categorical susceptible or resistant result is meaningful. Much is predictable from species identification alone.
  • Molds. Testing is available, largely in reference laboratories, but interpretive breakpoints are limited; epidemiologic cutoff values are often used instead, which distinguish wild-type from non-wild-type isolates rather than predicting clinical outcome. Azole resistance in Aspergillus fumigatus, partly driven by agricultural azole use, is an active concern.
  • Dermatophytes. A reference method exists but there are no broadly accepted clinical breakpoints, so results are comparative. Reserve the request for genuine treatment failure after an adequate course, and pair it with species-level molecular identification.

Which of these your institution performs in-house versus refers out varies considerably. Ask the microbiology laboratory before you promise the patient a result and a timeline.

What to carry out of this section

  • Depth first. Superficial, subcutaneous, systemic, angioinvasive — the compartment determines the specimen, and the specimen determines what you can be told.
  • The specimen is almost never a surface swab. Scale from the advancing edge, plucked hairs with roots, proximal nail clipping plus subungual debris, or deep tissue split between formalin and a sterile container.
  • Inflammation is ecological information. A furious lesion suggests a zoophilic or geophilic organism and an animal or soil exposure that also needs addressing.
  • Anatomy dictates route. Hair, follicle, nail matrix, and dermis are beyond topical reach.
  • Confirm before committing. Months of systemic therapy deserve a positive test, and half of dystrophic nails are not fungal.
  • Tell the laboratory what you are thinking. Media, cycloheximide, lipid overlay, incubation temperature, hold time, and biosafety containment all depend on it — and none of them are chosen by default in your favor.
  • A black eschar in a neutropenic or ketoacidotic patient is the one fungal diagnosis measured in hours.

Viral infections

How they are actually diagnosed

Viral infections of the skin

You already read the morphology. What separates a good virology consult from a wasted one is the specimen: which lesion you sample, how old it is, what you scrape, what medium it travels in, and which assay you actually ordered. Almost every viral dermatosis below is now diagnosed by nucleic acid amplification — but a polymerase chain reaction (PCR) assay can only amplify what you put in the tube.

First principle: viral dermatology is a sampling problem

Viral culture built the field and has largely left routine practice. Most clinical virology laboratories now run PCR — on herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), enterovirus, parvovirus B19, orthopoxvirus and mpox — because it is faster, far more sensitive, and works on degraded material that culture cannot rescue. That shifts the burden onto you. The assay is excellent; the swab is the weak link.

  • Pick the youngest lesion. A fresh, intact vesicle is the highest-yield target. Yield falls steeply as lesions crust, because you are sampling scab rather than infected keratinocytes.
  • Deroof, then scrape the base. For herpesviruses, lift the roof with a needle or blade and swab the floor firmly enough to harvest cells. Vesicle fluid alone is a lower-yield specimen; a light dab on an intact blister roof is the single commonest reason a true HSV case comes back negative.
  • Swab material matters. Use flocked nylon or Dacron on a plastic shaft. Calcium alginate swabs and wooden shafts contain substances that inhibit amplification and can render a true positive falsely negative.
  • Confirm the transport medium with your own laboratory. Some assays are validated on viral transport medium, some on a proprietary molecular transport medium, some on a dry swab. This is genuinely institution-dependent — call and ask rather than guessing.
  • Do not freeze in a standard freezer. Refrigerate and transport promptly; ordinary −20 °C storage degrades labile viruses, VZV worst of all.
  • Split the specimen when infection is broad on the differential. One swab for viral PCR, one for bacterial culture, and, when you biopsy, ask for a sterile portion to microbiology in addition to formalin. Formalin is the end of the road for culture and for many molecular assays.
Highest-yield lesion age< 48–72 h
Transport temperature2–8 °C
Conventional cell-culture incubation35–37 °C
Pitfall

The three ways a real herpesvirus infection is reported as negative: you swabbed an intact vesicle roof instead of the deroofed base; you swabbed a week-old crust; or you used the bacterial culture swab sitting in the room, which may be a calcium alginate or charcoal-containing device that inhibits amplification. A negative PCR from a bad specimen does not exclude the disease — if the clinical picture is eczema herpeticum or disseminated zoster, resample properly and treat while you wait.

MethodWhat it actually answersTurnaroundLimitation you must know
PCR / nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT)Which virus, and for HSV which typehoursOnly as good as the specimen; a positive from a chronically shed site does not prove causation of the lesion in front of you
Direct fluorescent antibody (DFA)Viral antigen in scraped cells1–2 hRequires abundant cellular material; less sensitive than PCR; withdrawn from many laboratories
Viral culture (conventional or shell vial)A live isolatedaysSlow and insensitive, catastrophically so for VZV and coxsackie A; its remaining virtue is producing an isolate for phenotypic antiviral susceptibility testing
Tzanck smearMultinucleated giant cells with nuclear moldingminutesCannot separate HSV from VZV, and cannot type HSV; sensitivity is moderate and operator-dependent
HistopathologyTissue-level pattern, plus immunohistochemistry and special stainsdaysFormalin-fixed tissue cannot be cultured; decide before you drop the specimen in the jar
SerologyPast exposure, or a recent immune responsedaysRarely answers “what is this lesion today”; unreliable in the immunocompromised, who may not seroconvert
Electron microscopyVirion morphology from lesion fluid or crustspecialistAvailable in few centers; still useful for parapoxvirus and orthopoxvirus morphology

Herpes simplex virus

Two alphaherpesviruses, HSV-1 and HSV-2, that establish latency in sensory ganglia and recur along the same neural territory. The clinical anatomy has drifted: HSV-1 now accounts for a large and growing share of first-episode genital herpes in many high-income settings, so the old “type 1 above the waist, type 2 below” heuristic is a starting point, not a diagnosis. Typing still matters, because HSV-2 genital disease recurs more often and that changes the counseling.

The recognizable phenotypes

Grouped vesicles on an erythematous base, recurring in one place

Orolabial. Primary herpetic gingivostomatitis in children involves the gingiva, anterior tongue and perioral skin with fever and refusal to eat; recurrences are the vermilion-border cold sore with a prodrome of tingling.

Genital. Primary disease is painful, bilateral, with tender inguinal nodes and often dysuria; recurrences are unilateral, fewer, shorter. Atypical fissures and erosions are common and are frequently misattributed to trauma or candidiasis.

Herpetic whitlow. Deep-seated painful vesicles on a distal digit, often on an erythematous swollen pulp. Historically an occupational infection of dental and respiratory-care staff before universal gloving; in children it is autoinoculation from gingivostomatitis and a thumb in the mouth.

Herpes gladiatorum. Contact-sport transmission, classically wrestling, with clusters on the head, neck, and lateral trunk or arms — the sites that grind against an opponent. Outbreaks are a team-level problem, and return-to-mat rules are governed by sport-specific regulations rather than by dermatology.

Pitfall

Herpetic whitlow gets incised as a felon or paronychia. Incision does not help, seeds the operator, and delays antiviral therapy. If the digit is exquisitely painful with clustered vesicles and no fluctuance, deroof one vesicle for PCR and keep the scalpel away.

Laboratory approach

PCR from a deroofed base, and almost nothing else

PCR is the test of choice and should be type-specific. It works on vesicle-base scrapings, ulcer swabs, and mucosal specimens, and outperforms every alternative on both fresh and older lesions.

DFA is faster and can be run on a slide of scraped cells, but it needs abundant intact cells and is meaningfully less sensitive; most laboratories that still offer it use it as an adjunct, not a replacement.

Culture is slow and insensitive, particularly for crusted or recurrent lesions where viral titers are low. Its one enduring role: an isolate is required for phenotypic antiviral susceptibility testing.

Tzanck can be done at the bedside and can tell you “this is a herpesvirus,” which is occasionally decisive at 2 a.m. It cannot tell you which one.

Serology has essentially no role in diagnosing the lesion in front of you. Type-specific glycoprotein G-based IgG assays answer a different question — has this person ever been infected with HSV-1 or HSV-2 — and are used for discordant-couple counseling or for recurrent genital disease that keeps swabbing negative. HSV IgM is not a reliable marker of acute infection and should not be used to date an episode.

Preferred specimenDeroofed vesicle base or ulcer floor, scraped
Preferred assayType-specific HSV PCR
Least useful in acute lesionsSerology

Eczema herpeticum — the dermatologic emergency

Kaposi varicelliform eruption is disseminated cutaneous HSV in skin whose barrier and immune environment are already abnormal. Atopic dermatitis is the classic substrate, but the same event occurs in Darier disease, Hailey-Hailey disease, pemphigus foliaceus, ichthyoses, cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, and burns. Impaired barrier function and a type 2-skewed cutaneous immune response are the usual explanations offered, and early-onset severe atopic dermatitis is the highest-risk group.

The morphology is the diagnosis: monomorphic, punched-out erosions of uniform size, often with scalloped or crenulated borders where adjacent erosions coalesce, hemorrhagic crust, and a striking disproportion between the amount of skin involved and how unwell and how sore the patient is. It expands over hours to days, favors the head, neck and upper trunk in atopic dermatitis, and is accompanied by fever and lymphadenopathy.

Management is systemic aciclovir-class therapy, started on clinical suspicion — not after the PCR resolves. Urgent ophthalmology involvement if the lesions are periocular, because herpetic keratitis is sight-threatening. Admit for intravenous therapy if the patient cannot take oral, is systemically unwell, is immunosuppressed, or the eruption is extensive.

What to do next

Send two specimens from the same field: a deroofed-base swab for HSV PCR and a separate swab for bacterial culture and susceptibility. Secondary infection with Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pyogenes is common and coexists with the virus. The correct answer is antiviral therapy plus culture-directed antibacterial cover where indicated — not one instead of the other.

Do not confuse

Eczema herpeticum vs. impetiginized eczema. Both occur in a flaring atopic patient with crusted, weeping skin, and eczema herpeticum is repeatedly discharged on oral antibiotics alone. The discriminator is monomorphism: bacterial superinfection gives honey-colored crust over lesions of varied size and shape following the underlying eczema, while eczema herpeticum gives uniformly sized, round, punched-out erosions that look stamped into the skin, plus pain and fever out of proportion. When you cannot decide, treat for both and swab for both. Antibiotics alone in true eczema herpeticum lose you the days that matter.

Advanced pearl

Aciclovir resistance is rare in immunocompetent hosts and a real problem in the profoundly immunosuppressed, where it presents as a persistent, enlarging, verrucous or ulcerated HSV lesion that fails to heal on adequate therapy. The commonest mechanism is loss or alteration of the viral thymidine kinase, which is why cross-resistance to the whole nucleoside-analogue group is expected and why the salvage agents — foscarnet and cidofovir — still work, because neither requires viral thymidine kinase for activation. Practically: this is one of the last situations in which you must ask the laboratory for culture, because phenotypic susceptibility testing needs a viable isolate; genotypic sequencing of the relevant viral genes is offered by some reference laboratories. Warn the laboratory in advance — this is not a routine add-on.

Varicella zoster virus

One virus, two diseases separated by decades of latency in the dorsal root and cranial nerve ganglia.

Primary varicella

Lesions in different stages at the same time

Successive crops mean that at any moment you see macules, papules, vesicles on an erythematous base, pustules and crusts side by side. Distribution is centripetal — trunk-predominant — and, it involves the scalp and the oral mucosa and characteristically spares the palms and soles — the historical contrast with smallpox, which was centrifugal and involved palms and soles. Contagious from shortly before the rash until every lesion has crusted; airborne plus contact precautions.

Adults, pregnant patients, and the immunocompromised carry disproportionate risk of varicella pneumonia and disseminated disease.

Student pearl

“Different stages simultaneously” for varicella; “same stage within an anatomic area” for the orthopoxviruses. That single contrast has been an examination discriminator for a century and became relevant again in 2022.

Herpes zoster

Dermatomal, unilateral, does not cross the midline

Prodromal pain, itch or dysesthesia precedes grouped vesicles confined to one or two contiguous dermatomes. A handful of scattered lesions just beyond the dermatome can occur in a normal host and does not by itself mean dissemination.

Disseminated zoster means lesions appearing outside the primary and immediately adjacent dermatomes — a threshold of roughly twenty such lesions is a widely used convention rather than a validated cut-off. It implies significant immunosuppression, mandates a search for the cause, and requires airborne plus contact precautions and systemic intravenous therapy.

Zoster ophthalmicus involves the ophthalmic division of the trigeminal nerve. The Hutchinson sign — vesicles on the tip, side or root of the nose, the territory of the nasociliary branch — raises the probability of ocular involvement, but its absence does not exclude it. Refer urgently either way.

Ramsay Hunt syndrome (herpes zoster oticus) is facial nerve involvement with vesicles in the external auditory canal, concha or on the anterior tongue and palate, plus a lower-motor-neuron facial palsy and sometimes vertigo or hearing loss. Recovery of facial function is poorer than in Bell palsy, which is why the ear must be examined in every facial palsy.

Antiviral therapy is most effective when started within 72 hours of rash onset, and should be given irrespective of that window in zoster ophthalmicus, disseminated disease, or immunosuppression. Zoster in a young adult without an obvious cause warrants HIV testing.

Postherpetic neuralgia is the dominant morbidity; increasing age is the strongest risk factor, with severe prodromal pain and severe acute rash also contributing.

Vaccination has changed what walks into your clinic

Widespread childhood varicella immunization has reduced incidence and, importantly, changed the phenotype. Breakthrough varicella in a vaccinated child is milder, with fewer lesions that are often predominantly maculopapular rather than frankly vesicular — which makes it far harder to recognize clinically and far more dependent on the laboratory. Zoster vaccination in adults has moved in most high-income programs from a live-attenuated product to a recombinant adjuvanted subunit product, and eligibility criteria are region-specific.

Zoster does occur in vaccinated children and can arise from the vaccine (Oka) strain. Reference and public health laboratories can genotype the isolate or amplicon to distinguish vaccine strain from wild-type virus — a request worth making when the epidemiology or a vaccine-safety question hangs on it.

Preferred specimenDeroofed vesicle base; crust is acceptable for VZV
Preferred assayVZV PCR (multiplexed with HSV in most laboratories)
Assay to avoid relying onCulture — VZV is labile and grows poorly

Zoster sine herpete — dermatomal pain without a rash — is a diagnosis of exclusion; when the question is central nervous system involvement or VZV vasculopathy, the specimen is cerebrospinal fluid, not skin — and request CSF anti-VZV IgG alongside VZV PCR, because CSF PCR is frequently negative in VZV vasculopathy while intrathecal anti-VZV IgG is the more sensitive marker.

Best-case scenario. Clean with saline rather than alcohol if you can, lift the roof with a sterile needle or the corner of a blade, and scrape the floor firmly with a flocked swab — you want keratinocytes, not just fluid. Send for the specific PCR you want; herpesvirus panels typically cover HSV-1, HSV-2 and VZV together.

If mpox or another orthopoxvirus is on the differential, do not follow the herpes reflex. Public health guidance generally asks for vigorous swabbing of the lesion surface without deroofing, and the collection device and medium are specified by the receiving public health laboratory. Call them first.

Debride any adherent slough, then swab the ulcer floor with pressure. For genital ulceration, the differential is not viral alone: send HSV PCR and the syphilis workup your region uses, and take a serologic panel including a fourth-generation HIV antigen/antibody test. Multiplex genital-ulcer NAAT panels exist in some laboratories and typically combine HSV, Treponema pallidum and Haemophilus ducreyi targets — availability is entirely institution-dependent.

In eczema herpeticum the erosions are the specimen. Choose one at the advancing edge rather than a central crust.

Yield falls, but not to zero. For VZV, crust material is a legitimate specimen and PCR performs respectably on it. For HSV, expect reduced sensitivity and interpret a negative result against the clinical probability. If the eruption is still evolving, the better move is to wait a few hours for a fresh vesicle rather than to sample old scab.

For suspected orf or milker's nodule, crust and lesion scrapings are exactly what a reference laboratory wants for parapoxvirus PCR or electron microscopy.

When you biopsy a violaceous nodule, an umbilicated papule in an immunocompromised patient, or an atypical persistent ulcer, split the specimen. One core in formalin for histopathology, immunohistochemistry and special stains; a second core in sterile saline to microbiology for bacterial, mycobacterial and fungal culture, and for molecular testing. Tell the pathologist what you suspect — the stains that answer the question are ordered on the basis of your clinical note.

Formalin destroys the option of culture. That decision is made at the bedside, not later.

Human papillomavirus

A non-enveloped double-stranded DNA virus whose entire life cycle is chained to a differentiating squamous epithelium — which is precisely why it cannot be grown in a routine clinical laboratory.

Cutaneous warts

Morphology follows site and type

Verruca vulgaris — hyperkeratotic papules with disrupted dermatoglyphics and thrombosed capillaries seen on paring, typically associated with types such as 1, 2, 4, 27 and 57.

Plantar — endophytic and painful with lateral compression; deep myrmecia lesions are classically HPV 1.

Flat warts — small, skin-colored to tan, minimally scaly, often linear from koebnerization on the face and shins; types 3 and 10.

Filiform — finger-like projections on the face, eyelids and neck.

Butcher's warts — extensive hand warts in meat, poultry and fish handlers, classically associated with HPV 7. A genuine occupational dermatosis worth asking about.

Anogenital disease

Low-risk and high-risk types

Condylomata acuminata are predominantly associated with the low-risk types 6 and 11, which are also the types implicated in recurrent respiratory papillomatosis. These types cause proliferative disease and are not the drivers of carcinoma.

High-risk types, 16 and 18 foremost, are associated with cervical, anal, vulvar, vaginal and penile squamous carcinoma and with a distinct subset of oropharyngeal squamous carcinoma. HPV 16 is also linked to periungual and digital squamous cell carcinoma, which is why a “wart” on a nail unit that resists treatment, distorts the nail, or bleeds deserves a biopsy rather than another round of cryotherapy.

Prophylactic vaccination targets the principal oncogenic types together with 6 and 11 in the multivalent products; schedules and eligible ages are set nationally.

The laboratory reality

HPV is not cultured

There is no clinical culture for HPV and no useful serologic test for an individual lesion. Diagnosis of cutaneous warts is clinical; histopathology, showing papillomatosis, hypergranulosis and koilocytes, confirms atypical or treatment-resistant lesions and, more importantly, excludes carcinoma.

Molecular typing is validated and clinically actionable in the anogenital and oncologic setting — cervical and anal screening specimens — not on a plantar wart. Sending a shave of a hand wart for “HPV typing” is a common trainee order that the laboratory cannot meaningfully act on.

p16 immunohistochemistry is used in pathology as a surrogate marker of transcriptionally active high-risk HPV, most established in oropharyngeal squamous carcinoma.

CultureNot available
Routine cutaneous wartClinical diagnosis
TypingAnogenital and oncologic contexts
Advanced pearl

Epidermodysplasia verruciformis is the human experiment that proves cutaneous HPV can be oncogenic. An inherited defect in a small number of genes governing keratinocyte control of beta-papillomaviruses produces lifelong susceptibility to types such as HPV 5 and 8, giving flat wart-like papules and pityriasis versicolor-like macules from childhood, and squamous cell carcinoma on sun-exposed skin in adulthood — ultraviolet radiation and virus acting together. An acquired, phenocopying “EV-like” syndrome occurs in advanced HIV infection and in transplant recipients, and should prompt you to check immune status. Management is lifelong photoprotection and surveillance; there is no antiviral cure.

Molluscum contagiosum

A poxvirus — a molluscipoxvirus — and the only one of the group most dermatologists see weekly.

Clinical and pathologic

Umbilicated, pearly, and self-limited in the immunocompetent

Dome-shaped, skin-colored to pearly papules with a central umbilication, in children on the trunk, axillae and flexures, spread by direct contact, fomites, shared towels and swimming. In adults the pubic, genital and inner-thigh distribution reflects sexual transmission. Autoinoculation produces linear clusters along scratch lines, and molluscum is particularly happy in atopic dermatitis.

Because poxviruses replicate entirely in the cytoplasm, the diagnostic inclusion is cytoplasmic: the large eosinophilic Henderson-Paterson bodies that fill and displace the nucleus of infected keratinocytes within lobules of acanthotic epidermis.

MCV-1 accounts for most disease overall; MCV-2 is over-represented in adult and HIV-associated infection.

Student pearl

A single molluscum that becomes red, swollen and tender, with surrounding eczematous change, is usually not bacterially infected. This inflammatory reaction — the “beginning of the end” — heralds immune clearance and resolution. Recognizing it saves the child an unnecessary antibiotic and the parents an unnecessary fright.

Laboratory and immune context

No culture; the test that matters is a serologic one

Molluscum contagiosum virus is not cultivable in routine clinical laboratories. Diagnosis is clinical; if there is doubt, curette a lesion and send it, or squash the core on a slide — the inclusion bodies are unmistakable. PCR exists in some reference and research laboratories and is not part of ordinary practice.

Extensive, facial, giant or refractory molluscum in an adult is an immunologic finding, not a cosmetic one. Offer HIV testing. In a patient with known advanced HIV, widespread facial molluscum-like papules is a presentation that must be biopsied rather than assumed.

CultureNot available
Confirmatory testHistopathology or a squash preparation
Adult with extensive diseaseHIV testing
Do not confuse

Molluscum vs. disseminated fungal infection in advanced HIV. Umbilicated, dome-shaped, flesh-colored facial papules in a patient with a very low CD4 count are the classic cutaneous presentation of disseminated cryptococcosis, and of disseminated histoplasmosis, coccidioidomycosis and Talaromyces marneffei infection in the appropriate geography. These are systemic, life-threatening infections whose first visible sign is on the face, and they can be clinically indistinguishable from molluscum. Biopsy: send one portion in formalin for histology with fungal stains and one sterile portion for fungal culture, and send a serum cryptococcal antigen with blood cultures at the same visit. Curetting these lesions as “molluscum” and moving on is a genuine cause of preventable death.

Enteroviruses and parvovirus B19

Two very different viruses that share one teaching point: the eruption you are looking at may be an immune phenomenon occurring after the infectious phase, which changes what you tell the family.

Enteroviruses

Non-enveloped RNA picornaviruses, summer and autumn in temperate climates

Classic hand-foot-and-mouth disease — coxsackievirus A16 above all — gives oval gray-white vesicles on erythematous bases on the palms, soles and lateral digits, oral erosions, and a low-grade fever in a preschool child. Enterovirus A71 produces similar skin findings but is the type associated with neurologic complications including brainstem encephalitis and acute flaccid paralysis, and with large outbreaks in the Asia-Pacific region.

Coxsackievirus A6 rewrote the chapter. It causes a far more widespread, atypical eruption that extends well beyond hands, feet and mouth — perioral, buttocks, limbs — is frequently bullous and hemorrhagic, has a striking predilection for areas of active or previous atopic dermatitis (“eczema coxsackium”, a mimic of eczema herpeticum), and readily infects adults. Weeks later come palmoplantar desquamation and onychomadesis, proximal nail-plate separation and shedding, which brings the family back convinced something new has happened.

Herpangina is coxsackie A disease of the posterior oropharynx — vesicles and ulcers on the soft palate, uvula and tonsillar pillars — in contrast to primary herpetic gingivostomatitis, which is anterior and involves the gingiva and perioral skin.

Enterovirus laboratory

Where a positive result can still mislead you

Enterovirus reverse-transcription PCR on a vesicle swab, throat swab, stool or cerebrospinal fluid is the workhorse. Culture is insensitive for many coxsackie A types and is not a reasonable first move.

The interpretive trap: enteroviruses are shed in stool for weeks after an infection resolves. A positive stool PCR therefore does not establish that enterovirus is causing the rash in front of you today. Lesion and throat specimens carry more weight for attribution.

Typing — distinguishing A6 from A16 from EV-A71 — requires sequencing, usually of the VP1 region, and is generally performed by reference or public health laboratories for outbreak investigation rather than for individual care.

Be aware that multiplex respiratory panels commonly report a combined “enterovirus/rhinovirus” target and cannot separate the two, which limits what such a result tells you about a rash.

Best attributing specimenLesion swab, then throat
Weakest attributing specimenStool
Typing methodVP1 sequencing, reference laboratory

Parvovirus B19

A single-stranded DNA virus with a taste for erythroid progenitors

Erythema infectiosum — fifth disease. A brief nonspecific prodrome, then confluent erythema of both cheeks with circumoral pallor, followed by a lacy, reticular erythema on the extensor limbs and trunk that fades and recurs over weeks with heat, sunlight, exercise or emotion. That relapsing behavior is diagnostic and reassures rather than alarms.

Papular-purpuric gloves-and-socks syndrome is the adolescent and young-adult presentation: sharply demarcated erythema, edema and petechiae or purpura of the hands and feet stopping abruptly at the wrists and ankles, often with painful oral erosions and fever.

Arthropathy in adults, particularly women, is symmetric and small-joint and mimics early rheumatoid arthritis closely enough to reach a rheumatology clinic.

The virus uses the P antigen (globoside) on erythroid precursors as its receptor — which explains everything that follows, and why the rare individuals who lack P antigen are naturally resistant.

The two situations that are not benign

Ask about hemolysis and about pregnancy, every time

Transient aplastic crisis. In anyone with chronic hemolysis and a shortened red cell lifespan — sickle cell disease, hereditary spherocytosis, thalassemia — a week of arrested erythropoiesis produces abrupt severe anemia with reticulocytopenia. These patients are usually still profoundly viremic and highly infectious when they present, and they need isolation precautions.

Pregnancy. Maternal infection can cause fetal anemia, hydrops fetalis and loss, with the greatest risk in the earlier half of pregnancy. Exposure during pregnancy warrants serologic assessment and obstetric involvement — this is not a reassurance-only conversation.

Immunocompromise. Patients who cannot mount antibody may fail to clear the virus and develop chronic pure red cell aplasia. Here serology is unreliable and B19 DNA PCR is the correct test, as it is for fetal diagnosis on amniotic fluid and for aplastic crisis. In the immunocompetent patient with a typical rash, IgM and IgG serology is what the laboratory expects.

Immunocompetent, typical rashB19 IgM and IgG serology
Immunocompromised or fetalB19 DNA PCR
Student pearl

The counterintuitive one, and it is asked constantly. In classic erythema infectiosum the rash is immune-complex mediated and appears after viremia has resolved. By the time you see the slapped cheeks, the child is generally no longer infectious, and exclusion from school or daycare achieves nothing. Every other exanthem you were taught works the other way round. The two exceptions that keep it honest: patients presenting with aplastic crisis are still viremic and infectious, and patients with papular-purpuric gloves-and-socks syndrome may still be viremic at the time of the eruption. So the rule is not “B19 rash equals non-infectious” — it is “the fifth-disease rash equals non-infectious.” Which patient is in front of you decides the infection-control answer.

Roseola, measles and rubella

The classic exanthems, reframed around which laboratory test to send and who else needs to know.

EntityAgentEruptionThe extracutaneous tellLaboratory
Roseola infantum (exanthem subitum)Human herpesvirus 6B, less often HHV-7Rose-pink macules and papules on the trunk appearing as the fever breaksThree to five days of high fever in a well-looking infant; Nagayama spots on the soft palate and uvula; febrile seizuresClinical. Serology is unhelpful because seroprevalence is near universal
MeaslesMeasles morbillivirusMorbilliform eruption starting at the hairline and behind the ears, spreading cephalocaudally and becoming confluent, then desquamatingThe prodrome — fever with cough, coryza and conjunctivitis — and Koplik spots on the buccal mucosa a day or two before the rashurgent Measles IgM serology plus RT-PCR on a throat or nasopharyngeal swab and urine; genotyping at the public health laboratory
RubellaRubella virusPink macules starting on the face and descending, clearing within about three daysTender posterior auricular, suboccipital and posterior cervical lymphadenopathy; arthralgia in adult womenIgM serology plus RT-PCR. False-positive rubella IgM is a recognized problem — confirm before acting
Why it matters

Measles is a public health emergency, and the notification comes before the result. It is among the most transmissible pathogens known, requires airborne precautions, and is immediately notifiable in essentially every jurisdiction. If you suspect it, you mask and isolate the patient on arrival, telephone your infection-prevention team and public health authority, and let them tell you which specimens their laboratory wants — testing pathways for measles are usually routed through public health rather than the hospital laboratory. Two clinical caveats: measles IgM may be negative in the first days of the rash, so a single early negative serology does not exclude it and PCR should be sent alongside; and partially immune or immunocompromised patients may have a modified rash or, rarely, no rash at all. Complications range from otitis and pneumonia to acute encephalitis and, years later, subacute sclerosing panencephalitis. The World Health Organization recommends vitamin A supplementation for children with measles.

Advanced pearl

Two HHV-6 facts that matter once you rotate through transplant dermatology. First, HHV-6 reactivation is well described in drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS) and is part of the reason DRESS behaves like a viral illness with a drug trigger. Second, chromosomally integrated HHV-6: in a small proportion of people the viral genome is integrated into telomeric DNA in every nucleated cell and inherited through the germline. Whole-blood HHV-6 PCR in these individuals returns persistently enormous viral loads that have nothing to do with active infection, and has led to patients being treated with antivirals for a laboratory artifact. If an HHV-6 viral load looks impossibly high and does not move with treatment, ask the virology laboratory about integration.

Zoonotic and occupational poxviruses

The parapoxviruses are a small, self-limited, and frequently over-treated corner of dermatology. The diagnosis lives in the exposure history, not in the morphology.

Orf (contagious ecthyma)

Orf virus, from sheep and goats

Transmission is direct contact with infected animals or contaminated equipment: farmers, shepherds, shearers, veterinarians, abattoir and butchery workers, and people who handle or slaughter animals for religious festivals — clusters around Eid al-Adha are well described in the literature and in urban centers far from any farm.

One or a few nodules on the fingers, hands or forearms, classically evolving through a sequence of stages over roughly six weeks — papular, target-like with a red center and white ring, weeping and nodular, then regenerative, papillomatous, and finally regressive. Low-grade fever, lymphangitis and regional lymphadenopathy can accompany it. A secondary erythema multiforme reaction some weeks later is a recognized sequel and is not a drug allergy.

Milker's nodule

Pseudocowpox virus, from cattle

Acquired from lesions on the teats and udders of cattle, so the classic patient is a dairy worker or anyone hand-milking. Clinically and histologically it is essentially indistinguishable from orf; the animal decides the name.

Histology of either shows ballooning degeneration with full-thickness epidermal necrosis, eosinophilic intracytoplasmic inclusions, and a densely vascular dermal infiltrate — which is exactly why these lesions are misread as pyogenic granuloma.

Laboratory confirmation, when needed, is parapoxvirus PCR on a lesion swab, crust or biopsy at a reference laboratory, or electron microscopy demonstrating the characteristic ovoid parapoxvirus particles. There is no routine culture. In profoundly immunosuppressed patients lesions can become giant and progressive and warrant specialist input.

Natural historySelf-limited over about 6 weeks
ConfirmationParapoxvirus PCR or electron microscopy
What to do next

The whole management of orf and milker's nodule is: take the exposure history, confirm if you must, protect the lesion, and wait. Do not excise. Do not incise and drain. Do not treat as a bacterial abscess. These lesions are self-limiting, surgery leaves a worse scar than the disease, and the operator risks inoculating themselves. Antibiotics are indicated only for genuine secondary bacterial infection. Counsel on animal-handling gloves and hand hygiene, and warn about the possible erythema multiforme reaction so it is not later blamed on a drug.

Mpox

An orthopoxvirus, in the same genus as variola and vaccinia, with two recognized clades and an epidemiology that changed abruptly in 2022.

What the lesions look like and why that matters

The classic description is of deep-seated, firm, well-circumscribed papules that become vesicles and then umbilicated pustules, often painful, and characteristically at the same stage of evolution within a given anatomic area — the contrast with the mixed-stage crops of varicella. Distribution is centrifugal, with palm and sole involvement, and lesions can be few in number. Prominent lymphadenopathy is the feature that historically separated mpox from smallpox at the bedside.

The 2022 global outbreak, driven by clade II virus and transmitted largely through sexual networks, shifted the presentation toward anogenital, perianal and oral lesions, sometimes a solitary lesion, frequently with proctitis, tonsillar or pharyngeal involvement, and lesions at different stages across different body sites. Very few of those patients had the textbook centrifugal eruption. If you anchor on the old description you will miss the modern case.

Diagnostic testOrthopoxvirus and mpox-specific PCR on a lesion swab
CollectionVigorous swabbing of the lesion; deroofing generally not required
ReportingNotifiable — contact public health before or while testing
What to do next

Suspected mpox is a coordinated event rather than a swab. Place the patient in a single room and apply the precautions your jurisdiction currently specifies — contact and droplet plus airborne measures for aerosol-generating procedures is the general shape, but guidance has changed repeatedly since 2022 and is genuinely region-specific, so check your local infection-prevention policy rather than relying on memory. Notify public health, then collect lesion swabs using the device and medium the receiving laboratory requests, label them as suspected orthopoxvirus, and inform the laboratory before the specimen arrives so it is handled at the correct containment level. Test concurrently for other sexually transmitted infections, including syphilis and HIV, because co-infection was common in the 2022 outbreak and secondary syphilis is a direct mimic. Antiviral therapy and vaccination are available through public health protocols, and access, indications and supporting evidence differ by country.

Human herpesvirus 8 and Kaposi sarcoma

A gammaherpesvirus driving an endothelial spindle-cell proliferation — and one of the few situations where a biopsy separates an incurable neoplasm from a curable bacterial infection.

Older men of Mediterranean, Middle Eastern and Eastern European origin. Violaceous macules, plaques and nodules on the distal lower legs and feet, often with lymphedema, running an indolent course over years. Visceral involvement is uncommon. Treatment is frequently local, and the competing risk is a second malignancy rather than the Kaposi sarcoma itself.

Described in sub-Saharan Africa before the HIV pandemic and independent of it. Behavior spans an indolent adult nodular form to aggressive infiltrative disease, and includes a lymphadenopathic form in children that can be fulminant with little or no skin involvement. HHV-8 seroprevalence in these populations is high, which is the background against which the disease occurs.

Transplant recipients and others on sustained immunosuppression, arising either from reactivation of the recipient's own HHV-8 or from donor-derived virus. The therapeutic lever is the immunosuppression itself: reduction, or a switch within the regimen toward an mTOR-inhibitor-based approach, can produce regression. That decision belongs to the transplant team and is balanced against graft rejection.

An AIDS-defining illness, more aggressive, often widely distributed with a tendency to follow skin tension lines, and with mucosal involvement — the hard palate is a classic site — plus gastrointestinal and pulmonary disease. Effective antiretroviral therapy is the foundation of treatment, with systemic chemotherapy added for extensive or visceral disease. HHV-8 also causes primary effusion lymphoma and multicentric Castleman disease, so a new Kaposi sarcoma diagnosis should prompt a broader assessment.

Do not confuse

Kaposi sarcoma vs. bacillary angiomatosis. In a patient with advanced HIV, friable violaceous-to-red vascular papules and nodules may be either. Bacillary angiomatosis is caused by Bartonella henselae (cat and cat-flea exposure) or Bartonella quintana (body lice, homelessness), can involve liver and spleen as peliosis, and is curable with a macrolide or tetracycline-class antibiotic. Kaposi sarcoma is not. The distinction is made on biopsy: bacillary angiomatosis shows a lobular vascular proliferation with neutrophils and amphophilic granular material, and a Warthin-Starry silver stain reveals clumped bacilli; Kaposi sarcoma shows spindle cells with slit-like vascular spaces and slotted red cells, and is confirmed by HHV-8 latency-associated nuclear antigen immunohistochemistry, which is negative in bacillary angiomatosis. Bartonella is fastidious and recovered poorly on routine culture, so tissue PCR or serology carries the diagnosis when histology is equivocal. Biopsy the violaceous nodule. Do not assume.

1
Violaceous or red vascular papules and nodules in an immunocompromised patient

Resist a clinical-only diagnosis. Take a punch biopsy and split it.

2
Formalin portion

Request routine histology plus HHV-8 latency-associated nuclear antigen immunohistochemistry and a Warthin-Starry stain. Write the differential on the request form — these stains are ordered because you asked.

3
Sterile portion to microbiology

Bacterial, mycobacterial and fungal culture, and hold for molecular testing. Tell the laboratory Bartonella is on the differential so the specimen is not discarded at 48 hours.

Bacillary angiomatosis

Warthin-Starry-positive bacilli, neutrophilic vascular proliferation, HHV-8 negative. Treat with an appropriate antibiotic class and assess for systemic involvement. Curable.

Kaposi sarcoma

Spindle cells with slit-like vessels, HHV-8 positive. Stage it, optimize antiretroviral therapy if HIV-associated or address immunosuppression if iatrogenic, and involve oncology for extensive or visceral disease.

Acute retroviral syndrome

The can't-miss viral exanthem, and the one where the reflex test gives you the wrong answer.

The clinical picture

Roughly two to four weeks after exposure

A mononucleosis-like illness — fever, sore throat, generalized lymphadenopathy, myalgia, headache, sometimes diarrhea — with a non-pruritic morbilliform eruption favoring the upper trunk, neck and face, and occasionally the palms and soles.

The finding that should stop you is painful mucocutaneous ulceration: shallow, well-demarcated oral, genital, anal or esophageal ulcers occurring together with the exanthem. A morbilliform rash plus mucosal ulcers plus fever is acute HIV until proven otherwise.

Secondary syphilis is the principal mimic and is common as a co-infection, so both are tested for together, not sequentially.

The laboratory point

Antibody may not have arrived yet

During acute infection the patient is intensely viremic but may not yet have seroconverted. An antibody-only test can be negative — and that includes many point-of-care rapid tests and oral-fluid devices.

Order a laboratory-based fourth-generation antigen/antibody immunoassay, which detects p24 antigen and therefore closes part of the window, and an HIV-1 RNA (viral load) test, which becomes detectable earliest. In suspected acute infection you send both.

A positive result is the beginning of care, not the end of the encounter: immediate linkage to HIV services, partner notification through the appropriate public health route, and concurrent screening for other sexually transmitted infections.

Earliest detectable markerHIV-1 RNA
Preferred screening assayFourth-generation antigen/antibody
Can be falsely negativeAntibody-only and rapid oral-fluid tests
Pitfall

This is the classic missed diagnosis in the emergency department and the walk-in clinic. A young adult with fever and pharyngitis is given an aminopenicillin; a morbilliform rash follows a few days later; the rash is labeled a drug eruption or “a viral exanthem”; a rapid HIV test is negative and everyone relaxes. Every step is plausible and the conclusion is wrong. Two safeguards: examine the mouth and the genitals in every morbilliform eruption, because the ulcers are the tell; and understand that a negative antibody-based HIV test in the window period is expected, not reassuring. If acute HIV is on your differential, the negative rapid test does not remove it — only a fourth-generation assay together with an HIV-1 RNA test does.

Consolidation

You are consulted on a 4-year-old with severe atopic dermatitis, fever, and rapidly spreading crusted facial lesions. What do you send, and what do you start?

Examine for monomorphic punched-out erosions with scalloped borders, especially at the advancing edge and around the eyes. Deroof or select an early erosion and send a firmly scraped swab for HSV PCR in the medium your laboratory specifies, and take a separate swab for bacterial culture and susceptibility. Start systemic aciclovir-class therapy on clinical suspicion — do not wait for the PCR. Assess hydration and oral intake and admit for intravenous therapy if the child cannot drink or is systemically unwell. Ophthalmology review is mandatory if the lesions are periocular. Add antibacterial cover directed by culture if there is clinical evidence of superinfection; do not substitute it for the antiviral.

Why does a Tzanck smear showing multinucleated giant cells not finish the job?

Because it identifies a cytopathic effect shared by the alphaherpesviruses. Multinucleated giant cells with nuclear molding and chromatin margination occur in HSV-1, HSV-2 and VZV alike, so the smear tells you “herpesvirus” and stops there. It cannot type HSV, which matters for prognosis and counseling in genital disease; it cannot separate zoster from a zosteriform HSV recurrence, which matters for infection control and for whether you consider immunosuppression; and its sensitivity depends heavily on lesion age and on the person making the slide. It remains a defensible bedside adjunct when a result in minutes would change your immediate action — it is not a substitute for PCR.

A patient asks why their child's wart cannot simply be “cultured and typed like a bacterial infection.”

Because human papillomavirus completes its life cycle only inside a differentiating stratified squamous epithelium. It has no free-living replicative stage that a broth or an agar plate or a standard cell monolayer can support, so there is no clinical culture and there are no susceptibility breakpoints — the concepts do not transfer from bacteriology. Nucleic acid detection and typing assays do exist, but they are validated and clinically actionable on anogenital and cervical screening specimens where the result changes cancer-prevention management. On a plantar wart, a type result changes nothing you would do. The diagnosis is clinical, and histopathology is reserved for lesions that are atypical, refractory, or suspicious for carcinoma.

A returning traveler and abattoir worker has two painless nodules on the index finger, three weeks old, with a red center and pale ring. The referral asks about incision and drainage.

This is orf until proven otherwise, and the target-like stage fits the timeline. Take the exposure history explicitly — sheep or goats, shearing, slaughter, or preparation of an animal for a religious festival — because the history carries more diagnostic weight than the morphology. Confirmation is optional and, if wanted, is parapoxvirus PCR on a swab, crust or biopsy at a reference laboratory, or electron microscopy; there is no routine culture. Decline incision and drainage. The lesion is self-limiting over roughly six weeks, surgery scars worse than the natural history, and manipulation risks inoculating the operator. Dress the lesion, advise glove use for animal handling, and warn about a possible erythema multiforme reaction weeks later so it is not misattributed to a drug.

What do you actually say when you telephone the virology laboratory?

Give them five things and you will almost never get the wrong test. First, the specific organism or organisms you are asking about, not “a viral screen.” Second, the specimen you have or intend to take, and the age of the lesion. Third, whether the patient is immunocompromised, pregnant, or has a hemolytic anemia — each changes which assay is valid. Fourth, whether this is a notifiable or high-consequence pathogen, so the specimen is handled at the right containment level and public health is engaged. Fifth, what you will do differently depending on the result, which lets the laboratory tell you whether the turnaround makes that plan realistic. Then ask two questions back: which transport medium and swab type their assay is validated on, and whether the multiplex panel they will run actually includes your target.

Why it matters

Across this whole section, three habits do most of the work. Sample the youngest lesion and sample it properly, because no assay recovers from a bad swab. Split the specimen whenever infection and neoplasia are both on the list, because formalin closes the door on culture. And name the pathogen you are chasing when you speak to the laboratory, because the correct test is almost never the default one.

Infestations and parasitic disease

Scabies, lice, and the travel differentials

Infestations and parasitic skin disease

This is the corner of dermatology where the laboratory is least able to help you unless you tell it exactly what you want. There is no reflex panel for an itchy patient. Almost every diagnosis here is made by choosing the right lesion, sampling it the right way, and then naming the organism you expect — because the mount, the stain, the medium and the incubation temperature all differ by parasite, and most of them are not part of a routine workflow. What follows is organized around that interface: what the organism is doing in the skin, what specimen actually contains it, and what the bench can and cannot do once it arrives.

Why it matters

Three ideas carry most of the clinical weight in this section. First, a negative scraping never excludes scabies. Second, crusted scabies is not a severe rash — it is a transmission event, and treating it as a dermatologic problem alone is how ward and nursing-home outbreaks begin. Third, for leishmaniasis and for the migratory helminth tracks, the specimen you send and the temperature it is held at determine whether the diagnosis is ever made at all.

Scabies: the organism, and why the itch lags the infestation

Sarcoptes scabiei var. hominis is an obligate human mite. The fertilized female tunnels within the stratum corneum — she never enters the living epidermis — and advances a few millimeters a day, laying two to three eggs daily and depositing fecal pellets (scybala) behind her. Egg to adult takes roughly two weeks. An ordinary infestation is a remarkably small ecological event: on the order of ten to fifteen adult female mites on the entire body. That number explains nearly everything about how scabies is diagnosed and mis-diagnosed.

Mite biology you can use at the bedside

Sarcoptes scabiei var. hominis

Host specificity is near-absolute. Animal-derived Sarcoptes variants (dog sarcoptic mange) will bite a human and cause a pruritic papular eruption, but they cannot burrow or reproduce in human skin — so there are no burrows, no interdigital predilection, and the eruption is self-limited once the animal is treated. That distinction gets asked.

Site in skinStratum corneum only
Burrow advance~2 mm/day
Egg to adult~10–14 days
Typical mite burden10–15 females
Off-host survival~24–72 h

Off-host survival is longer in cool, humid conditions and shorter in warm dry air, which is why fomite transmission matters far more in crusted disease (enormous burden shed continuously) than in ordinary scabies.

Itch as a type IV response

The single most useful mechanistic fact here

Pruritus in scabies is not caused by the mite mechanically. It is a delayed-type hypersensitivity response to mite antigen, eggs and scybala, with an IgE-mediated component layered on top. Sensitization must occur first.

First infestation, itch onset3–6 weeks
Re-infestation, itch onset1–3 days

Two consequences follow directly. A contact who is currently asymptomatic may already be infested and infectious, which is the entire argument for treating all household and close contacts simultaneously rather than waiting for them to itch. And a patient who itches within forty-eight hours of an exposure has almost certainly met this mite before.

Advanced pearl

The same immunology explains nodular scabies. Persistent firm pruritic nodules on the scrotum, penile shaft, axillae or groin represent a hypersensitivity granuloma to retained antigen, not living mites. They can persist for months after cure and respond to intralesional or potent topical corticosteroid — not to more scabicide.

Where to look

Distribution is diagnostic

The burrow is the pathognomonic lesion: a thread-like, grey-white, linear or S-shaped tract two to ten millimeters long, often with a minute vesicle or dark speck at the leading end where the mite sits. Everything else — the widespread excoriated papules, eczematous change and secondary impetiginization — is host response and is not specific.

  • Finger web spaces and the sides of the fingers — the highest-yield site.
  • Flexor wrists, elbows, anterior axillary folds.
  • Periumbilical skin, waistband, buttocks.
  • Areolae and periareolar skin in women.
  • Penile shaft and scrotum in men — a burrow or nodule here is close to diagnostic.

In immunocompetent adults the head, neck, palms and soles are spared. In infants and young children they are not: palmoplantar vesiculopustules and scalp or facial involvement are typical, and the same is true in the elderly and the immunosuppressed. Do not talk yourself out of scabies in a baby because the face is involved.

Getting the specimen right

Mineral oil, not potassium hydroxide

Place a drop of mineral oil on the lesion, scrape firmly along the length of a burrow or across an intact non-excoriated papule with a number 15 blade held at an angle — enough to remove stratum corneum, to the point of pinpoint bleeding — then transfer oil and scale to a slide and coverslip. Read at low power.

MountMineral oil
Read at4× then 10×
Diagnostic findingMite, egg, eggshell, or scybala

Any one of these findings confirms. Mineral oil is preferred over potassium hydroxide because it keeps the mite and eggs intact and adherent, whereas alkali clears and dissolves scybala and lets material float off the slide. Use potassium hydroxide when the question is dermatophyte, not mite.

What to do next

Scrape several sites, not one. Choose non-excoriated lesions — the patient has usually already removed the mite from anything that looks impressive. In practice this preparation is made and read by dermatology at the point of care; many microbiology laboratories have no workflow for it, so do not assume sending scale in a container will produce an answer.

The reference bedside test. Specificity is essentially one hundred percent — a mite, an egg or a cluster of scybala in the oil mount ends the discussion. Sensitivity is the problem, and it is a sampling problem, not a laboratory problem: with roughly a dozen mites distributed over an entire body surface, a small number of scrapes will frequently sample uninfested skin.

Yield rises when you scrape burrows rather than papules, scrape multiple sites, and scrape lesions the patient has not excoriated. Scybala are often the easiest positive finding — small oval brown pellets, usually in clumps, trailing behind the mite in the tract. Eggs are ovoid and refractile; hatched eggshell fragments retain a characteristic curled outline.

Dermoscopy has become the practical first-line confirmatory step because it is non-invasive, repeatable, and lets you select the exact burrow to scrape. The classic finding is a small dark triangular structure at the end of a faint linear tract — the pigmented anterior mouthparts and forelegs of the mite, described as the delta-wing jet, with the burrow behind it as the contrail. It is also called the hang-glider or jet-with-contrail sign.

Yield depends heavily on magnification, on operator experience, and on skin tone: the dark triangle is harder to appreciate on richly pigmented skin, and dermoscopy is less reliable when lesions are heavily excoriated or eczematized. Use it to target the scrape rather than to replace it.

The burrow ink test — ink applied to a suspicious lesion, then wiped with alcohol, leaving the tract outlined — is a simple bedside adjunct, particularly useful in children and in teaching settings. Adhesive tape stripping of lesions and examination of the tape can capture mites and is more practical in an uncooperative patient than blade scraping. Reflectance confocal microscopy visualizes mites in vivo but is confined to centers that have it.

Polymerase chain reaction assays for Sarcoptes deoxyribonucleic acid on scrapings or tape lifts exist and are more sensitive than microscopy, but they are not standardized and are not offered by most clinical laboratories. Availability is genuinely institution- and region-dependent; ask your laboratory before you promise a patient a test. Histopathology (as in the figure above) is a bystander diagnosis — useful when a biopsy was taken for another reason, not something to order deliberately for suspected scabies.

The 2020 International Alliance for the Control of Scabies consensus criteria formalize what most clinicians already do: a tier of confirmed scabies (organism visualized), clinical scabies (burrows, or typical male genital lesions, or typical distribution in two of the classic sites plus a suggestive history), and suspected scabies. The framework exists precisely because organism visualization cannot be required.

Pitfall

A negative skin scraping does not exclude scabies, and never has. With roughly ten to fifteen mites on an entire body, a negative preparation is far more likely to reflect where you scraped than whether the patient is infested. Scabies remains a clinical diagnosis supported — not gated — by microscopy. Treating on strong clinical grounds after a negative scrape is correct practice, and withholding treatment because “the scraping was negative” is a recognized source of prolonged household transmission. Two other traps sit alongside this one: prior topical corticosteroid produces scabies incognito, in which burrows are effaced and the eruption looks like eczema while the mite burden climbs; and an itchy patient with a bland examination should still prompt you to look at the finger webs and genitalia. When the examination and repeated scrapings are genuinely negative, the leading alternatives are biting arthropods rather than an infestation of the skin itself: Cimex lectularius (bed bugs), producing grouped or linear “breakfast, lunch and dinner” papules on exposed skin with a travel or new-mattress history; Cheyletiella and other animal mites acquired from a pet, where the animal is infested and the human is not; and chiggers after outdoor exposure. None of these burrow, none of them will ever produce a positive scraping, and all of them are diagnosed by history and by finding the source — which is why they are the ones that get treated with repeated scabicide instead., because the specific lesions are small and easily missed.

Crusted (Norwegian) scabies: a public-health problem wearing a dermatologic costume

Crusted scabies is the same organism with the host response removed. Where an ordinary infestation carries a dozen mites, crusted scabies carries thousands to millions, because a defective or blunted cell-mediated response — or simply an inability to scratch — permits unchecked proliferation within hyperkeratotic scale. The clinical picture inverts your expectations, and so does the infection-control picture.

Recognizing it

Often not itchy — that is the trap

Thick, adherent, fissured hyperkeratotic crusts, frequently on hands, feet, elbows and scalp, with gross subungual and periungual debris that is packed with mites. Erythroderma is a recognized presentation. Pruritus is mild or absent in a substantial proportion of patients, which is exactly why the diagnosis is delayed and why the eruption is repeatedly treated as psoriasis, eczema, an adverse drug eruption or seborrheic dermatitis.

The corollary is a gift diagnostically: because mite density is enormous, a scraping is almost always positive, often with numerous mites and eggs in a single field. If you suspect crusted scabies, the microscopy will usually reward you immediately.

Mite burden10³–10&sup6;
PruritusOften mild or absent
Scraping yieldVery high

Who gets it

A host-defense and behavior list
  • Human immunodeficiency virus infection, and human T-lymphotropic virus type 1 infection — the latter is a classic and geographically concentrated association.
  • Systemic corticosteroids, other immunosuppressants, and transplant recipients; also potent topical steroid applied to undiagnosed ordinary scabies.
  • Hematologic malignancy.
  • Neurologic or cognitive impairment, sensory neuropathy, paralysis, dementia — anyone who cannot perceive itch or cannot scratch.
  • Down syndrome.
  • Advanced age, frailty, and residence in long-term care.
  • Crowded and resource-limited settings where scabies is hyperendemic.

Note that two mechanistically distinct routes converge: genuine immunosuppression, and mechanical failure to scratch. The second is easy to forget on rounds.

Why it matters

A patient with crusted scabies sheds mite-laden scale continuously into bedding, clothing, furniture and onto the hands and sleeves of everyone who touches them. Transmission requires only brief contact or a fomite, unlike ordinary scabies which generally needs prolonged skin-to-skin contact. A single unrecognized case in a nursing home, rehabilitation unit or hospital ward reliably seeds an outbreak among residents, staff and visitors, and the index case is frequently identified only in retrospect after multiple staff members present with ordinary scabies. Secondary bacterial infection through fissured skin is the other danger: Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pyogenes invade readily, and bacteremia and sepsis are the usual mechanism of death. In populations where scabies is hyperendemic, the downstream burden of streptococcal skin infection includes acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis. Crusted scabies is therefore an infection-prevention notification, not merely a prescription.

Managing crusted scabies: the five things that must all happen

  • Combined therapy. Topical scabicide plus repeated oral therapy. Topical alone is inadequate because scabicide cannot penetrate thick crust, and oral alone is inadequate for the same reason in reverse — drug reaches the mite poorly within dense keratin. Repeat dosing of the oral agent is required because avermectin-class drugs are not ovicidal, so eggs surviving the first dose must be caught after they hatch.
  • Keratolysis. Softening and physically debulking crust so the scabicide can reach the mites. Skipping this step is the commonest reason for apparent treatment failure.
  • Contact precautions and cohorting until treatment is complete and the patient is assessed as no longer infested. Gowns and gloves, dedicated equipment, careful handling of linen.
  • Contact tracing. Household members, all care staff, other residents on the unit, therapists, and visitors — treated simultaneously and regardless of symptoms, because the incubation logic above means asymptomatic contacts are still infested and still transmitting.
  • Environmental measures. Hot-water laundering and hot drying, or sealing non-washable items for a period exceeding mite survival; vacuuming; attention to mattresses and upholstered furniture. Environmental measures matter far more here than in ordinary scabies.

Specific agents, dosing intervals and the number of oral doses vary by protocol, by regulatory setting and by patient factors including weight, pregnancy and age; scabicide resistance has been reported in several regions and influences local choice. Follow your institution’s and your region’s current guidance rather than a remembered regimen. The principles above — combined therapy, keratolysis, precautions, simultaneous contact treatment, environment — do not vary.

Do not confuse

Post-scabetic itch is not treatment failure. Because the pruritus is a hypersensitivity response to antigen that remains in the stratum corneum until it is shed, itch routinely persists for two to four weeks after every mite has been killed, and occasionally longer. This is expected and it is not a reason to re-treat. Overtreatment is the commonest error in scabies management and it is not harmless: repeated scabicide application produces an irritant and sometimes allergic contact dermatitis that itches more, which the patient and the clinician then interpret as ongoing infestation, and a cycle begins that can end in fixed delusional infestation.

Post-scabetic itch versus genuine treatment failure

What actually distinguishes them is new lesions, not persistent symptoms. Ask for and look for these specific findings:

  • Suggests failure or re-infestation: new burrows appearing after treatment; new inflammatory papules in classic sites; a previously well household contact who has newly begun to itch; a positive scraping on repeat sampling; incomplete or incorrect application (missed web spaces, subungual skin, or washing hands after application).
  • Suggests post-scabetic itch: steadily improving rather than worsening pruritus; no new lesions; excoriations and eczematous change in previously involved sites; a normal repeat examination of the finger webs and genitalia.
  • Suggests iatrogenic dermatitis: burning and stinging with application; a diffuse xerotic or eczematous eruption in the distribution of the scabicide rather than in classic scabies sites; escalating symptom severity with each additional course.

Manage expected post-scabetic itch with emollients, a topical corticosteroid, and a sedating antihistamine at night for sleep — and, critically, by telling the patient in advance that this will happen, so that they do not interpret it as failure and self-treat.

Pediculosis: three lice, three completely different clinical problems

The head louse and the body louse are morphologically near-identical ecotypes of Pediculus humanus; the pubic or crab louse, Pthirus pubis, is a different genus with a short broad body and powerful claws adapted to widely spaced coarse hair. What separates them clinically is not appearance but where they live — and in the case of the body louse, that it does not live on the body at all.

LouseWhere it livesWhat confirms active infestationSpecimen for the laboratoryVector role
Head louse
Pediculus humanus capitis		 Scalp hair; spread by direct head-to-head contact. Not a marker of poor hygiene. Only a live, moving louse confirms active infestation. Nits cemented within roughly a centimeter of the scalp raise suspicion but are not confirmatory. The louse or nit-bearing hairs, dry or in alcohol, for morphologic identification. There is no swab and no culture. none Not an established vector of human disease.
Body louse
Pediculus humanus (corporis)		 Clothing seams — it feeds on the body and returns to clothing to rest and lay eggs. Lice and eggs found in the seams of the garment. The skin shows only excoriations and post-inflammatory change. Examine the clothing. Send lice recovered from seams. Examining the patient’s skin alone will miss the diagnosis. vector Rickettsia prowazekii, Bartonella quintana, Borrelia recurrentis.
Pubic louse
Pthirus pubis		 Coarse hair — pubic and perianal, and also axillary, chest, beard, eyelashes and eyebrows. Lice or nits on coarse hairs; maculae ceruleae (slate-blue macules) at feeding sites support the diagnosis. Louse or nit-bearing hair for identification. none But a marker of sexual contact — screen for other sexually transmitted infections.

Head lice: nit distance and the diagnosis of “active”

A treatment-decision problem

Nits are cemented to the hair shaft and remain there after hatching. Because scalp hair grows at roughly one centimeter per month, nits found well out along the shaft are typically old, hatched, or nonviable, and represent past rather than current infestation. Nits close to the scalp are the ones that suggest current activity. Exact thresholds differ between sources and between school policies — treat the principle, not a memorized millimeter figure — but the clinical rule is stable: only a live louse definitively confirms active infestation.

Detection by wet combing — conditioner applied, then systematic combing with a fine-toothed nit comb onto a white surface — is substantially more sensitive than dry visual inspection and is the practical way to find live lice.

Pitfall

Treating on the basis of distal nits alone leads to repeated unnecessary pediculicide exposure and drives resistance, which is already widespread for pyrethroid-class agents in many regions. Hair casts, seborrheic scale, hair-product residue and peripilar keratin casts are all mistaken for nits; they slide freely along the shaft, whereas a nit is cemented and resists being moved.

Body lice: the highest-yield vector association in dermatology

Three pathogens, three different specimens

Body lice are a disease of crowding, displacement, homelessness, conflict, cold climates and the inability to launder or change clothing — not of the skin itself. The cutaneous picture is nonspecific: excoriated papules over the trunk, shoulders and waist, with lichenification and post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation from chronic scratching in long-standing cases. The organism is in the garment.

Rickettsia prowazekiiEpidemic typhus
Bartonella quintanaTrench fever
Borrelia recurrentisRelapsing fever

Each is transmitted by contamination rather than by the bite itself, which is why scratching is part of the transmission mechanism — but the vehicle differs: Rickettsia prowazekii and Bartonella quintana are shed in louse feces and rubbed into excoriated skin or mucosa, whereas Borrelia recurrentis lives in the louse haemolymph, is not present in the feces, and is transmitted only when the louse is crushed against the skin, which is why scratching is part of the transmission mechanism.

Advanced pearl

Recrudescent epidemic typhus — Brill-Zinsser disease — can emerge years or decades after primary infection when immunity wanes, in a patient with no current louse exposure at all. In the United States a sylvatic reservoir of R. prowazekii exists in flying squirrels and their ectoparasites, producing sporadic cases without any body-louse history.

What to do next

If you suspect a louse-borne infection, the specimen differs by organism and the laboratory needs warning for all three. Rickettsia prowazekii is not recoverable by routine culture — it requires cell culture in a high-containment laboratory — so diagnosis rests on serology, on molecular testing, and on immunohistochemistry of skin biopsy in some centers; alert the laboratory rather than sending a blood culture and waiting. Bartonella quintana is fastidious and slow, so a standard five-day blood culture protocol will be reported as negative; if you want it, say so, because prolonged incubation, lysis-centrifugation methods, serology or molecular testing are what will find it, and it is a genuine cause of culture-negative endocarditis. Borrelia recurrentis is the one that is easy if the timing is right: spirochetes are visible on a Giemsa- or Wright-stained peripheral blood smear or by dark-field microscopy during a febrile episode, and are absent between relapses. Draw the smear while the patient is spiking, not the next morning.

Cutaneous larva migrans: the dead-end host

Animal hookworm larvae — most often Ancylostoma braziliense, also A. caninum and related species of dogs and cats — penetrate human skin from sand or soil contaminated with animal feces. In their natural host the larvae penetrate the basement membrane, enter the circulation and complete a pulmonary and intestinal migration to adulthood. In humans they cannot: they lack the enzymatic machinery to breach the basement membrane, so they remain trapped within the epidermis, wandering aimlessly until they die.

The clinical picture and why the disease self-limits
