Humans are an accidental host

An intensely pruritic, raised, erythematous, serpiginous track advancing perceptibly — the patient can often tell you it moved since yesterday. Vesiculobullous and eczematous variants occur, and secondary bacterial infection from scratching is common. Sites follow contact with contaminated sand or soil: feet and interdigital spaces, buttocks and posterior thighs from sitting, and the trunk in someone who lay on a beach. The exposure history that cracks the case is walking barefoot, sunbathing, or sitting on sand in the tropics or subtropics, including the Caribbean, Southeast Asia, sub-Saharan Africa, South and Central America and the southeastern United States.

Track advancemm to ~2 cm/day
Natural courseSelf-limited, weeks to months

Because the parasite is in the wrong host and cannot mature or reproduce, the infestation resolves when the larvae die. Antihelminthic therapy — benzimidazole-class agents, or ivermectin — is given to shorten a miserable and prolonged course rather than to prevent progression.

What the laboratory cannot do for you

This is a clinical diagnosis

Understanding the dead-end biology tells you exactly which tests will be useless, and this is a favorite examination point.

  • Do not send stool for ova and parasites. There is no adult worm in the human gut, so there are no eggs to find. A negative result is expected and reassures no one.
  • Biopsy is usually unrewarding. The larva sits ahead of the visible track, often by a centimeter or more, so a biopsy taken at the leading edge of the erythema typically samples empty tissue and shows only a nonspecific eosinophilic infiltrate.
  • Serology is not part of routine practice for animal hookworm larva migrans.
  • Peripheral eosinophilia may be present but is frequently absent; its absence does not argue against the diagnosis.

Pulmonary eosinophilia (Löffler syndrome) is a recognized though uncommon accompaniment, and A. caninum is additionally associated with eosinophilic enteritis, so a persistently symptomatic patient warrants a wider look.

Do not confuse

Larva currens is not cutaneous larva migrans, and the distinction is urgent. Larva currens is the cutaneous manifestation of autoinfection by Strongyloides stercoralis. It differs on three axes. Speed: the track advances on the order of centimeters per hour and can be watched moving over minutes, whereas an animal hookworm track advances millimeters to a couple of centimeters per day. Duration and character: larva currens is an urticarial, evanescent wheal-like track that appears and disappears within hours and recurs episodically over years, whereas cutaneous larva migrans is a persistent fixed inflammatory track. Location: larva currens arises perianally and spreads to buttocks, groin, thighs and trunk, reflecting larvae re-penetrating the perianal skin from the gut. And the laboratory advice reverses completely — here you do want stool examination, agar plate culture, and serology, because Strongyloides is the one diagnosis in this section where missing it can kill the patient: corticosteroids or other immunosuppression can precipitate hyperinfection and disseminated strongyloidiasis with polymicrobial gram-negative bacteremia and meningitis from enteric organisms carried on migrating larvae. A serpiginous eruption in a patient about to be immunosuppressed is a stop-and-think moment.

1
A migrating or serpiginous track

Establish the migration rate first — mark the leading edge with a pen and re-examine. Rate, not appearance, is the primary discriminator.

Slow, fixed, feet or buttocks

Cutaneous larva migrans. Sand or soil exposure in an endemic area. Clinical diagnosis; no stool, no serology. Antihelminthic therapy to shorten the course; treat secondary bacterial infection if present.

Fast, evanescent, perianal

Larva currens. Investigate for Strongyloides stercoralis with stool examination, agar plate culture and serology — and screen before any planned immunosuppression.

2
Neither pattern fits

Consider migratory panniculitis and migratory swellings with marked eosinophilia after freshwater fish or undercooked meat consumption (gnathostomiasis), and consider non-parasitic mimics: phytophotodermatitis, factitial lesions, and linear inflammatory dermatoses that are static on re-examination.

Cutaneous leishmaniasis: the diagnosis that depends on how you talk to the laboratory

Leishmaniasis is transmitted by the bite of the female phlebotomine sandfly — genus Phlebotomus in the Old World, Lutzomyia in the New World. The fly inoculates flagellated promastigotes, which are phagocytosed by dermal macrophages and transform into aflagellate amastigotes that replicate within the phagolysosome. That intracellular amastigote, with its nucleus and its distinctive rod-shaped kinetoplast, is what you are looking for on the slide.

The evolution of the lesion

Painless is the discriminator

A small painless papule appears at the bite site after an incubation of weeks to months, enlarges to a nodule, then ulcerates, leaving a shallow ulcer with a raised indurated rolled border and a clean granulating base — often described as the volcano sign. Lesions sit on exposed skin: face, ears, forearms, dorsal hands, lower legs. Regional adenopathy and sporotrichoid nodules tracking along lymphatics occur and do not exclude the diagnosis.

The lesion is characteristically painless unless secondarily infected — a useful separator from bacterial pyoderma and from a neglected ulcerated pyogenic process, which usually hurt. It also fails to respond to antibacterial therapy, and a chronic non-healing ulcer in a returning traveler that has already failed two antibiotic courses should raise this diagnosis immediately.

Clinical pattern

Chronic, painless, indolent ulcer with a rolled border, on exposed skin, in a patient who has been in an endemic area — military deployment, ecotourism, jungle or desert fieldwork, migration, or residence. Ask specifically about overnight exposure, because sandflies feed around dusk and at night and are small enough to pass through standard mosquito netting.

Sampling: never a surface swab

The single most common laboratory error here

Parasites are concentrated in the active raised border, within dermal macrophages. The necrotic exudate at the ulcer base contains few organisms and abundant secondary bacterial colonizers, so a swab of the ulcer floor produces a report of mixed skin flora and no diagnosis.

  • Clean and debride the surface first, then sample the indurated border.
  • Slit-skin smear or scraping of the border, with touch preparations made onto slides.
  • Punch biopsy through the active edge — divide the tissue: one portion in formalin for histopathology, one fresh and sterile for culture and molecular testing.
  • Needle aspirate of the border after injecting a small volume of sterile saline is an alternative in some centers.
Pitfall

Putting the entire specimen in formalin destroys any chance of culture and compromises the yield of species-level molecular work. Decide before the blade touches the skin how the tissue will be divided, and call the laboratory first — both the medium and the incubator settings have to be arranged in advance.

MethodWhat it detectsPractical notesSpeciates?
Giemsa-stained smear or touch preparation Amastigotes within macrophages — small round-to-oval bodies roughly 2–5 micrometers, each with a nucleus and an adjacent rod-shaped kinetoplast. Fast, cheap, available almost anywhere. The kinetoplast is the feature that separates amastigotes from Histoplasma yeasts and from artifact. Yield falls as lesions become chronic and parasite density drops. no
Culture Promastigotes emerging in vitro from inoculated tissue. Novy-MacNeal-Nicolle or comparable biphasic media, or Schneider’s insect medium with serum. Incubate at insect-vector temperature, around 22–26 °C — not at 35–37 °C. Hold up to about four weeks. Requires explicit arrangement with the laboratory. isolate Yields an isolate for typing
Polymerase chain reaction Leishmanial deoxyribonucleic acid — commonly kinetoplast minicircle targets or ribosomal internal transcribed spacer regions. The most sensitive method, and the one that works when smears are negative in chronic pauciparasitic lesions. Often a send-out; in the United States species identification is performed at reference level including by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Availability is region-dependent. yes The reason to order it
Histopathology Granulomatous or mixed dermal infiltrate; amastigotes within histiocytes, sometimes arrayed along the cell membrane. Useful for excluding mimics — deep fungal infection, mycobacterial disease, lymphoma, squamous cell carcinoma. Organism detection is insensitive in chronic lesions. no
Serology / leishmanin skin test Host response rather than organism. Serology performs poorly in purely cutaneous disease and is not a substitute for organism detection. The leishmanin (Montenegro) skin test is largely historical and is unavailable in many countries. no

Vector Phlebotomus. Distribution spans the Mediterranean basin, the Middle East, Central and South Asia, North and East Africa. Principal species include Leishmania major, which classically produces a rapidly ulcerating exudative “wet” lesion in rural zoonotic settings with rodent reservoirs; L. tropica, an often anthroponotic urban species producing a drier, more chronic, slowly evolving lesion; L. aethiopica, associated with diffuse cutaneous leishmaniasis in anergic hosts; and L. infantum, which also causes visceral disease.

Many uncomplicated Old World lesions caused by low-risk species are self-limited over months and can be managed with local therapy — cryotherapy, controlled local heat, intralesional pentavalent antimonial injection, or topical paromomycin-based preparations — provided lesions are few, small, and not on cosmetically or functionally critical sites.

Vector Lutzomyia. Distribution spans Mexico, Central America and much of South America. The critical division is between the Leishmania mexicana complex and the subgenus Viannia — notably the L. (V.) braziliensis complex, along with L. (V.) panamensis and L. (V.) guyanensis.

The Viannia species carry a risk of mucosal leishmaniasis (espundia): metastatic spread to the nasal, oral and pharyngeal mucosa, which can appear months to years after the primary cutaneous lesion has healed, and which causes progressive destruction of the nasal septum, palate and upper airway. This late, disfiguring, potentially airway-threatening complication is the entire reason species identification matters.

Chiclero ulcer — a chronic destructive lesion of the pinna in forestry and chicle workers in Central America — is the classic L. mexicana complex presentation and a recognizable board image.

Species identification is not academic. It is what determines whether a patient needs systemic therapy for a lesion that would otherwise be watched or treated locally, because the goal of systemic therapy in Viannia infection is prevention of later mucosal disease rather than healing the skin lesion in front of you.

In broad terms, systemic therapy is favored when species carries mucosal risk; when lesions are numerous, large, or over a joint; when they involve the face, ears, digits or genitalia; when there is lymphatic or sporotrichoid spread; when the host is immunosuppressed; and when local therapy has failed. Local therapy is reasonable for small, few, uncomplicated lesions caused by species without mucosal risk in a competent host.

Agent selection among pentavalent antimonials, liposomal amphotericin B, miltefosine and other options is species-, region- and host-dependent, and drug availability varies substantially between countries — in the United States, access to pentavalent antimonial therapy has historically been through a national public-health mechanism rather than routine pharmacy supply. Confirm current species-specific recommendations and local availability rather than relying on a remembered regimen; the principle to carry is that the polymerase chain reaction result changes the treatment plan.

Myiasis, tungiasis, and Demodex

Three infestations that are diagnosed almost entirely by pattern recognition plus a single specific question, and where the wrong reflex — antibiotics, incision and drainage, or another course of topical antibiotic for rosacea — is the usual reason for delay.

Furuncular myiasis

Dermatobia hominis, Cordylobia anthropophaga

A furuncle-like nodule with a central punctum that intermittently discharges serosanguineous fluid, and which the patient describes as painful, throbbing, or — the giveaway — as though something inside is moving. The larva breathes through that punctum, and its posterior spiracles are sometimes visible if you watch. Cultures are sterile or grow only contaminants, and antibiotics do not work.

Dermatobia hominisCentral & South America
Cordylobia anthropophagaSub-Saharan Africa

The transmission biology is worth knowing because it generates the prevention advice. The human botfly uses phoresis: it captures a mosquito or other flying arthropod, glues eggs to it, and releases it — larvae hatch and enter the skin when the carrier next feeds on a human. The tumbu fly instead lays eggs on soil or on damp laundry left to dry outdoors, so the classic preventive counsel is to dry clothing indoors or to iron it, which kills the eggs.

Management is occlusion of the punctum to force the larva toward the surface, then manual or surgical extraction; avoid rupturing the larva in situ, which provokes an intense foreign-body inflammatory reaction.

Tungiasis

Tunga penetrans, the sand flea

The gravid female flea burrows into the epidermis and remains there, enlarging as her abdomen fills with eggs. The result is a firm white or yellowish papule with a central black dot — the exposed posterior end of the flea — usually periungual or subungual on the toes, on the soles, or at the heel, and often multiple. Lesions itch and become painful as they enlarge, and egg extrusion may be visible.

ExposureBarefoot, sandy soil
EndemicLatin America, sub-Saharan Africa, Caribbean

Management is complete extraction of the flea under sterile conditions with an intact removal, followed by wound care. Two things routinely get forgotten: heavy infestation causes real morbidity through secondary bacterial infection, ulceration and deformity, and tetanus immunization status must be checked in a patient with penetrating foot lesions acquired in soil.

Demodex

D. folliculorum, D. brevis

These are commensal mites of the human pilosebaceous unit — D. folliculorum in the follicular infundibulum, D. brevis deeper in sebaceous glands — present in most adults. Finding them is therefore not a diagnosis; density and the host response are what matter.

Increased density is implicated in papulopustular rosacea and in the rosacea-like eruptions that respond to acaricidal rather than antibacterial therapy. Pityriasis folliculorum presents as diffuse follicular scale with a rough sandpaper texture and background erythema, often in women who over-cleanse or avoid moisturizer. Demodex blepharitis produces cylindrical dandruff at the lash bases.

In the immunosuppressed — human immunodeficiency virus infection, transplant recipients, hematologic malignancy, and patients on prolonged topical or systemic corticosteroid — florid demodicosis occurs, with dense follicular papulopustules and very high mite counts.

Student pearl

Quantification is done by standardized skin surface biopsy using cyanoacrylate adhesive on a slide, or by scraping with a mount. Counts above roughly five mites per square centimeter are conventionally regarded as increased, but thresholds and technique vary between laboratories — interpret the number against the clinical picture, not in isolation.

Pitfall

A “furuncle” that does not respond to appropriate antistaphylococcal therapy, and whose culture is sterile or grows only skin flora, should not be met with a broader antibiotic. Re-take the history for travel. Furuncular myiasis, tungiasis, a ruptured epidermal cyst, hidradenitis, and inoculation-site deep fungal or mycobacterial infection all masquerade as recurrent boils, and every one of them is diagnosed by asking a different question rather than by escalating antibacterial coverage. Related trap: incising a myiasis lesion blindly and fragmenting the larva leaves retained chitinous material and a persistent granulomatous nodule.

Travel and exposure panel

In parasitic skin disease the history does more work than any test. Ask where, when, what activity, what contact, and how long ago — incubation periods here range from days to more than a year.

ExposureThink ofLesion clueWhat to send, and what to say

Immunosuppression, transplant, prolonged corticosteroid Demodicosis; crusted scabies; disseminated strongyloidiasis Dense follicular papulopustules; hyperkeratotic crusts; petechial or purpuric periumbilical eruption in hyperinfection Cyanoacrylate surface biopsy for mite density; scraping for Sarcoptes; stool, sputum and serology for Strongyloides — and screen before immunosuppressing.
How do I actually phrase the request when I call the microbiology laboratory?

Say the organism you suspect, the specimen you are about to take, and the question you want answered. Most failures in this section come from a requisition that says “skin, culture” and nothing else.

  • Leishmaniasis: “I am biopsying the active border of a chronic ulcer in a returning traveler for suspected cutaneous leishmaniasis. I need fresh sterile tissue for culture at reduced temperature held for four weeks, touch preparations for Giemsa, and molecular testing with species identification — can you take it, or does it need to go to a reference laboratory?” The reduced-temperature, prolonged-hold requirement is the part a routine bacteriology bench will not do unless asked.
  • Lice: send the organism, not a swab. Identification is morphologic. Lice and nit-bearing hairs go dry or in alcohol to the parasitology bench.
  • Myiasis: send the intact larva. Entomologic identification depends on preserved surface structures, so formalin is generally not the fixative of choice — confirm the preferred method locally before you drop it in a pot.
  • Scabies: expect to make and read the preparation yourself. Ask whether your laboratory offers a molecular assay before you tell a patient one exists.
  • Louse-borne febrile illness: name the organism. Standard blood culture protocols will miss Bartonella quintana, and Rickettsia prowazekii is not something a routine laboratory will attempt to culture at all.
Why does Leishmania culture require a lower incubation temperature?

Because the stage that grows in vitro is the promastigote — the form that lives in the sandfly. Temperature is one of the principal triggers of stage differentiation in this parasite: within a mammalian macrophage at core body temperature the organism exists as the amastigote, while at the cooler temperature of the insect vector it reverts to and proliferates as the flagellated promastigote. Culture media such as Novy-MacNeal-Nicolle or Schneider’s insect medium therefore recreate vector conditions, incubated in the region of 22 to 26 degrees Celsius rather than at 35 to 37 degrees Celsius.

The practical consequence is the examinable one: a specimen incubated at standard bacteriology temperature will not yield the organism, and a laboratory that has not been forewarned will discard the culture long before four weeks have elapsed. This is the same principle that makes several other cutaneous pathogens temperature-dependent in the laboratory and, in some cases, cooler-body-site-dependent in vivo.

My patient with treated scabies is still itching at four weeks. Do I re-treat?

Not on the basis of itch alone. Work through four questions in order.

One: are there new lesions — new burrows, new inflammatory papules in classic sites — or only persistent symptoms in old sites? New lesions argue for failure or re-infestation; persistent symptoms alone do not.

Two: was the original treatment applied correctly? The classic misses are the web spaces, under the fingernails, the genitalia, the scalp in infants and the elderly, and hand-washing after application. Ask how it was applied, for how long, and whether a second application was given.

Three: were all household and close contacts treated at the same time, whether or not they itched? If a contact was left untreated, the patient has probably been re-infested, and re-treatment of the patient alone will fail again.

Four: is this now an iatrogenic dermatitis? Repeated scabicide produces an irritant dermatitis that itches, prompting more scabicide. Look for a burning quality, a distribution matching the applied area rather than classic scabies sites, and escalating rather than improving symptoms.

If the answers point to expected post-scabetic itch, treat the itch — emollients, topical corticosteroid, a sedating antihistamine at night — re-examine rather than re-prescribe, and set expectations explicitly. If they point to failure or re-infestation, confirm with a repeat scraping if you can, then re-treat the patient and every contact simultaneously.

Rapid-fire pairings worth having automatic
  • Burrow in a finger web → Sarcoptes scabiei var. hominis; mount in mineral oil, not potassium hydroxide.
  • Delta-wing jet with contrail on dermoscopy → the mite’s pigmented anterior end at the head of its burrow.
  • Hyperkeratotic crusts in a patient who does not itch → crusted scabies; call infection prevention before moving the patient.
  • Lice in clothing seams → Pediculus humanus body louse → epidemic typhus, trench fever, louse-borne relapsing fever.
  • Spirochetes on a blood smear during a fever spike in a person experiencing homelessness → Borrelia recurrentis; the smear is negative between relapses.
  • Slate-blue macules with lice on coarse hair → maculae ceruleae, Pthirus pubis; screen for other sexually transmitted infections. Eyelash involvement in a prepubertal child mandates a safeguarding evaluation.
  • Serpiginous track advancing millimeters per day on the foot after a beach holiday → animal hookworm larva migrans; do not send stool.
  • Urticarial track advancing centimeters per hour from the perianal skin → larva currens; investigate for Strongyloides before any immunosuppression.
  • Painless rolled-border ulcer in a returning traveler → cutaneous leishmaniasis; sample the border, request species identification.
  • Amastigote with nucleus plus kinetoplast inside a macrophage → Leishmania; the kinetoplast is what distinguishes it from intracellular yeasts.
  • Ulcer of the pinna in a Central American forestry worker → chiclero ulcer, L. mexicana complex.
  • Mucosal destruction years after a healed skin ulcer from South America → Leishmania (Viannia) braziliensis complex; this is why species matters.
  • Boil with a punctum that the patient says moves → furuncular myiasis; antibiotics will not help and the culture will be sterile.
  • White papule with a black dot on a toe after barefoot exposure → Tunga penetrans; extract intact and check tetanus status.
  • Rosacea-like papulopustules failing antibacterial therapy, or florid follicular pustules in a transplant recipient → think Demodex density and acaricidal therapy.

Throughout this section, drug availability, scabicide resistance patterns, molecular test availability, reference-laboratory referral pathways and public-health notification requirements are all region- and institution-dependent. The organism biology, the specimen choice, and the reasoning are portable; the regimens and the referral routes are not — check yours.

Exposures, hosts, and traps

The synthesis worth rereading before clinic

Most of the microbiology that changes a dermatology consult is decided before the specimen ever reaches the bench — in the exposure history you take, in the tissue you choose to send instead of a swab, and in the sentence you write on the requisition telling the lab what you are actually looking for. This closing section pulls the whole Derm Pearls thread together: the exposure-to-organism map, the sporotrichoid pattern, the immunocompromised host, the post-procedure infection, the tests not to order, and the traps that catch trainees on rounds.

The exposure-to-organism map

Cutaneous infection is one of the few areas of medicine where a single line of history genuinely narrows the organism list to two or three names. The reason is ecological: most of these organisms do not live on human skin. They live in water, soil, animals, or arthropod vectors, and they get into the dermis only through a specific, describable event. If you can name the event, you can usually name the shortlist — and, critically, you can tell the laboratory what to set up.

  • Ask about water, and specify: fresh, salt, brackish, chlorinated, or standing.
  • Ask about animals by species and by contact type — bite, scratch, handling carcasses, feeding, milking.
  • Ask about soil contact with a breach: thorns, splinters, barefoot walking, gardening, farm work.
  • Ask about procedures in the preceding three months, including the ones patients do not consider medical — tattoos, fillers, laser, pedicures, mesotherapy.
  • Ask about travel and residence, not just recent trips. Endemic mycoses can reactivate years later.
  • Ask about immune status last, because it reframes everything above.
Why it matters

Standard bacterial culture is set up at 35 to 37 °C, held two to five days, on media chosen for the organisms that cause ordinary pyogenic infection. Nearly every organism in the table below fails at least one of those conditions. If you do not tell the lab, the plate is discarded as no growth while the patient is on their third failed course of cephalexin.

ExposureOrganisms it should raiseLesion pattern that fitsWhat the laboratory actually needs
Fish tank, aquarium maintenance, fresh water, swimming pool abrasion Mycobacterium marinum; Aeromonas hydrophila M. marinum: indolent violaceous nodule on a finger or dorsal hand two to six weeks later, often with ascending satellites. Aeromonas: fast, sometimes necrotizing, after freshwater immersion of a wound. Deep punch or incisional biopsy for mycobacterial culture. Request incubation at 28–32 °C and holding for up to six weeks — M. marinum grows poorly or not at all at 37 °C. Aeromonas grows on routine aerobic media, but ask for full susceptibility testing because many isolates carry inducible beta-lactamases.
Warm salt or brackish water; raw or undercooked shellfish — especially with cirrhosis, hemochromatosis, or iron overload Vibrio vulnificus Rapidly expanding painful cellulitis with hemorrhagic bullae, usually on a lower limb, with sepsis out of proportion to the skin. Ingestion-acquired disease seeds the skin from the bloodstream; wound-acquired disease starts locally. Blood cultures plus bulla fluid and tissue. Tell the lab you suspect Vibrio: it is halophilic, and stool specimens need selective TCBS agar. This is a same-hour surgical and infectious diseases call, not a next-morning problem.
Hot tub, whirlpool, water slide, inadequately chlorinated pool Pseudomonas aeruginosa Pruritic follicular papulopustules in a swimsuit distribution one to two days after exposure; tender plantar nodules (“hot foot”) in children; also otitis externa and green nail syndrome in wet-work occupations. Usually none — hot tub folliculitis is self-limited and does not need culture or systemic antibiotics in an immunocompetent host. Culture only if the host is immunocompromised, the course is atypical, or you are considering ecthyma gangrenosum.
Cat bite or scratch; kitten contact Pasteurella multocida; Bartonella henselae; Sporothrix in endemic regions; rarely Francisella tularensis Pasteurella: intense cellulitis within hours of the bite, often with serosanguineous drainage. Bartonella: inoculation papule with tender regional lymphadenopathy one to three weeks later. Cat-transmitted sporotrichosis is a recognized zoonosis in parts of South America. Pasteurella: aspirate or deep tissue, aerobic and anaerobic. It grows on blood and chocolate agar but not on MacConkey. Bartonella: culture is impractical — use serology plus PCR on tissue or node aspirate, and Warthin-Starry on histology.
Dog bite Pasteurella species; Capnocytophaga canimorsus; staphylococci, streptococci, oral anaerobes Capnocytophaga is the one to fear in an asplenic, cirrhotic, or heavily alcohol-using patient: fulminant sepsis with purpura fulminans and acral necrosis, sometimes from a bite that looked trivial. Blood cultures, and tell the lab — Capnocytophaga is a fastidious, capnophilic, slow-growing fusiform Gram-negative rod that may need extended incubation. In fulminant cases a buffy-coat Gram stain can show the organism the same day.
Human bite; clenched-fist injury over a knuckle Eikenella corrodens; viridans streptococci; oral anaerobes; Staphylococcus aureus A small wound over a metacarpophalangeal joint that the patient attributes to punching a wall, presenting late with disproportionate pain, tenosynovitis, or septic arthritis. Operative deep specimen, aerobic and anaerobic. Eikenella is capnophilic and pits the agar surface. Clinically the key point is that Eikenella and Pasteurella are both poorly covered by first-generation cephalosporins and by clindamycin, which is why beta-lactam plus beta-lactamase inhibitor coverage is the standard bite regimen. Every fight bite gets a hand surgery opinion.
Rose thorns, sphagnum moss, hay, straw, gardening; also cat contact in endemic areas Sporothrix schenckii complex Painless papule at the inoculation site that ulcerates, followed by a chain of subcutaneous nodules ascending the lymphatics. Tissue for fungal culture — this is the reference standard, because organisms are sparse on histology. Sporothrix is thermally dimorphic: a mold at 25–30 °C that converts to cigar-shaped budding yeast at 37 °C. Asteroid bodies on histology are suggestive but neither sensitive nor specific.
Sheep and goat handling; bottle-feeding lambs; home slaughter around a religious festival Orf virus (a parapoxvirus) Solitary, often targetoid nodule on a finger or hand that evolves through recognized stages over roughly six weeks and resolves without scarring. Erythema multiforme can follow. Usually a clinical diagnosis anchored on the exposure. PCR or electron microscopy of a lesional specimen confirms it if needed. The management point is negative: do not incise and drain, and do not treat it as a bacterial abscess.
Cattle, milking, contact with calves’ mouths or cows’ teats Pseudocowpox virus (milker’s nodule) — also a parapoxvirus Clinically near-identical to orf. The exposure history, not the morphology, separates them. Same as orf. Reassurance plus wound care; self-limited.
Barefoot walking or agricultural work on soil in the tropics and subtropics Mycetoma — eumycetoma (fungal, e.g. Madurella mycetomatis) or actinomycetoma (bacterial, e.g. Nocardia brasiliensis, Actinomadura, Streptomyces species); also Tunga penetrans Mycetoma triad: painless tumefaction, draining sinus tracts, and extruded grains, usually on a foot, over months to years. Tungiasis: periungual white papule with a central black dot on the toe. Collect the grains and deep tissue — not a sinus-tract swab. Grain color, Gram stain, and a modified acid-fast stain separate the filamentous bacteria from the fungi, and that separation determines whether the patient gets antibacterial therapy or prolonged antifungal therapy plus surgery.
Beach sand, moist sandy soil, crawl spaces contaminated with dog or cat feces Ancylostoma braziliense and related animal hookworms (cutaneous larva migrans) Intensely pruritic, serpiginous, slowly advancing erythematous track, most often on the foot, buttock, or trunk. Clinical diagnosis. Biopsy is usually unhelpful because the larva sits ahead of the visible track. Distinguish from larva currens of strongyloidiasis, which migrates far faster and centers on the perianal skin and trunk.
Sandfly bite in an endemic region — Middle East, North Africa, Central and South Asia, Latin America, southern Europe Leishmania species Painless chronic ulcer with a raised, indurated, rolled border at the site of a bite on exposed skin, present for weeks to months and failing antibacterials. Sample the active edge: slit-skin scraping or punch biopsy, with a Giemsa-stained touch preparation looking for amastigotes with their kinetoplast, plus culture and PCR. Species-level identification matters because New World species associated with mucosal disease change the treatment decision; in the United States, CDC provides reference testing.
Tick bite or wooded and grassy exposure Borrelia burgdorferi; Rickettsia rickettsii; Francisella tularensis Erythema migrans: expanding annular patch at the bite site. Rocky Mountain spotted fever: fever then a rash beginning on wrists and ankles, becoming petechial. Tularemia: a punched-out ulcer with tender regional nodes. Erythema migrans is a clinical diagnosis in an endemic area — serology is insensitive in the first weeks and a negative test does not exclude it. For spotted fever, treat empirically with a tetracycline; skin biopsy PCR or immunohistochemistry can confirm acutely, and serology is retrospective.
Rabbit or hare handling, deer fly bite, mowing over an animal carcass Francisella tularensis Ulceroglandular tularemia: inoculation ulcer plus impressive regional lymphadenopathy and systemic symptoms. Telephone the laboratory before the specimen leaves your clinic. Francisella is a documented cause of laboratory-acquired infection and requires containment practices; it is fastidious and needs cysteine-enriched media. A specimen worked up unannounced on an open bench exposes the technologists.
Hides, wool, animal hair, drum-making, livestock in endemic regions Bacillus anthracis Painless papule that vesiculates and evolves into a black eschar with dramatic surrounding non-pitting edema and regional adenopathy. The painlessness is the discriminator. Notify the laboratory and public health immediately. This is a select agent, a laboratory hazard, and a reportable condition; do not let a routine bench work it up blind.
Tattoo, permanent makeup, dermal filler, laser, liposuction, mesotherapy, nail salon footbath, leg shaving before a pedicure Rapidly growing non-tuberculous mycobacteria — M. fortuitum, M. chelonae, M. abscessus Papules, nodules, or sterile-looking abscesses appearing weeks after the procedure, failing standard antibacterials. In tattoos the lesions classically map to the gray-wash areas, where ink was diluted with non-sterile water. Biopsy for mycobacterial and fungal culture. Tell the lab. Identification is by MALDI-TOF or sequencing, and susceptibility testing by broth microdilution genuinely guides therapy for this group.
Butcher, fishmonger, fish or shellfish handler, veterinarian, abattoir worker Erysipelothrix rhusiopathiae (erysipeloid) Sharply marginated violaceous plaque on a hand or finger, burning rather than purulent, spreading slowly and classically sparing the terminal phalanx. No pus, no fluctuance. Full-thickness biopsy of the leading edge — a surface swab will not grow it, and aspiration is usually dry. The trap is therapeutic: Erysipelothrix is a Gram-positive rod that is intrinsically resistant to vancomycin, so empiric vancomycin for a “hand cellulitis” fails. It is penicillin-susceptible.
Freshwater leech therapy after flap or replant surgery Aeromonas species (gut symbionts of medicinal leeches) Wound infection or flap failure days after leech application. Deep tissue or aspirate, not a swab. Ask for full susceptibility testing; empiric coverage is usually chosen with beta-lactamase production in mind.
Chronic plant or thorn injury with slowly enlarging verrucous plaque, tropical and subtropical regions Fonsecaea, Cladophialophora, and other dematiaceous fungi (chromoblastomycosis); Nocardia Verrucous, cauliflower-like plaque with “black dots” of transepidermal elimination on the surface. Scrape the black dots and take a biopsy: muriform (sclerotic) bodies on direct microscopy or histology are diagnostic of chromoblastomycosis. Send tissue for fungal culture at routine mycology temperature (25–30 °C) and warn the lab that these dematiaceous fungi are slow growers, often needing several weeks.
Travel to or residence in an endemic region for the systemic mycoses Coccidioides (southwestern United States, parts of Mexico and Central and South America); Histoplasma (river valleys, caves, bird and bat guano); Blastomyces (Great Lakes, St. Lawrence and river valley regions); Paracoccidioides (Latin America); Talaromyces marneffei (Southeast Asia, southern China); Emergomyces (southern Africa) Coccidioidomycosis: erythema nodosum with pulmonary symptoms, or verrucous facial plaques in disseminated disease. Blastomycosis: verrucous plaque with peripheral pustules and central scarring that mimics squamous cell carcinoma. Talaromycosis and disseminated histoplasmosis: umbilicated molluscum-like papules in advanced HIV. Tissue for fungal culture and histology, plus antigen testing where available. Name the suspected organism on the requisition: the mold phases are laboratory hazards, and Coccidioides arthroconidia in particular are notorious for causing laboratory-acquired infection.
What to do next

Write the exposure on the requisition, not just the diagnosis. “Aquarium hobbyist, nodular lymphangitis of the right index finger, rule out M. marinum — please incubate at 30 °C and hold six weeks” is a request the laboratory can act on. “Rule out infection” is not.

Sporotrichoid spread: five organisms, one pattern

Nodular lymphangitis — an inoculation lesion with a chain of subcutaneous nodules ascending along the draining lymphatics — is the single most reliable pattern-recognition question in infectious dermatology. The morphology is nearly identical across the differential; the exposure history and the laboratory request are what separate them. All five of these require tissue, and none of them will be diagnosed from a surface swab.

Sporothrix schenckii complex

The eponymous one

Thermally dimorphic fungus of soil, decaying vegetation, sphagnum moss, and rose bushes. Also transmitted by cats in parts of South America, where scratches from infected cats have driven large zoonotic outbreaks.

ExposureGardening, moss, hay, thorns, cats
SpecimenPunch or incisional biopsy
Mold phase25–30 °C
Yeast phase37 °C
Therapy classTriazole (itraconazole) first line
Student pearl

Histology is often disappointing — the yeast are sparse and easily missed even with silver stains. Culture is the reference standard, which is exactly why the biopsy must not all go into formalin.

Mycobacterium marinum

The temperature-dependent one

Non-tuberculous mycobacterium of fresh and salt water, aquaria, and fish. Weeks of incubation, and a hand or forearm lesion in someone who cleans a fish tank without gloves.

ExposureAquarium, fish, water sports
SpecimenTissue for AFB culture
Incubation28–32 °C
Hold timeup to 6 weeks
Therapy classMultidrug, months
Advanced pearl

Temperature is a diagnostic variable in cutaneous mycobacteriology. M. marinum, M. ulcerans, and M. haemophilum all favor cooler incubation around 30 °C, matching their preference for cooler acral skin. M. haemophilum additionally requires iron-supplemented media — if you do not request it, the plate stays sterile.

Nocardia brasiliensis

The one that looks bacterial and behaves fungal

Aerobic actinomycete from soil. N. brasiliensis is the species most associated with primary cutaneous and lymphocutaneous disease in an otherwise healthy host; other species dominate in disseminated disease in the immunosuppressed.

ExposureSoil, thorn or splinter injury
StainBranching Gram-positive filaments
Key stainModified (partial) acid-fast
Hold platesup to 2–3 weeks
Therapy classSulfonamide backbone
Pitfall

Nocardia grows on routine bacteriology media but slowly. A standard plate discarded at 48 hours misses it. You must ask explicitly for prolonged incubation, and you should ask for species identification and susceptibility testing, because susceptibility varies meaningfully between species.

Leishmania species

The travel one

Usually a chronic ulcer, but a subset produces true nodular lymphangitis with subcutaneous nodules tracking proximally from the bite site.

ExposureSandfly bite, endemic travel
Sample fromActive raised edge, not the base
MicroscopyGiemsa touch prep, amastigotes
Best sensitivityPCR, with species identification
Why it matters

Species drives management. New World species in the L. braziliensis complex carry a risk of later mucosal disease, which pushes toward systemic rather than local therapy. “Leishmania seen” is not a complete answer.

Francisella tularensis

The one you must warn the lab about

Ulceroglandular tularemia can present with an inoculation ulcer and ascending nodes that mimic sporotrichoid spread, typically with fever and more systemic upset than the others.

ExposureTicks, deer flies, rabbits, hares
MediaCysteine-enriched
BiosafetyNotify lab before sending
ConfirmationOften serology or PCR at a reference lab
Pitfall

This is the one entry on the sporotrichoid list where failing to communicate with the laboratory can injure someone other than your patient. Francisella is highly infectious by the aerosol route and has repeatedly caused laboratory-acquired infection in technologists who handled an unlabeled plate on an open bench.

Do not confuse

Acute, febrile, rapidly ascending lymphangitis with a red streak and a hot tender limb is ordinary streptococcal or staphylococcal lymphangitis, and it belongs on antibiotics today. The sporotrichoid differential above is subacute to chronic: weeks of indolent nodules in a patient who is systemically well and has already failed a beta-lactam. Tempo is the first discriminator, before you ever get to exposure.

Why can I not just swab the draining nodule?

Three reasons, and they apply to almost every chronic cutaneous infection in this section.

First, these organisms live in the dermis and subcutis, not on the surface. A swab samples the epidermal surface and whatever colonizes it, so the yield for the true pathogen is poor even when the infection is unambiguous.

Second, a swab carries a vanishingly small volume of tissue. For organisms present in low numbers — mycobacteria, Sporothrix, Leishmania — the inoculum on a swab is often below what the culture can recover, and swab transport media are not designed to keep fastidious organisms viable for the weeks these cultures run.

Third, a swab grows skin flora, and skin flora grown from a chronically ulcerated lesion reads as a result. Someone will treat it. The single most common way a chronic cutaneous infection gets mismanaged is a surface swab returning coagulase-negative staphylococci or Pseudomonas, being treated as the cause, and the real diagnosis being delayed by months.

The correct move is a punch or incisional biopsy from the edge of an active lesion, divided: part in formalin for histology with special stains, and part sent fresh in sterile saline for bacterial, mycobacterial, and fungal culture. If you can only get one core, most laboratories would rather have it fresh — they can still make touch preparations and stains from fresh tissue, but they cannot culture formalin.

The immunocompromised host: skin as the window onto a disseminated infection

Immunosuppression changes the rules in four consistent ways. Presentations become less inflammatory, because the visible signs of infection are host-derived; they become atypical, because organisms that never invade normal skin now do; they become faster, because containment fails; and they become disseminated, so a skin lesion is often the accessible surface of a systemic problem. That last point is the practical one: in a neutropenic or transplant patient, the skin is frequently the easiest biopsiable site of an infection that is otherwise buried in lung or sinus.

1
New cutaneous nodule, papule, or necrotic eschar in a neutropenic or transplant patient

Treat this as an urgent request, not a routine consult. Time to biopsy is the modifiable variable in outcomes for angioinvasive mold infection.

Do this

Punch or incisional biopsy today. Divide the specimen: formalin for histology with GMS, PAS, Gram, and an acid-fast stain; fresh tissue in sterile saline for bacterial, fungal, and mycobacterial culture. Draw blood cultures at the same time, including a set held for fungi.

Do not do this

Do not swab it, do not send the whole specimen in formalin, and do not observe it for 48 hours on broadened antibacterials. Histology can give you a hyphal morphology within hours; culture gives you species and susceptibility later. You need both.

2
What does the histology show?

Hyphal morphology on frozen or rapid section is often the first actionable result and can redirect antifungal therapy the same day.

Broad, ribbon-like, pauciseptate, wide-angle branching

Mucorales. Surgical debridement plus an amphotericin B formulation is the framework. Critically, Mucorales are intrinsically resistant to voriconazole and to echinocandins — breakthrough infection on voriconazole prophylaxis is the classic clinical story.

Narrow, septate, acute-angle branching

Aspergillus, Fusarium, and several other hyaline molds are indistinguishable on morphology alone. Culture, or molecular identification, is what separates them — and it matters, because Fusarium is far less predictably susceptible to the mold-active azoles.

3
Look for the systemic compartment

Cutaneous disease in these hosts is usually not isolated. Image the sinuses and chest, examine the eyes, and consider lumbar puncture when Cryptococcus is on the list.

Hematologic malignancy with prolonged, profound neutropenia is the highest-risk setting for angioinvasive fungal disease, and the skin is where it declares itself.

  • Fusarium. Multiple painful erythematous papules and nodules with central necrosis, often appearing in crops. Onychomycosis or paronychia is a recognized portal of entry, so examine the nails. The single most useful laboratory distinction: unlike Aspergillus and the Mucorales, Fusarium sporulates in tissue and is frequently recovered from blood cultures.
  • Mucorales. A necrotic eschar, sometimes at a site of skin trauma, adhesive dressing, or a burn. Angioinvasion produces early infarction, which is why the lesion looks dusky and out of proportion to surrounding inflammation.
  • Aspergillus. Primary cutaneous disease at catheter insertion sites and under occlusive dressings, or secondary lesions from disseminated pulmonary disease. Blood cultures are almost never positive; galactomannan on serum or bronchoalveolar lavage supports the diagnosis.
  • Ecthyma gangrenosum. A round lesion evolving from an erythematous macule to a hemorrhagic bulla to a necrotic ulcer with a gunmetal-gray eschar and an erythematous halo, classically anogenital or axillary. Pseudomonas aeruginosa bacteremia is the prototype, but morphologically identical lesions arise from other Gram-negative rods and from molds — so send blood cultures and biopsy.
Pitfall

Mucorales hyphae are fragile and largely aseptate. Standard laboratory processing that grinds or homogenizes tissue shears them and can render the culture negative in a patient with florid histologic mucormycosis. Send the tissue intact, and tell the laboratory that mucormycosis is suspected so the specimen is minced rather than ground. A negative culture never overrules ribbon-like hyphae on histology.

Transplant recipients on calcineurin inhibitors and corticosteroids present later and more quietly. The timeline after transplant is itself a diagnostic tool, and steroid-dampened inflammation makes a serious infection look like a bland nodule.

  • Cryptococcus neoformans. Can present as a cellulitis-like plaque in a transplant patient on steroids — a “cellulitis” that is not responding to antibacterials in this host deserves a biopsy. Serum cryptococcal antigen is highly sensitive when disease is disseminated, but it can be negative in genuinely primary cutaneous cryptococcosis, so a negative result does not overrule the biopsy, and any patient with cutaneous cryptococcosis needs assessment for central nervous system disease.
  • Non-tuberculous mycobacteria and M. tuberculosis. Indolent nodules and abscesses, often on extremities, sometimes sporotrichoid. M. haemophilum is disproportionately represented in this population and requires both reduced-temperature incubation and iron-supplemented media.
  • Nocardia. Cutaneous lesions may be primary or metastatic from pulmonary disease. If Nocardia is found in skin, image the chest and consider the brain.
  • Disseminated varicella zoster. Three or more dermatomes involved, or roughly twenty or more lesions outside the primary and immediately adjacent dermatomes, defines dissemination and signals visceral risk; a few satellite lesions in an adjacent dermatome are common in ordinary zoster. Treat with intravenous antiviral therapy and use airborne plus contact precautions, unlike localized zoster in an immunocompetent host with covered lesions, which needs only standard precautions — in an immunocompromised patient even apparently localized zoster warrants airborne plus contact precautions until dissemination is excluded.
  • Reactivated endemic mycoses. Take a lifetime residence history, not a recent travel history. Latent Histoplasma, Coccidioides, and Blastomyces can reactivate years after exposure once T-cell immunity is suppressed.
Clinical pattern

The recurring signature in this group is the “boring” nodule — minimally tender, minimally erythematous, slowly growing, in a patient who feels reasonably well. In a normal host that appearance would justify watchful waiting. In a transplant recipient it justifies a biopsy sent for both histology and culture.

In advanced HIV with a low CD4 count, the dermatologic differential expands to include organisms that essentially never cause skin disease in immunocompetent people, and several of them mimic banal lesions.

  • Disseminated cryptococcosis. Umbilicated, molluscum-like papules, most often on the face. This is the highest-yield mimic in the entire section: molluscum in an adult with advanced HIV should be biopsied rather than curetted, and serum cryptococcal antigen sent.
  • Talaromyces marneffei and disseminated histoplasmosis. Produce the same umbilicated papular eruption in the right geographic context — Southeast Asia and southern China for the former, and endemic river valleys for the latter.
  • Bacillary angiomatosis. Bartonella henselae and B. quintana produce friable vascular papules and nodules that mimic Kaposi sarcoma and pyogenic granuloma. It is treatable and potentially fatal if missed.
  • Chronic ulcerative or verrucous herpes simplex. Large, persistent, painful ulcers that do not look like a typical vesicular eruption; acyclovir-resistant disease occurs in this population and viral culture with phenotypic susceptibility testing, or resistance genotyping, may be needed.
  • Disseminated Mycobacterium avium complex and other mycobacteria. Nodules, ulcers, and abscesses; blood cultures using mycobacterial bottles are worthwhile.
Advanced pearl

Immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome inverts the usual logic. Weeks after antiretroviral therapy is started, a previously indolent infection can become dramatically inflamed while the organism burden is falling. The skin lesion looks worse and the patient looks sicker, but the driver is returning immunity rather than treatment failure. Recognizing this prevents an unnecessary and destabilizing change of antimicrobial therapy.

Post-procedure and iatrogenic infection in cosmetic practice

This is the infection a dermatologist is most likely to be the first — and sometimes the only — physician to recognize, because the patient presents to the clinic that performed the procedure. The clinical signature is remarkably consistent regardless of which procedure was involved.

The pattern

Recognize it and the diagnosis follows

A cosmetic or minor surgical procedure, then a latent period of roughly two weeks to a few months, then erythematous papules, nodules, or fluctuant lesions confined to the treated area. The patient is systemically well. One or two courses of a standard antistaphylococcal antibiotic produce no durable improvement, and incision and drainage yields fluid that grows nothing.

LatencyWeeks to months, not days
DistributionFollows the treated field
Systemic illnessUsually absent
Response to beta-lactamsPoor or transient
Clinical pattern

“Culture-negative abscess” is not a diagnosis. It is a statement that the organism you are looking for does not grow under the conditions you ordered. In post-procedure dermatology it means rapidly growing mycobacteria until proven otherwise.

The usual sources

Water and non-sterile dilution

Rapidly growing non-tuberculous mycobacteria are environmental water organisms. They form biofilms in plumbing, tolerate standard disinfectants far better than ordinary bacteria, and thrive wherever tap water meets a breach in the skin.

  • Tattoo ink diluted with non-sterile water — classically producing lesions restricted to gray-wash shading.
  • Nail salon whirlpool footbaths, especially after leg shaving; furunculosis on the lower legs is the described pattern.
  • Mesotherapy, dermal filler, and injectable procedures.
  • Liposuction, abdominoplasty, and breast augmentation.
  • Laser resurfacing and microneedling with contaminated water or contaminated cooling systems.
  • Acupuncture and, in outbreak settings, contaminated multi-use solutions.
Why it matters

These present in clusters. If you see two of these in a month, you are looking at a point source — a specific device, water supply, or product — and the correct response includes notifying public health, not just treating the individual patient.

How do I actually work up a suspected post-procedure mycobacterial infection?

Take tissue, not fluid. A punch or incisional biopsy of an active lesion. If you drain a fluctuant lesion, send the wall and the tissue as well as the aspirate.

Split the specimen. Formalin for histology with acid-fast and fungal stains; fresh sterile saline for culture. Remember that granulomatous inflammation with negative special stains does not exclude mycobacterial infection — organisms are frequently scant in these lesions.

Order the right cultures explicitly. Mycobacterial culture and fungal culture, in addition to routine bacterial culture. Write on the requisition that rapidly growing mycobacteria are suspected. Ask for incubation at both ordinary and reduced temperature if an acral site is involved.

Set expectations about time. “Rapidly growing” is a taxonomic term meaning visible growth within about a week on subculture. It does not mean a fast answer from a clinical specimen — identification and susceptibility results commonly take several weeks.

Insist on species-level identification and susceptibility testing. This is one of the few dermatologic infections where susceptibility testing is genuinely decision-changing. Identification is by MALDI-TOF mass spectrometry or gene sequencing; susceptibility is by broth microdilution using CLSI methods for non-tuberculous mycobacteria.

Know the macrolide trap. M. abscessus commonly carries an inducible macrolide resistance gene, erm(41), that is invisible on a short-incubation clarithromycin result. Detecting it requires extended incubation of the macrolide well, or genotypic assessment. A clarithromycin result read too early can look susceptible while the organism is functionally resistant — and macrolide activity is a pillar of therapy for this group. Subspecies matters too: the massiliense subspecies typically carries a non-functional erm(41) and behaves as macrolide-susceptible.

Expect multidrug, prolonged therapy, and get help. Treatment is combination therapy over months, often with parenteral agents, and it is guided by susceptibilities. This is an infectious diseases co-management situation, and it usually includes source control — removal of implanted material or excision of involved tissue.

Diagnostic stewardship: the tests not to order

Antimicrobial stewardship in dermatology begins one step earlier than the prescription. A large share of unnecessary antibiotic exposure in skin disease is triggered by a test that should never have been sent, returning an organism that was never causing anything. The discipline is to send the test only when the result will change what you do.

Do not do thisWhy it misleadsDo this instead
Swab intact skin over a cellulitis You are sampling normal flora across an unbroken barrier. The organism is in the dermis, and the swab cannot reach it. Guidance from infectious diseases bodies advises against routine cultures in uncomplicated cellulitis. Diagnose uncomplicated cellulitis clinically and treat empirically for streptococci and staphylococci per local patterns. Reserve blood cultures, aspiration, or biopsy for severe illness, immunocompromise, unusual exposure, animal or water contact, or failure to respond.
Surface-swab a chronic ulcer or chronic wound Every chronic wound is colonized. A swab reliably grows a polymicrobial mixture of colonizers and biofilm organisms that has little relationship to whether the wound is infected, and the report reads convincingly enough that someone treats it. Decide clinically whether the wound is infected — increasing pain, spreading erythema, warmth, purulence, odor, systemic signs, failure to progress. If it is, cleanse and debride first, then take deep tissue or a curettage specimen from the wound base.
Send the whole biopsy in formalin when you want a culture Formalin kills organisms. There is no way to rescue a culture from a fixed specimen, and no amount of urgency after the fact recovers it. Divide the specimen at the point of collection. Formalin for histology, fresh tissue in a sterile container with a small amount of sterile saline for culture. If you can take only one core, send it fresh rather than in formalin — touch preparations and special stains can still be made from fresh tissue, but a fixed specimen can never be cultured.
Order fungal serology or fungal biomarkers for a routine superficial infection Antibody and antigen assays exist for the systemic mycoses and for invasive disease. They do not answer “is this scaly plaque tinea?” and a positive result in that setting reflects prior exposure or a false positive, not the diagnosis in front of you. Direct microscopy with potassium hydroxide, fungal culture, or a validated dermatophyte molecular assay. Histology with PAS on a nail clipping is a reasonable alternative when scrapings are repeatedly negative.
Treat a positive wound swab in a clinically uninfected ulcer This is the dermatologic equivalent of treating asymptomatic bacteriuria. It selects for resistance, exposes the patient to adverse effects and Clostridioides difficile risk, and does not improve healing. Treat the patient, not the report. Optimize the actual drivers of non-healing: offloading, compression, arterial perfusion, glycemic control, nutrition, and debridement.
Repeat testing as a test of cure for most superficial infections Nail plate takes many months to grow out and retains non-viable hyphal fragments that keep microscopy and molecular tests positive long after the infection is cured. Molecular assays detect nucleic acid from dead organisms. Follow clinical response, and where relevant follow the proximal growth of clear nail. Re-test only when there is genuine clinical suspicion of persistence or relapse.
Order a nasal MRSA screen to decide how to treat a skin infection Nasal colonization status was validated for a different question in a different anatomic setting. Colonization does not establish the cause of a given skin lesion, and its absence does not exclude MRSA in a purulent skin infection. If the lesion is purulent, drain it and culture the drainage. That specimen answers the actual question and gives you a susceptibility result.
Send an anaerobic culture on a superficial swab Surface specimens are contaminated with anaerobic skin and mucosal flora, and anaerobes are killed by ambient oxygen during transport of an inadequately protected swab. The result is uninterpretable either way. Send anaerobic cultures only from closed-space specimens — aspirates, deep tissue, operative material — in an appropriate anaerobic transport system, and only when anaerobic infection is genuinely plausible.
Advanced pearl

One selective-media detail worth knowing before you order fungal culture: routine dermatophyte media contain cycloheximide to suppress fast-growing environmental molds, which is exactly what you want when you are hunting a dermatophyte. But cycloheximide also inhibits many of the non-dermatophyte molds and yeasts — including some of the organisms causing onychomycosis in immunosuppressed patients, and Cryptococcus. If you suspect anything other than a dermatophyte, ask for a cycloheximide-free plate in addition. This is a request the laboratory will honor without argument, and it is invisible unless you know to make it.

What to do next

Before you send any specimen, answer three questions out loud: what organism am I looking for, what will I do differently for each possible result, and does the laboratory need to change anything about how they handle this. If you cannot answer the second question, do not send the test. If the answer to the third is yes, telephone the laboratory rather than relying on the requisition alone.

Top traps across the whole section

These are the errors that come up on rounds, on boards, and in real morbidity. Each one has the same shape: a common diagnosis is available, it is nearly right, and the discriminator is a single feature you have to actively look for.

The trapWhat it actually isThe discriminator
“Bilateral cellulitis” Pseudocellulitis — most often stasis dermatitis, but also lipodermatosclerosis, contact dermatitis, gout, deep vein thrombosis, calciphylaxis, Sweet syndrome, and pyoderma gangrenosum. True cellulitis is almost always unilateral. Bilateral lower limb erythema is stasis dermatitis until proven otherwise. Fever and leukocytosis are frequently absent in pseudocellulitis, and the skin is itchy rather than exquisitely tender. Misdiagnosis leads to unnecessary hospitalization and antibiotics — and debriding a pyoderma gangrenosum ulcer makes it worse through pathergy.
“Eczema that got worse on the steroid” Tinea incognito — dermatophyte infection modified by topical corticosteroid. The steroid suppresses inflammation and scale, so the lesion loses its annular scaly edge and looks bland, extensive, or follicular. Deeper follicular involvement produces Majocchi granuloma, which topical antifungals cannot reach. Stop the steroid, re-scrape from the advancing edge, and be prepared to treat systemically.
“Impetiginized eczema” in a distressed atopic patient Eczema herpeticum — disseminated herpes simplex on atopic skin. Monomorphic, punched-out, uniform erosions with scalloped borders, disproportionate pain, and fever. Impetigo is polymorphic with honey crust; eczema herpeticum is monotonous. Send viral PCR from the base of an unroofed fresh vesicle and start systemic antiviral therapy without waiting. Periocular involvement requires urgent ophthalmology.
Widespread superficial desquamation in a febrile child Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome, not toxic epidermal necrolysis. SSSS spares mucous membranes; TEN characteristically involves them. SSSS is toxin-mediated, so the blisters are sterile — culture the source of the toxin-producing organism (nasopharynx, conjunctivae, umbilicus, perineum), not the blister fluid. If a frozen section is available, SSSS shows a superficial subcorneal split at the granular layer, whereas TEN shows full-thickness epidermal necrosis. The management diverges completely.
Violaceous vascular papules in advanced HIV Bacillary angiomatosis, not Kaposi sarcoma. They are clinically indistinguishable, and one of them is a curable bacterial infection that can be fatal untreated. Biopsy: bacillary angiomatosis shows lobular vascular proliferation with neutrophils and amphophilic granular material representing clumped bacteria, demonstrable on Warthin-Starry. Kaposi sarcoma shows spindle cells and slit-like vascular spaces, with HHV-8 latent nuclear antigen immunostaining as the confirmatory marker.
“Molluscum” in an adult with advanced HIV Disseminated cryptococcosis — or talaromycosis, or disseminated histoplasmosis, depending on geography. Umbilicated papules on the face in a profoundly immunosuppressed adult are a systemic fungal infection until disproven. Biopsy rather than curette, send serum cryptococcal antigen and fungal blood cultures, and assess for meningitis. Treating this as molluscum loses weeks in a disease where weeks matter.
“The HIV test was negative” in a patient with a febrile morbilliform eruption and pharyngitis Acute retroviral syndrome during the antibody window period. Antibody-based tests can be negative in acute infection while the plasma viral load is extremely high. If acute HIV is on your differential, order a combination antigen and antibody immunoassay and an HIV-1 RNA test. A negative antibody-only rapid test does not exclude the diagnosis, and this is a public health moment as well as a clinical one.
“The scraping was negative, so it is not scabies” Classical scabies — which typically involves only a dozen or so mites on the entire body. The sensitivity of skin scraping in classical scabies is low; a negative result has little value against a convincing history of nocturnal itch, affected household contacts, and burrows in typical sites. Dermoscopy of a burrow improves yield. Treat on clinical grounds and treat contacts simultaneously. Crusted scabies is the opposite — enormous mite burden, floridly positive scrapings, and high transmissibility requiring contact precautions.
Chronic foot swelling with sinuses and grains Mycetoma — and the fungal and bacterial forms need entirely different therapy. Eumycetoma is caused by fungi and typically extrudes black grains; actinomycetoma is caused by filamentous bacteria including Nocardia and Actinomadura, and grain color varies with species. Actinomycetoma responds to prolonged antibacterial combinations; eumycetoma requires prolonged antifungal therapy and usually surgery. Send the grains, examine them, and stain them — grain morphology, Gram stain, and modified acid-fast staining make the split.
A necrotic eschar in a neutropenic patient An angioinvasive mold infection until proven otherwise — a mycology emergency. Biopsy today, split the tissue, tell the laboratory, call surgery, and involve infectious diseases. Do not observe it, do not swab it, and do not send it all in formalin. Survival in mucormycosis is tied to early diagnosis and early debridement, and the skin lesion may be the only accessible site of a disseminated process.
Pitfall

The most consequential single error in this entire section is sending the whole specimen in formalin. It is silent — the report comes back with a real histologic description, so nothing looks wrong — and it is unrecoverable. Every time you biopsy a lesion that might be infectious, say the words out loud before the specimen leaves the room: half in formalin, half fresh.

Student pearl

If you remember nothing else from this section, remember four sentences. Take the exposure history. Send tissue, not a swab. Split the specimen and tell the laboratory what you are hunting. And in an immunocompromised patient, a new nodule or eschar is a same-day biopsy, not a follow-up appointment.

Practice varies by institution and by region: available media, reference laboratory arrangements, molecular assay menus, endemic organisms, and local resistance patterns all differ. Where this section describes what to request, treat it as the conversation to have with your own microbiology laboratory rather than a universal protocol. Antimicrobial selection here is deliberately described at the level of drug classes and principles; specific agents, doses, and durations should come from current guidance and, for the less common organisms, from infectious diseases consultation.

References & further reading

Primary guidance and reputable clinical references for the material above. Every link was checked and resolves at time of build. Antimicrobial choice, susceptibility interpretation and dosing are institution- and region-dependent — always confirm against your local antibiogram and current guidance.

Guidelines and standards

  1. Stevens DL, Bisno AL, Chambers HF, et al. Practice Guidelines for the Diagnosis and Management of Skin and Soft Tissue Infections: 2014 Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America. Clinical Infectious Diseases 2014;59(2):e10–e52. Also indexed at PubMed 24947530.
  2. IDSA Practice Guideline hub: Skin and Soft Tissue Infections. Infectious Diseases Society of America — current guideline landing page.
  3. American Academy of Dermatology — Clinical Guidelines. AAD guideline index, including acne and related cutaneous infection topics.
  4. CLSI M100 Performance Standards for Antimicrobial Susceptibility Testing and EUCAST breakpoint tables — the authorities behind every S/I/R on a susceptibility report. Consult the current edition through your laboratory; breakpoints are revised periodically.

World Health Organization fact sheets

  1. Leprosy (Hansen disease) — classification, diagnosis and multidrug therapy.
  2. Buruli ulcer (Mycobacterium ulcerans infection) — epidemiology, mycolactone, and management.
  3. Mycetoma — eumycetoma versus actinomycetoma and why the distinction changes treatment.
  4. Leishmaniasis — cutaneous and mucocutaneous forms, geography and species.
  5. Scabies — burden, crusted scabies and mass drug administration.
  6. Mpox (monkeypox) — clinical features, transmission and diagnosis.

Clinical dermatology reference — DermNet topic pages

Peer-reviewed dermatology reference maintained in New Zealand; useful for morphology, images and management summaries. Grouped to mirror the sub-sections above.

  1. Bacterial — Impetigo, Cellulitis, Necrotising fasciitis, Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome, Erythrasma
  2. Mycobacterial — Leprosy
  3. Fungal — Tinea corporis, Tinea capitis, Pityriasis versicolor, Sporotrichosis
  4. Viral — Herpes zoster, Eczema herpeticum, Molluscum contagiosum, Hand, foot and mouth disease, Orf, Mpox, Kaposi sarcoma
  5. Infestations — Scabies, Head lice, Cutaneous larva migrans

Also consult

  1. US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (cdc.gov) — the practical authority for Lyme disease, rickettsial disease, mpox, scabies, leishmaniasis and fungal disease, including reporting requirements. Its pages block automated checking, so they are named here rather than deep-linked.
  2. IDSA/ASM Guide to Utilization of the Microbiology Laboratory for Diagnosis of Infectious Diseases — the definitive reference for specimen selection, including when a swab is the wrong specimen.
  3. Standard texts: Bolognia, Dermatology; Fitzpatrick's Dermatology in General Medicine; Mandell, Douglas, and Bennett's Principles and Practice of Infectious Diseases; ASM Manual of Clinical Microbiology.

Wrap-up · Take it to the floor

Final recap — what to carry onto service.

If you remember nothing else from today, Jaina, remember these. They are enough to read a micro report intelligently and to avoid the mistakes that actually harm patients — and the Derm Pearls traps sit on top of them.

  1. The specimen decides the answer. Right site, right container, ideally before antibiotics — and tissue or fluid beats a swab almost every time.
  2. The central question in all of microbiology is contamination vs colonization vs infection. Ask it of every positive result.
  3. The Gram stain gives you a usable answer in minutes: purple or pink, cocci or rods, clusters or chains.
  4. A sputum sample full of squamous epithelial cells is saliva, not lung — the lab may reject it, and you should not treat it.
  5. Blood cultures: two sets, adequate volume, before antibiotics. Volume is the single biggest driver of yield in adults.
  6. S. aureus, S. pneumoniae, Enterobacterales and Candida in a blood culture are essentially never dismissed as contaminants. Coagulase-negative staph in one of several bottles usually is.
  7. Staphylococcus aureus bacteremia demands a bundle: repeat cultures to document clearance, echocardiography, source control, intravenous therapy, and an infectious diseases consult.
  8. MALDI-TOF identifies the organism; it does not tell you susceptibility. Those are two different questions and two different waits.
  9. Molecular tests detect nucleic acid — fast and sensitive, but they can flag dead or merely colonizing organisms, and they only find what is on the panel.
  10. Never compare MICs between different antibiotics. Each drug has its own breakpoint; a lower number is not a stronger drug.
  11. S, I and R are predictions tied to breakpoints and usual dosing at the usual site — not measures of potency.
  12. Know the intrinsic resistance traps: enterococci are not treated with cephalosporins, Listeria is not covered by cephalosporins, and organisms without a cell wall are untouched by beta-lactams.
  13. Recognize MRSA, VRE, ESBL, AmpC and CRE by name — and treat carbapenem-resistant organisms as both a therapeutic problem and an infection-control one.
  14. An “S” is necessary but not sufficient: the drug still has to reach the site (daptomycin is inactivated in the lung), and no antibiotic substitutes for source control.
  15. Asymptomatic bacteriuria is the most over-treated finding in the hospital. Do not culture urine without urinary symptoms; pregnancy and pre-urologic procedures are the well-established exceptions.
  16. Test for Clostridioides difficile only on unformed stool, only when it will change management, and never as a test of cure.
  17. Reassess at 48–72 hours when the data return: de-escalate to the narrowest effective agent, switch intravenous to oral when appropriate, and set a stop date.

How to sound smart on rounds

  • She has no urinary symptoms — I would not send that culture.
  • That is one of four bottles with coagulase-negative staph; it looks like a contaminant.
  • S. aureus in the blood — can we get repeat cultures, an echo, and ID on board?
  • Cultures are back and it is a pan-susceptible E. coli. Can we come off the carbapenem?
  • The MRSA nares screen is negative, so I think we can stop the vancomycin.
  • Before we escalate antibiotics — is there a source we still need to drain?

Good questions to ask on service

  • What does our local antibiogram say for this organism here?
  • Were these cultures drawn before the first dose of antibiotics?
  • Is this a true pathogen, or is it this patient’s normal flora?
  • Do we have source control, or is there still something to drain or remove?
  • Now that we have an identification, what is the narrowest agent that works?
  • Is this an organism with an inducible AmpC we should worry about?

You already know the skin. What this adds is the laboratory behind it — the right specimen, the right request, and the traps that turn a confident diagnosis into the wrong one.
